# Decision log

Append-only. **Newest first.** Never edit an entry to change its meaning — if a decision is reversed, add a new entry that says so and link back.

**Bar for an entry:** something a competent person would later ask *"why is it like this?"* about. Architecture changes, dependency choices, resolved `docs/PRD.md` §11 questions, reversals. **Not** routine implementation choices — those are visible in the diff.

## 2026-09-25 — `M8-FIX-BE-178`: the approved disclosure is set as version `1`; the refusal it replaced stays, reachable only by setting `None`; the test pins the text by retyping it

**Decision.** `askwell.online.DISCLOSURE` is the statement approved in the correction entry below, version `1`, verbatim. The code path for an undefined statement is kept (the `DISCLOSURE_UNDEFINED` refusal in `POST /ask`, `confirm_disclosure` and the interface), and its tests now set `DISCLOSURE = None` themselves rather than assert that it is `None`. The verbatim test types the statement out in `api/tests/test_online.py` rather than reading the constant back. The three suites that used a stand-in `Disclosure(version="1", text="What goes.")` to get past the gate now run against the real constant, and a new walkthrough test goes key → online → statement shown → `NOT_CONFIRMED` → confirm `1` → the request reaches the provider, through the real `_online_client`.

**Why.** The ticket required every clause to be re-checked against the code before setting it. It was, on this branch, and all hold. Only `askwell.ask._run_generation`'s `compose_conflict` path builds an `OnlineClient`. Its prompt is the question, the above-threshold candidates, the inline clarification answer if one was given this turn, and `memory.retrieve_relevant_facts`'s facts and notes. That function has no conversation filter and returns inferred rows alongside user ones. Schema notes carry table and column names from `schema_introspect.describe_table`/`describe_column`, `connections` and `table_load`, and in one place a cell: `table_infer.raise_table_inference` writes the date-format `evidence_value`. Its other ambiguity reasons are fixed strings. The abstention branch, SQL turns and the tool loop never reach `_online_client`, and no conversation history enters `compose_conflict`. A future change to any of these is a change to what the statement claims, which is why the constant's comment names them.

Keeping the `None` path, rather than deleting it now that it is unreachable, is deliberate. Setting `DISCLOSURE = None` is the one-line way to withdraw a statement that stops being true, as #753 nearly required. That only works if `None` still refuses every send, so a test keeps proving it does. Reading the constant back in the verbatim test would pass for any wording. Retyping it means a change to either copy fails, so a reworded statement cannot ship as the same version `1` without someone noticing.

**Consequences.** Online sends are permitted for a conversation that has confirmed version `1`. `test_no_online_send_is_possible_until_redis_is_authenticated` was not edited, only its docstring: it passes because `compose.yaml` runs Redis with `--aclfile` (`M8-FIX-SEC-177`). G13's positive half (`docs/online-release-test.md` §4.3) can now run, and needs a person with a real key and a capture. `test_provider_key._clean` truncates `sources` with `CASCADE`, so it no longer depends on running before `test_ask_online` (#752).

**Refs:** #737, #753, #752; `M8-FIX-BE-178`; `api/src/askwell/online.py`; `api/tests/test_online.py`, `test_ask_online.py`, `test_provider_key.py`.

---

## 2026-09-25 — `M8-FIX-DOC-179`: the licence gate asks "compatible with GPLv3?", an unclear licence fails it, and AGPL stays refused although GPLv3 could carry it

**Decision.** The relicence decided earlier today is carried out. `LICENSE` is the FSF's GPLv3 text, fetched from `gnu.org/licenses/gpl-3.0.txt` and byte-identical to the copy Fedora ships in `/usr/share/licenses/cockpit-bridge/GPL-3.0.txt`. `api/pyproject.toml`, `web/package.json` and `web/src-tauri/Cargo.toml` declare `GPL-3.0-or-later`. The About screen, `README.md`, `docs/PRD.md`, `SUPPORT.md` and `llms.txt` say GPLv3. In `askwell.notices`, `DISALLOWED_LICENSES` loses every GPL that can be taken under v3 (`GPL-2.0-or-later`, `GPL-3.0*`, `GPL-1.0-or-later`) and keeps `GPL-1.0-only`, `GPL-2.0-only` and SPDX's deprecated `GPL-1.0`/`GPL-2.0`, which mean "only". A new `UNCLEAR_LICENSES` holds a GPL with no version (`GPL`, `GNU GPL`), a v2 with no "only" or "or later" (`GPLv2`, `GPL-2`), and no licence at all (`UNVERIFIED`, `UNKNOWN`, empty). `scripts/generate_notices.py` fails the gate on either list.

**Why change the rule instead of naming `phonemizer`.** The ticket forbids allow-listing by name, and for a reason: a name-based exception says nothing about the next GPL dependency, and the gate would then be judging packages by whether someone has already argued for them. The rule is now the question GPLv3 actually asks, "can this be distributed as part of a GPLv3 work?", so `phonemizer` passes for the same reason any other GPLv3 package would, and nothing in the code mentions it.

**Why an unclear licence fails.** A bare "GPL" is exactly the case where the answer depends on what the package did not say: GPL-2.0-only is incompatible and GPL-2.0-or-later is not. Passing it would be assuming the answer, and failing it as "disallowed" would be asserting one. So it fails as its own category, `unclear licence GPL, needs a decision`, and a person reads the package's actual terms. The same reasoning covers a package whose licence could not be read at all. Before this change, `UNVERIFIED` passed the gate silently, although `M7-DOC-DOC-163`'s own edge case said such a package is "flagged for a decision rather than assumed permissive". Nothing currently installed is unverified, so the gate still passes; it is the next one that this catches. The PyPI classifier `GNU General Public License (GPL)` now maps to `GPL` rather than falling through to free text. Packages often list it next to a versioned GPL classifier, so the versioned one wins when both are present. The unversioned `GNU General Public License v2 (GPLv2)` classifier keeps its old reading, `GPL-2.0-only`: it fails loudly rather than passing on a guess. All classifier strings were checked against PyPI's published list today.

**Why AGPL stays refused.** Strictly, GPLv3 §13 lets a GPLv3 work be combined with AGPLv3 code, so AGPL is GPLv3-compatible. The ticket and the relicence decision both keep it refused, and that is right for Askwell. Combining with AGPL code brings its network-use obligation into the combined work. Askwell serves its interface over HTTP, even if only on loopback. The PyMuPDF decision (2026-08-27) rejected AGPL on the obligation, not only on Apache-2.0 compatibility. Allowing it would reopen that decision as a side effect of this one. If an AGPL component is ever wanted, that is its own decision.

**Why `GPL-1.0-or-later` is now allowed.** "Or later" includes v3, so it is compatible for the same reason GPL-2.0-or-later is. Keeping it refused would have made the list inconsistent with its own stated rule. No current dependency uses it.

**Why the Tauri bundle config was not given a licence field.** Cargo's `license` key is metadata nothing reads for `publish = false`. `tauri.conf.json`'s bundle licence settings change what the installers show and embed, which cannot be checked on this machine for all three platforms. The installer licence display belongs with the bundle work (`M7-OFFLINE-DEPLOY-144`) and is recorded in #754, together with the GPLv3 §6 obligation to provide the Corresponding Source with a distributed bundle, which Apache-2.0 never imposed.

**Consequences.** `scripts/dev.sh notices` passes with `phonemizer` 3.4.0 listed as `GPL-3.0-or-later`, and fails when fed a fabricated GPL-2.0-only, AGPL-3.0-only or bare-GPL package through the real script. `test_notices.py` pins both halves through the generator's own `check()`. The historical references to "Apache-2.0 stays" in `docs/release-procedure.md` and `deploy/macos/install.sh` quote the title of the 2026-08-26 entry and are left as quotations. This is not legal advice, and a lawyer's review before the first public release is still advisable.

**Refs:** `M8-FIX-DOC-179`; #619; the relicence entry below; `LICENSE`, `api/src/askwell/notices.py`, `scripts/generate_notices.py`, `api/tests/test_notices.py`, `web/components/settings/about.tsx`, `AGENTS.md` §3 C9.

## 2026-09-25 — `M8-FIX-SEC-177`: Redis has one user per service that connects; the ACL is rendered from `.env` at every start; voice gets no user; the proxy's startup `DEL` is an ACL selector

**Decision.** Option 1 of #730. `deploy/redis/users.acl` defines five users: `default` (off, no commands, no password — an unauthenticated connection is refused), `healthcheck` (`PING` only), `api`, `worker` and `proxy`. Each starts from `resetkeys resetchannels -@all` and names every command and key pattern it holds. Only `api` may write `askwell:egress:permitted_host`, `askwell:egress:grant:*` and `askwell:egress:conversation:*`. `proxy` reads those, writes its own five counters, and may `DEL` under `askwell:egress:conversation:` through a selector. `worker` holds `arq:*` and `askwell:schema_introspect:*`, nothing under `askwell:egress:`. Every Redis client in the code goes through `askwell.redis_client.connect`, and arq through `worker.redis_settings`, both presenting `ASKWELL_REDIS_USERNAME`/`ASKWELL_REDIS_PASSWORD`. Compose sets those per service from three new `.env` passwords, `REDIS_API_PASSWORD`, `REDIS_WORKER_PASSWORD` and `REDIS_PROXY_PASSWORD`. The three installers generate them on every run.

**Why the ACL is a template rendered at start, not a file with passwords in it or `--user` flags.** Redis reads ACL passwords from its ACL file or its command line, never from the environment, and the passwords belong in `.env` (C8) like the database's. `--user api on >password ...` on the command line would put every password in the process arguments, visible to `ps` and `podman inspect`. So `deploy/redis/start.sh` runs as the container's entrypoint. It checks all three passwords are set, and fails the container if one is not. It writes the ACL with their SHA-256 (`#<hash>`) into `/tmp/askwell-redis/`, unsets the plain-text variables, and `exec`s the image's own `docker-entrypoint.sh`, so privilege dropping and data-directory handling are unchanged. The committed file holds placeholders, and a placeholder is not a valid hash, so loading the template by mistake fails closed. `requirepass` alone was rejected because one shared password is exactly the "any container can write a grant" problem with an extra step.

**Why `voice` has no user, though the ticket says voice gets "the queue keys only".** Voice never opens a Redis connection: nothing under `askwell/voice/` imports Redis or arq, and the WebSocket channel lives in `api`. The ticket's own validation rule is that no service holds a Redis permission it does not use. For voice the narrowest set is none, so it has no user and no password, and its compose block no longer names a Redis host. The test "tries from each and is refused" covers voice as an unauthenticated connection, the only kind it could make. `inference-bridge` is the same. That makes five users, one per service that connects plus `default` and `healthcheck`: the number the ticket asked for, not the split it assumed.

**Why the proxy's `DEL` is a selector.** Redis key permissions are per key, not per command. `%W~askwell:egress:conversation:*` would let the proxy `SET` a conversation grant as well as delete one. The ticket asked to be told if the startup `DEL` could not be expressed narrowly. It can: the selector `(+del %W~askwell:egress:conversation:*)` binds that one command to that one prefix, and the proxy's root rules have no `DEL` at all. Verified live: the proxy deletes a conversation grant and is refused `DEL` on a turn grant, the permitted host and a counter, and refused `UNLINK` and `GETDEL` on a turn grant.

**Why a health-check user with a password generated at each start.** A bare `redis-cli ping` is now refused, which would report a healthy Redis as down and stop every dependent service. A `nopass` health user would be a user that authenticates with anything. A fourth `.env` password would be one more secret for three installers to manage, for a command that only reads `PONG`. So `start.sh` generates a random one at each start into a file only the container reads, and that user may `PING` and nothing else.

**Why the installers generate these on every run, upgrades included.** The database passwords are generated only when `.env` is first created, and an existing `.env` is never touched. Applied here, that rule would leave an upgraded install with no Redis passwords, and Compose would refuse to start it. That is the right refusal for someone editing `.env` by hand and the wrong one for an upgrade. `ensure_redis_passwords`/`Set-AskwellRedisPasswords` fill a missing, empty or `change-me*` value and leave a real one alone. Replacing one is always safe. Redis keeps no users between starts, since `start.sh` renders them from `.env` every time, so there is nothing stored to fall out of step.

**Why the processes check their credential at start as well.** Redis is what enforces this. A process without a password gets `NOAUTH` on its first command, and there is no code path that falls back to no authentication. But the first visible symptom would be a health panel saying the worker has not checked in, not the variable that is missing. So `api`, `worker` and `egress-proxy` refuse to start with a message naming it (`redis_client.require_credentials`). The settings fields are optional only because `voice` and `inference-bridge` load the same `Settings` and must not be made to carry a credential they never use. That is the latent gap #524 records for the database URLs, and it is not repeated here.

**What this does not cover.** `SCAN` is not key-restricted in Redis, so `api` and `proxy` can list key *names* outside their patterns, though not read their values. The worker has no `SCAN`. A refused write from code that is not Askwell's, such as a compromised dependency, is recorded in Redis's `ACL LOG`, but no user can read that log. That is #749. A compromised `api` process can still write a grant, but it also holds the database and the provider key, so the ACL does not pretend otherwise.

**Consequences.** `.env` gains three required passwords; an old `.env` without them stops `podman compose up` with a message naming the variable, and the installer fixes it on upgrade. Command names in the ACL are what reaches Redis, not client method names: redis-py's `incr()` sends `INCRBY`, and arq's worker needs `INFO` and `DBSIZE` at startup. Both were found by bringing the stack up, which is why a new Redis call in the code now needs a matching line in `users.acl`. The refusal is loud (`redis_write_refused`, or the existing `*_failed` warnings), and `test_redis_acl.py` runs against a real Redis in `test-db` and CI. `scripts/dev.sh eval` connects as `api`. The guard `test_no_online_send_is_possible_until_redis_is_authenticated` now passes on `--aclfile`, so #737 is unblocked on this side.

**Refs:** `M8-FIX-SEC-177`; issues #730, #737, #749, #524; `deploy/redis/users.acl`, `deploy/redis/start.sh`, `api/src/askwell/redis_client.py`, `compose.yaml`, `deploy/{linux,macos}/lib.sh`, `deploy/windows/lib.ps1`; `api/tests/test_redis_acl.py`, `api/tests/test_redis_client.py`.

## 2026-09-25 — `M8-ONLINE-TEST-176`: the online-mode gate is G13, a second gate beside the offline one; the capture reads names as well as addresses; the credential edge cases are pinned by tests, not the script; a blocked run is `BLOCKED`, and it is blocked until #737

**Decision.** Online mode gets its own release gate, G13 in `docs/release-checklist.md`: the procedure `docs/online-release-test.md`, the record `docs/online-test-log.md`, and `scripts/verify-online-egress.sh` for the part that is HTTP. It is modelled on the offline gate (`M7-OFFLINE-TEST-145`): the proxy's counters plus an independent packet capture, with a script for the automatable half and a person reading the capture. The script exits `0`, `1`, or `2` for "nothing failed but the online send could not run", and `2` is `BLOCKED`. G13 cannot be `ACCEPTED`, like every C1 gate. It runs after G4, on the same machine.

**Why a second gate, not steps added to G4.** G4 runs with the cable out, and online mode needs the cable in. One procedure that plugs the cable back halfway through would leave the offline half resting on a machine that has since been online. The ticket's own assumption is that "this is an additional gate, not a replacement", and two log files make it impossible to record one as a stand-in for the other.

**Why the capture checks the name in the TLS handshake, not only the address.** The authorisation is for a named service, and providers sit behind shared CDN edges. An address filter would pass a request to a different service at the same edge, which is the ticket's "different service, same address" edge case. The proxy compares the `CONNECT` target as written, so the SNI in the capture is the matching independent check. A session to an allowed address with any other SNI fails the run. Tunnel contents are TLS, so this cannot see a `Host` header that differs from the SNI inside a tunnel. Only the API process holds a conversation's credential, and only `OnlineClient` uses it, so nothing else can open such a tunnel at all. Intercepting TLS to look inside was rejected: it would mean Askwell holding a CA that can read the user's provider traffic, to catch a case that needs the credential anyway.

**Why the name-versus-address and retry-storm cases are tests, not script steps.** Both need the conversation's credential. The credential lives only in the API process's memory, by design (`M8-ONLINE-SEC-169`). A script that could present it would prove that the protection has a hole. So the proxy's half is pinned in `test_egress.py`: the resolved address, another case, the fully qualified form and an IPv4-mapped IPv6 spelling are each refused even with the credential, while the granted name reaches the same listener; a plain-HTTP request with the credential is refused; an unreachable destination is one upstream attempt per `CONNECT`, answered `502`, counted as nothing permitted. `test_ask_online.py` now asserts one provider request per question on every fallback path. The procedure names these tests, and `test_release_checklist.py` fails if one is renamed away. The bound is therefore **one connection attempt per question**, and the proxy makes one attempt per address the name resolves to within its 10-second connect limit. There is no retry anywhere to storm. The capture checks it: no more TLS sessions to the provider than online questions asked.

**Why "switching off stops it at once" is measured against one second.** That is the stated bound from `M8-ONLINE-SEC-169` (`CONVERSATION_RECHECK_SECONDS`). The script checks the part it can see: no authorisation left once `DELETE` returns, and the next question counts nothing. The in-flight case, a long answer streaming when the user clicks off, is timed from the capture, because only a capture sees the last packet.

**Why update checks are off for the run.** The update check is the one other permitted destination. With it on, every non-provider packet in the capture needs an argument about which feature sent it. With it off, anything that is not the authorised destination is a failure, and a person reading a capture can judge that.

**Why it is `BLOCKED`, and why that is not a stub.** #737 refuses every online send, so there is nothing to capture going to a provider, and the gate records `BLOCKED` until #737 is decided (and #730 before it). Everything that does not need a send ran live on the development stack against `api.openai.com:443` with a placeholder key: one authorisation, `403` for the `api` and `worker` dependencies, no route from the sandbox, nothing for the local conversation, nothing after switching off (`docs/online-test-log.md`). The alternative, marking the gate "not applicable until online AI ships", was rejected. A release whose code can go online is exactly the one this gate exists for, and the known-holds table now lists #737 against G13, so nobody meets it for the first time on release day.

**Consequences.** Every release has one more gate, about an hour. #255 closes with this ticket. Its remaining text-only items (`docs/PRD.md`, `docs/build-plan.md` Phase 7 and `AGENTS.md` §1 still describing credits) move to #744, where the owner decides the wording, because the PRD is shared outside the project. When #737 is decided, the first full run of G13 is the proof the ticket asks for, and its entry goes at the top of `docs/online-test-log.md`.

**Refs:** `M8-ONLINE-TEST-176`; issues #255, #730, #737, #744; `docs/online-release-test.md`, `docs/online-test-log.md`, `scripts/verify-online-egress.sh`; `api/tests/test_egress.py`, `api/tests/test_ask_online.py`, `api/tests/test_release_checklist.py`.

## 2026-09-25 — `M8-KEY-FE-175`: a rejected key or an empty account ends the conversation's online authorisation; a rate limit does not; the marker names why

**Decision.** When the provider rejects the key (`401`/`403`, now `key_rejected` rather than the generic `refused`) or says the account is out of quota (`402`, or a `429` whose OpenAI-compatible error `code`/`type` is `insufficient_quota`, now `quota_exhausted`), the turn answers locally as before, and the conversation's online authorisation also ends at once: the in-process credential is forgotten, the proxy grant closed, `conversations.ai_backend` set to local, and an `online_ai_revoked` record written with reason `provider_rejected_key` or `provider_quota_exhausted` (`askwell.online.end_after_provider_refusal`, called from `askwell.ask._end_online_after_refusal` in its own transaction before the local answer runs). The answer's closing line adds *"This conversation is local now."* The online state gains `ended_reason`, the conversation's latest revocation reason, and the marker names it for those two and for `key_removed`/`key_replaced`. Every other failure (rate limit, network, provider error, interruption) answers that turn locally and leaves the conversation online.

**Why end it rather than fall back per turn.** `M8-ONLINE-BE-170` already fell back per turn, so the ticket's "answers locally" was true before this change. What it did not do was stop. With a dead key or an empty account, every later question would be composed, sent through the proxy, refused, and answered locally. Each refusal still carries the question and its passages to the provider (`M8-ONLINE-OBS-172` counts a refused request as sent). Sending the user's material again for a no that is already known is egress with no purpose, which is the one thing C1 is about. Ending it also makes the ticket's "the marker updates so later turns read as local" true in fact, not only in the labels. And it makes "credit restored later — the conversation does not silently return to online" true by construction: there is no grant left for a restored account to resume, so only the user switching it on again brings it back. The disclosure confirmation outlives the authorisation, so switching back on is one click.

**Why a rate limit does not end it.** A plain `429` asks for a pause, and the next question minutes later will probably go through. Ending the conversation on it would turn a temporary condition into something the user has to notice and undo, and it would lose the distinction the ticket asks for between the provider saying "not now" and "no". The same reasoning applies to network, provider-error and interrupted failures. They are transient, and the proxy's `502` never sent the content anyway. A generic `4xx` (`refused`) also stays online. `400` or `413` can be about this one question, and guessing that it is permanent would end conversations on a hunch.

**Why read the 429 body at all.** The OpenAI-compatible API, which `M8-ONLINE-BE-170` chose (2026-08-10, 2026-09-24), returns `429` for both a rate limit and an exhausted quota and tells them apart only by `error.code`. Without reading it, "your quota ran out" and "slow down" read the same, and the ticket's point is that they have different fixes. The body is parsed for that one field and compared to a fixed value. It is never repeated, because a provider's body can echo the key (`M8-KEY-BE-173`). Anything unreadable is treated as a rate limit, because that reading leaves the conversation online, and the only cost of being wrong is one more refused request. `402` is Payment Required, which some OpenAI-compatible providers use for the same condition. Rejected: treating every `429` as exhaustion (it ends conversations on ordinary throttling), and adding a per-provider mapping (it picks providers on the user's behalf, which 2026-09-24 declined).

**Why the marker names only four reasons.** The two refusals are the ticket's, and each has a different next step that belongs to the user and their provider, never to Askwell. `key_removed`/`key_replaced` are the wording `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` assigned to this ticket. `disabled` is something the user just did. `lapsed` is also what reconciliation records for a grant that vanished for any reason, such as a proxy restart, so naming it "the time ran out" could be wrong. Those keep the plain marker. An unknown reason also falls back to the plain marker, so a reason added later cannot render as `undefined`.

**Why this ticket touched the backend.** It is labelled frontend, but its scope ("per-turn backend recording", "the marker updating to reflect the mixed state") and its acceptance ("later turns read as local") cannot be met from the screen alone. The screen would need to guess from a closing line whether the conversation had stopped, and the next question would still go out. Per-turn recording already existed (`trace.backend`, `ask_asked.backend`/`online_fallback`), so nothing new was needed there.

**Consequences.** Trace and interaction records written before this change carry `refused` for a rejected key. From `0.7.45` it is `key_rejected`. Nothing reads the code to decide anything, and the transmission outcome takes the same code. Real exhaustion cannot be walked until #737 lets anything be sent. The simulated path is covered end to end through the routes (`test_ask_online.py::test_an_empty_account_answers_locally_ends_online_and_loses_nothing`).

**Refs:** `M8-KEY-FE-175`; `docs/ux/ask.md` §5; `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §1; issues #255, #733, #737.

## 2026-09-25 — `M8-KEY-FE-174`: the key section states when and where the key is used but not what a question carries; a padded key is refused, not trimmed; the passphrase is asked for in place

**Decision.** Settings → Online AI gains the key control (`web/components/settings/online-key.tsx`, wording in `web/lib/online-key.ts`). Above the field, before anything can be pasted, the section says what the key is for, when it is used (only in a conversation the user switched online, only after that conversation's own statement of what it sends has been agreed to), where it goes (the provider address entered with it, nowhere else), and that the cost is between the user and their provider. Once saved, the section shows the provider and never the key. The server does not return the key, so it cannot be shown. Replace and remove each state their consequence for online conversations beside the button, before it is pressed. Remove is one click with no confirmation dialog. A key that is empty, only whitespace, or has whitespace in it is refused in the browser, with that reason, before it is sent. Everything else is left to the server's own check (`provider_key.validate`), and its sentence is shown as it comes. On a locked install, adding or replacing a key asks for the passphrase in place, through `POST /settings/passphrase/unlock`. That makes this the first screen that calls it.

**Why the section does not list what a question sends.** The ticket asks for the explanation to name "what is sent". There are two different things here. The key is sent to the provider as the request's credential, and the section says so exactly: to that address and nowhere else, only from an online conversation. The *contents* of a question (the question, which passages, which facts) are what #737 is deciding, and the ticket itself puts that statement out of scope as "a different moment and a different sentence". Writing a second description of the payload into settings now would mean deciding #737's wording on a screen that is not where the user confirms it. After that it would be two copies of the most sensitive sentence in the product, free to drift. So the settings section says that a question's contents are stated in the conversation before it is sent, and the existing "What leaves this machine" paragraph still says that the statement has not been written and nothing is sent until it is. When #737 is decided, the settings text does not need to change.

**Why a padded key is refused rather than trimmed.** The ticket's edge case is "refused at entry with the reason". Trimming quietly would save something other than what was pasted, and it would do that without saying so, on the one field where the user cannot see afterwards what was saved. A key with a space in the middle cannot be trimmed into a correct one either. The browser check covers only whitespace and emptiness, the mistakes a paste makes, and the sentence names them. It does not copy the server's full pattern: that would give two validators that can disagree, and the server's is the one that decides.

**Why the passphrase is asked for here, not by sending the user to Privacy and security.** The ticket's edge case says entry on a locked install "asks for the passphrase first". The passphrase control has no unlock action at all today, only set, change and remove, so sending the user there would lead nowhere. Unlocking is not a decisions record (`askwell.passphrase.unlock`), so asking for it here adds no record and no new mechanism. Removing a key needs no passphrase and does not ask for one, because deleting the key does not decrypt it.

**Why replace pre-fills the provider address and model.** They are not secret (the server returns them), and most replacements are key rotation at the same provider. Pre-filling them means a rotation leaves every online conversation's grant standing, because the address is unchanged (`M8-KEY-BE-173`). Re-typing the address by hand risks a small typo that revokes them all. The key field always starts empty.

**Consequences.** A user can now store a key, but online AI still sends nothing until #737 is decided (and #730 before it, enforced by a test). The section says both. Unlocking from the key form unlocks the whole API process, the same as any other unlock would. The locked path was not walked live on the development stack, because setting a passphrase there re-encrypts the whole development library. Unit tests cover the `423` → passphrase prompt path, and `M8-KEY-BE-173`'s database tests cover the server side.

**Refs:** `M8-KEY-FE-174`; `docs/ux/settings.md` §3; issues #255, #658, #730, #737; `docs/decisions.md` 2026-09-25 (`M8-KEY-BE-173`).

## 2026-09-25 — `M8-KEY-BE-173`: the provider key carries its provider, which replaces the destination and model settings; removing or re-pointing it revokes every conversation; an online send stays gated on Redis authentication

**Decision.** The user's provider key is stored as one `settings` row (`online_provider_key`): the key as a Fernet token under `askwell.passphrase.current_key`, and beside it, in the clear, the provider it belongs to: `destination` (`host:port`) and `model`. `ASKWELL_ONLINE_AI_DESTINATION` and `ASKWELL_ONLINE_AI_MODEL` are removed. Online AI is available only while a key is held and this process can read it. `askwell.online.enable` authorises the key's destination, and `askwell.ask._online_client` decrypts the key at the moment of generating and refuses to use it unless its destination is the one the conversation's grant names. Removing the key, or replacing it with one for a different destination, closes every conversation grant and puts each conversation back to local with a revocation record (`key_removed`, `key_replaced`). Replacing it for the same destination leaves the grants standing. Storing, replacing and removing are decisions records (`online_key_stored`, `online_key_replaced`, `online_key_removed`) that name the provider and never the key. The HTTP surface is `GET`/`PUT`/`DELETE /settings/online-key`; its responses say whether a key is set and for which provider, and never contain the key, masked or otherwise.

**Why the provider is stored with the key, not kept as configuration.** The ticket's scope line is "which provider it belongs to, so the destination the egress proxy authorises matches it". With the destination in the environment and the key in the database, two things name the provider, and nothing stops them disagreeing: a key for one provider would be sent, under a grant, to another. Storing them together makes the match true by construction instead of by a check. The model goes with them because a model name belongs to a provider; leaving it in the environment would be the same disagreement one field over. The rejected alternative was a built-in list of providers mapping a name to a destination. That picks providers on the user's behalf, which `M8-ONLINE-BE-170` explicitly declined to do (2026-09-24), and it would have to be maintained. `online_ai_api_path` stays configuration: it says where a provider mounts the OpenAI-compatible endpoint, not which provider.

**Why `settings` and not a table.** `db.models.Setting`'s own docstring: every setting added later would otherwise be a migration on a user's own laptop. One row is also one atomic write, so the key and its provider can never be half-replaced. Reset already deletes it with the rest of `settings`, and "Export everything" already leaves `settings` out; its README reason now names the key.

**Why the destination change revokes, and a same-provider change does not.** A grant authorises a destination for a conversation. Once the key is for somewhere else, that grant is a door to a provider the user no longer holds a key for, so it closes. A new key for the same provider is key rotation: the door is the one the user opened, and closing it would make them re-enable every conversation for no reason they could see. Removal revokes in the same transaction so that online AI is "unavailable, stated plainly" before the next question, not a failure at it (the ticket's acceptance criterion).

**Why the key is redacted by construction, not by a filter.** The key exists in plaintext in exactly three places: the request that stores it, `ProviderKey.api_key` for one send, and the `Authorization` header. `ProviderKey` and `OnlineTarget` mask both secrets in their `repr`, so a log line or a traceback that captures either object does not capture the value. `PUT /settings/online-key` parses its body by hand, because FastAPI's validation error echoes the offending input, and for a body missing one field that input is the whole body. A provider body is already never repeated (`M8-ONLINE-BE-170`). A value-matching log filter was rejected: it would need the plaintext key in the logging module's memory at all times, which widens exactly what this ticket narrows. What proves the result is a test that stores a sentinel, restarts, answers one online turn and has the provider reject the key on a second (echoing it, as real providers do), then reads the logs, the audit records, `messages.trace`, the trace files, "Export everything" and every response for the sentinel. It appears only in the provider's `Authorization` header. A mutation that logs the key fails it.

**Why a rejected key says so.** The ticket's edge case: reported as the provider rejecting it, never as Askwell being broken. A `401`/`403` now reads *"The online provider rejected your key (401). Check it with your provider, or replace it in Settings."* under the existing `refused` reason code, so no interface or record learns a new code.

**Issue #735 is fixed here.** A connection that could not be opened at all is a connection to Askwell's own egress proxy, since every call goes through it. It now reads *"Online AI could not start: Askwell's network gateway is not running."* with reason `failed`. *"The network is unavailable"* is left to the proxy's own `502`, where the proxy tried the destination and could not reach it. Option 1 of the issue, as recommended.

**Why #730 does not land here, and what stops it being skipped.** #730 (Redis has no authentication, so any container on the internal network can write an egress grant) was re-owned to "before `M8-KEY-BE-173` closes", on the reasoning that this ticket makes online AI usable against a real provider. It does not, quite. Every online send is still refused until the disclosure wording is decided (#737), so after this ticket a grant still cannot carry the key anywhere. Redis ACLs are a change to every service's connection, the proxy's permissions and the installers' secret generation. That is a ticket of its own, not a side effect of storing a key. Re-owning it again, with nothing enforcing the order, is how it would never land. So the order is now a test: `test_no_online_send_is_possible_until_redis_is_authenticated` fails as soon as `online.DISCLOSURE` is set while the `redis` service in `compose.yaml` has neither `--requirepass` nor `--aclfile`. #737 cannot be closed without #730 first.

**Consequences.** A fresh install cannot go online until a key is stored, and says so with *"no provider key is stored"* instead of *"no online provider is configured"*. A locked install says it is locked. There is no screen to store a key yet: that is `M8-KEY-FE-174`, which this endpoint serves. Whitespace and malformed values are refused by the backend, naming the field and never the value, so the interface can show the server's sentence. An install that had set `ASKWELL_ONLINE_AI_DESTINATION`/`_MODEL` for testing loses them silently: `compose.yaml` no longer passes them, and nothing reads them. No shipped install set them; they were empty by default.

**Refs:** `M8-KEY-BE-173`; issues #255, #730, #734, #735, #737; `docs/decisions.md` 2026-09-24 (`M8-ONLINE-BE-170`, `M8-ONLINE-SEC-169`), 2026-09-23 (the credit system is dropped); `api/src/askwell/provider_key.py`, `api/src/askwell/online.py`.

## 2026-09-24 — `M8-ONLINE-OBS-172`: there is no transmitted record, because there is no billing; the local record of each provider request is kept by reference, never by text; a refused request counts as sent

**Decision.** Askwell transmits nothing to itself. The ticket's "transmitted record" was a billing record for Askwell's credit service, and #45's four fields (token counts, timestamp, model, opaque account id) were its specification. #255 dropped the credit service on 2026-09-13, so that record has no recipient and is not built, and #45's list governs nothing now. What the ticket still asks for, and what this change builds, is the local half: every provider request a turn makes is recorded on this machine, and the user can read it per turn. `askwell.inference.provider.OnlineClient` leaves a `Transmission` after every call: destination, model, `sent_at`, `request_bytes` (the length of the exact body handed to the network, serialised by Askwell rather than by `httpx` so the number is the bytes sent), `content_sent`, the provider's status code, and an outcome (`answered`, `stopped`, or the failure's reason code). `askwell.ask` adds what the body was made of, by reference: the prompt version, that the question was included, the chunk ids of the passages, the memory fact and schema note ids, and whether a clarification answer was included. That entry goes on `messages.trace.backend.transmission` and into a new interaction record, `online_ai_request`, in the same transaction as the turn's `ask_asked` record. The trace panel's "show what was sent" reads it: where, which model, when, how many bytes, which parts, and how it ended.

**Why a separate record, not a field on `ask_asked`.** The ticket says the local interaction record stays "unchanged in shape", and that online mode "adds a record and never replaces one". A new key on `ask_asked` would change its shape for every consumer that reads it (the observability count, the export, the verifier). A second record kind in the same store is what web search already does (`web_search_escalated`, `web_search_grant_opened`), so the pattern existed. A test compares the `ask_asked` keys of an online turn and a local one and requires them to be equal.

**Why identifiers and not the text that was sent.** "Exactly what was transmitted" pulls toward storing the request body. That body is the question and the passages. Passages are encrypted at rest when a passphrase is set (`M7-SEC-BE-152`). A plaintext copy in the trace ring buffer or the interaction log would sit outside that encryption, and the interaction log is exported. So the record names each part and its identifiers, and the passages themselves stay where they are. The exact bytes are still pinned: `request_bytes` equals the length of the body the provider received, and a test checks that against the mock provider's own count. A SHA-256 of the body was considered and rejected. The body travels inside TLS, so no capture the user can make would ever produce a hash to compare it with, and a hash of confidential text is one more derived copy with no reader.

**Why a refused request counts as sent.** A provider that answers 401 or 429 has already received the body. Recording that as "nothing sent" because the answer came from the local model would be the one misstatement this ticket exists to prevent. `content_sent` is false only when the connection itself was never made: the proxy refused the tunnel, or nothing answered. Only then is it certain the body stayed. Every other failure, a timeout included, counts as sent. Over-reporting what left is the safe direction to be wrong in. The trace shows the request on a local fallback answer too, not only on an online one.

**Why no token counts.** They were a billing field. Under #255 the bill is between the user and their provider, and the provider's own dashboard is the authority on it. The OpenAI-compatible stream only reports usage when the request asks for it (`stream_options`), and adding that would change the payload that #737 is describing. So nothing is added to the request for this ticket, and no half-populated token field is recorded.

**What this does not settle.** *What the provider receives* is #737, still open and still a product call: the disclosure wording, and whether the payload is narrowed. This record describes whatever the payload is, so it does not depend on #737's answer, and it needs no change when #737 is decided. The ticket's "network capture confirms nothing beyond it left" cannot be walked today, because every online send is refused until #737 is decided and there is no key until `M8-KEY-BE-173`. The independent capture is `M8-ONLINE-TEST-176`'s own acceptance criterion, and it stays there. The body's fields are pinned in the meantime by a test that reads the request off the wire and allows only `model`, `messages`, `max_tokens`, `temperature` and `stream`, and only standard headers. A failure to write the record fails the turn's write as a whole, the same as `ask_asked` (C6, `M1-ASK-OBS-041`). The ticket's one fixed edge case, that a failed *billing transmission* must never fail the answer, has nothing left to apply to.

**Consequences.** `docs/audit-log.md` §6 no longer describes a billing connection, and its §9 item for #45 is closed. The ticket's Scope line "the transmitted record's exact fields" is answered with "none". Settings gets no new view. The interaction export already carries every `online_ai_request` record, and `GET /network` already counts the permitted requests per conversation (`M8-ONLINE-SEC-169`). The trace panel's placeholder `backend.sent` string, built before M8 and never written by anything, is replaced by the structured `transmission`.

**Refs.** `docs/backlog/M8-the-paid-upgrade.md` `M8-ONLINE-OBS-172`; issues #255, #45, #737; `api/src/askwell/inference/provider.py` (`Transmission`), `api/src/askwell/ask.py` (`_sent_contents`, `_transmission_record`, `ONLINE_AI_REQUEST`); `web/lib/trace.ts` (`transmissionLines`), `web/components/ask/trace-panel.tsx`; `api/tests/test_inference_provider.py`, `api/tests/test_ask_online.py`, `web/lib/trace.test.ts`.

---

## 2026-09-25 — Correction: the first approved online-AI disclosure was false, and is replaced

**Decision:** The disclosure recorded earlier today is withdrawn. The payload is kept as it is and stated honestly. Version `1` of the statement is now, verbatim: *"When you ask in this conversation, Askwell sends your online AI provider your question, the passages from your files that it found relevant to it, and what Askwell knows about your material that bears on the question: facts you have taught it, conclusions it has drawn about your files and databases on its own — including the names of your tables and columns — and, occasionally, a single value from one of your tables that it used to work out how the dates in a column are written. What Askwell knows is not tied to one conversation, so something you taught it elsewhere can be included. It does not send whole files, database query results, or the questions and answers from earlier turns. A question Askwell cannot answer from your files sends nothing."*

**Why:** The earlier entry said the draft was "read from the code, not assumed". It was not read carefully enough, and the orchestrating session is the one that approved it without checking. The build agent for `M8-FIX-BE-178` checked every clause and halted sixteen times rather than set it. It found three untrue claims. "Facts you have taught Askwell": `retrieve_relevant_facts` also returns inferred facts and schema notes, and orders them by origin without filtering on it. "Does not send database rows": `askwell.table_infer` writes the cell value that settled a column's date format into a schema note, and schema notes are sent. "Nothing from other conversations": memory is global.

Two fixes were put to the product owner. Narrowing the payload to user-taught facts, with no cell values, would have kept the original sentence true. It needed a ticket and an eval run, and online answers would lose the inferences that make them as good as local ones. Rewording to match the payload keeps the answer quality and asks the sentence to carry more. The product owner chose the rewording. The replacement was checked clause by clause against `compose_conflict`, `delimit_candidates` and `table_infer` before it was shown for approval.

**What this entry is really about:** a statement of what leaves the machine is a C1 promise the user relies on to decide whether confidential material may go. The safeguard that caught this was an agent that refused to set text it could not verify. The approval process had no such check: the orchestrator restated a draft's own claim of accuracy instead of re-reading the code. Any future change to this statement, or to what is sent, has to be verified against the code first.

**Consequences:** `M8-FIX-BE-178` is unblocked with the new text and told to re-verify before setting it. The earlier entry stays as written, because this log is append-only, and this one supersedes it.

**Refs:** #753, #737; `M8-FIX-BE-178`; `api/src/askwell/agent/conflict.py`, `api/src/askwell/memory.py`, `api/src/askwell/table_infer.py`; product owner's decision, 2026-09-25.

---

## 2026-09-25 — Askwell is relicensed to GPLv3, so that spoken answers ship

**Decision:** Askwell moves from Apache-2.0 to GPL-3.0-or-later. `AGENTS.md` C9 changes from "must permit redistribution under Apache-2.0" to "must be GPLv3-compatible, permit commercial use and redistribution, and not be gated". The release licence gate keeps refusing GPL-2.0-only, AGPL, SSPL, non-commercial, no-derivatives and unlicensed material, and stops refusing GPLv3. The mechanics are `M8-FIX-DOC-179`.

**Why:** Voice synthesis needs `phonemizer` (GPLv3+) through `kokoro-onnx`, and the release gate built by `M7-DOC-DOC-163` failed on it (#619). The product owner requires voice in both directions. Every alternative was checked against PyPI on 2026-09-25 rather than from memory, and all route English pronunciation through espeak-ng, which is GPL: `espeakng` GPLv3, `piper-tts` GPL-3.0+, and even `misaki`, Apache-2.0 at its core, pulls `phonemizer-fork` and `espeakng-loader` in its English extra.

Three ways out were put to the product owner. Removing espeak for a dictionary-only pronunciation keeps Apache-2.0 but mispronounces exactly what people's documents are full of: names, jargon, acronyms. Shipping voice input only keeps the licence and does not give what was asked for. Relicensing to GPLv3 gives voice both ways today with no loss of quality.

The product owner first asked whether switching to **MIT** would resolve it. It would not, and this is worth recording because it is the natural first guess: the conflict does not come from Askwell's licence being Apache-2.0. It comes from GPLv3's own condition that a distributed work including GPL code be offered under GPL terms. MIT and Apache-2.0 are both permissive, and neither can override that. Only a GPL-compatible licence for the whole work resolves it.

The trade-off, accepted knowingly: GPLv3 is copyleft. Anyone who distributes a *modified* Askwell must release their changes under GPLv3 too, and some organisations avoid GPL software as policy. This reverses the original reason for Apache-2.0 — "chosen deliberately for contribution and adoption", recorded in the PyMuPDF entry. That cost is smaller here than it looks. Askwell earns nothing from its licence, is free, and has no commercial fork to protect, and the people it is for install and use it; they do not redistribute it. Nothing has been published, so there is no Apache-2.0 release in anyone's hands to reconcile with.

This is not legal advice. A lawyer's review before the first public release is advisable.

**Consequences:** C9 now reads "GPLv3-compatible". A future GPL-2.0-only dependency is still a blocker, since GPL-2.0-only is incompatible with GPLv3. PyMuPDF (AGPL) stays rejected: AGPL's network clause is a different obligation. Reversing this later means removing every GPL dependency first, which is the engineering the relicence avoided.

**Refs:** #619; `M8-FIX-DOC-179`; `M7-DOC-DOC-163`; `AGENTS.md` §3 C9; product owner's decision, 2026-09-25.

---

## 2026-09-25 — The online-AI disclosure is approved as written

**Decision:** The statement shown before an online conversation's first send is, verbatim, version `1`: *"When you ask in this conversation, Askwell sends your online AI provider your question, the passages from your files that it found relevant to it, and any facts you have taught Askwell that bear on it. It does not send whole files, earlier questions, database rows or anything from other conversations. A question Askwell cannot answer from your files sends nothing."* The mechanics are `M8-FIX-BE-178`.

**Why:** Online AI refuses every send until this exists, by design: the product never sends something it cannot describe. The draft in #737 was read from the code, not assumed. `M8-ONLINE-BE-170` sends the system prompt, the passages that cleared the threshold, the relevant memory facts and schema notes, and the question, which is exactly what the local model receives. Narrowing the payload first, for example by dropping memory facts, was rejected. It would change the prompt and need an eval run, to remove the one ingredient that makes an online answer as good as a local one. The only edit from the draft is `<provider>` becoming "your online AI provider", so that the statement is one constant rather than a template that can drift.

**Consequences:** The statement lands only after Redis is authenticated (`M8-FIX-SEC-177`). An agent-written guard, `test_no_online_send_is_possible_until_redis_is_authenticated`, fails the build otherwise, because with a key stored, an unauthenticated Redis lets any container open a paid route to the user's provider. Any later change to what is sent needs a new version of this statement and a fresh confirmation from every conversation.

**Refs:** #737, #730; `M8-FIX-SEC-177`, `M8-FIX-BE-178`; `api/src/askwell/online.py`; product owner's decision, 2026-09-25.

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M8-ONLINE-FE-171`: the disclosure is enforced by the server, not only the screen; the confirmation is per conversation and per statement version, and outlives the authorisation; the undefined wording is `None` in code, tracked as #737; the marker reads the record; Settings loses its switch

**Decision.** `askwell.online.DISCLOSURE` is the single statement of what an online conversation sends, as a `(version, text)` pair. It is `None`. Confirming it (`POST /conversations/{id}/online/disclosure` with the version shown) writes an `online_ai_disclosure_confirmed` decisions record naming the conversation and the version. A send needs the conversation online *and* the current version confirmed (`OnlineState.send_permitted`). `POST /ask` refuses an online conversation's question with `409` before inserting anything when that is false, and `askwell.ask._online_client` returns no provider client. So while `DISCLOSURE` is `None`, nothing can be confirmed and nothing is sent. `GET /conversations/{id}/online` adds `used_online` (any `online_ai_enabled` record for the conversation), `disclosure` and `send_permitted`. `POST /conversations` creates an empty local conversation. In the Ask screen, the marker, the disclosure and the per-conversation switch all sit in the composer's sticky band, the Ask button is disabled while the send is refused (the draft is kept), and each finished turn in a conversation that has used online AI is labelled *Answered locally* or *Answered online · model* from its `model_identity`. A turn that never reached the provider in a conversation that was online when asked records `requested: online, sent_online: false` in its trace backend. The Settings section's inert switch is removed.

**Why the server enforces it, when the ticket is a frontend ticket.** The acceptance criterion is "the product must never send something it cannot describe", and the screen is not the only way into generation. Voice drives `askwell.ask._generate` directly, and any caller of `POST /ask` bypasses a disabled button. A rule that only a component keeps is one refactor from gone. Two checks were chosen, not one. `POST /ask` refuses, so the user is told and nothing is recorded, rather than quietly getting a local answer they did not expect. `_online_client` also refuses, because it is the one point every send passes through. Rejected: gating `POST /conversations/{id}/online` itself on the disclosure, so that enabling *is* confirming. The ticket's walkthrough enables first and expects the disclosure to follow, and "switch it on for this conversation" and "yes, send this kind of content" are two decisions. Merging them would make the disclosure a click-through on the way to a switch.

**Why per conversation and per version, and why it outlives the authorisation.** The ticket's edge cases pull two ways. A conversation switched online mid-thread must see the disclosure before its first online send. A conversation resumed weeks later must *not* see it again. Authorisations lapse after four hours and on every restart (`M8-ONLINE-SEC-169`), so tying confirmation to the authorisation would re-ask every afternoon and train the user to click through. Tying it to the conversation meets both cases. The version is there because the statement will change: once #737 is answered, and again whenever what is sent changes. A confirmation of words that are no longer in force is not consent to the new ones, so a changed version is asked again, and a confirmation naming a stale version is refused by name. The record is append-only in the decisions store, so it cannot be quietly undone, and it is also the audit requirement.

**Why `None` rather than placeholder text, and why a new issue.** The ticket forbids sending under a vague disclosure. A placeholder string that sounds like a disclosure is exactly how a vague one ships. `None` cannot be confirmed by construction, and a test pins that it is `None` until #737 changes it. The ticket names its blocker as "the decision about what online mode transmits". That was #45, and #45 was about billing telemetry to Askwell. It was resolved, and then made moot when credits were dropped (the 2026-09-23 entry below says so). Nobody owned *what the provider receives*, so the gate would have stayed shut with nobody deciding to keep it shut. #737 is that decision. It records what `M8-ONLINE-BE-170` actually sends, read from `compose_conflict`: the system prompt, the above-threshold passages with chunk ids but not filenames, relevant memory facts and schema notes, and the question. It recommends wording. The wording is a product call, because a user relies on it to decide whether confidential material may leave, so it was not chosen here.

**Why the marker reads the record, not only the live state.** A marker that disappears when the authorisation lapses would tell someone resuming a conversation nothing about what it already sent. `used_online` comes from the conversation's own `online_ai_enabled` records, so it survives lapse and restart. It reads "was on … local now", which is true, and says nothing it cannot know. The marker is `--ink` weight on `--surface` rather than a colour, because `design-system.md` §2 reserves colour for how Askwell knows a thing. It sits in the composer band because that is the one region that is never scrolled away, and it is next to the button that sends.

**Why Settings loses its switch.** Acceptance says there is no global online setting. An inert switch labelled "Use online AI" in Settings is what a global setting looks like, whatever it does. The section now says where the switch is.

**Why `answer_reset` (#733) and the per-turn label (#734) landed here.** Both issues recommended this ticket. Both are about what the conversation says about online mode, and both were small. The label reads `model_identity.source`, which the server already sets to `online` only for an answer the provider wrote. That makes it correct for every local path (database, tool loop, web, fallback) without a new field.

**Consequences.** Online AI cannot send anything until #737 sets `DISCLOSURE`. That change should come with its own decisions entry. The frontend holds one conversation per page load, so "a conversation resumed weeks later" can only be walked through the API today. A past conversation cannot be reopened in the interface (#199). The server keeps everything the marker needs for when it can. Voice turns go to a conversation of their own and are never online. `#735` (the proxy-down message) is re-owned to `M8-KEY-BE-173`, the next ticket to change the provider client.

**Refs.** `docs/backlog/M8-the-paid-upgrade.md` `M8-ONLINE-FE-171`; issues #737, #733, #734, #735, #255, #45, #199; `api/src/askwell/online.py`, `api/src/askwell/ask.py` (`POST /conversations`, the `POST /ask` gate, `_online_client`, `_local_backend`); `web/lib/online-conversation.ts`, `web/components/ask/online-conversation.tsx`, `web/components/ask/ask-state.tsx`, `web/lib/ask.ts`, `web/components/settings/online-ai.tsx`; `api/tests/test_online.py`, `api/tests/test_ask_online.py`, `web/lib/online-conversation.test.ts`, `web/lib/online-ai.test.ts`.

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M8-ONLINE-BE-170`: only the answer's generation goes online, over the OpenAI-compatible chat stream; every provider failure answers locally and says which; a failure mid-answer withdraws and restarts; one request per tunnel; the ticket's "no key" line is superseded by #255

**Decision.** A conversation switched to online AI sends one step of a document-grounded turn to the provider: generating the answer from the prompt `compose_conflict` already built. Retrieval, reranking, the threshold and abstention decision, the C7 delimiting, claim segmentation, citation resolution and every record run exactly as before, and before and after the call, not around it. The backend is `askwell.inference.provider.OnlineClient`. It speaks the OpenAI-compatible chat-completions stream at `https://<ASKWELL_ONLINE_AI_DESTINATION><ASKWELL_ONLINE_AI_API_PATH>`, through the egress proxy, with the conversation's `askwell.online.proxy_credentials` as `Proxy-Authorization`. It yields the local client's own `StreamChunk`. The model is `ASKWELL_ONLINE_AI_MODEL`, and online AI is unavailable unless both it and the destination are set (`askwell.online.configured`). Whether a turn goes online is read at the moment of generating, from `online.get_state`, so a lapse or revocation during retrieval answers locally. Any failure raises `OnlineFailed` with one of six reason codes: `network_unavailable` (the proxy's 502, or no route), `not_authorised` (the proxy's 403), `refused` (a provider 4xx), `rate_limited` (429), `failed`, and `interrupted`. The turn then answers the same prompt locally, emits a `backend` step naming the reason, and closes the answer with "Answered by the local model. <reason>". A failure after tokens have streamed also emits `answer_reset`, and the local answer starts clean: text, citations and fact usage are cleared first. `messages.trace.backend` is `{mode, model, destination}` for an online answer. For a local answer in an online conversation it is `{mode: local, model, requested: online, fallback: {reason_code, detail}}`. The interaction record carries `backend`, `model` and `online_fallback`.

**Why only the answer.** It is the one generation whose prompt is chat-shaped already: a system prompt and a user message, split only by how the local server wanted them concatenated. The tool loop, SQL generation and the web-escalation answer are written against the local model's raw completion format, with a `<think>` prefill and a tool-call grammar the loop parses. They are measured only against that model. Sending them to a provider unchanged would be a prompt change with no eval run, which `AGENTS.md` §4 forbids, and on the path that reaches the user's real database. So they stay local, and each such turn's record says `local`, which is true. The gap is visible and filed (#734), not hidden. Rejected: routing everything online with the existing prompts. It would have met "generation uses the online backend" literally, and nothing would have measured whether the answers were any good.

**Why the OpenAI-compatible chat stream.** The local server already speaks it (`docs/decisions.md`, 2026-08-10, where that compatibility was chosen so the client "could be pointed elsewhere"). It is also the shape most hosted providers offer, and `M8-KEY-BE-173` has not chosen a provider. The path is configuration because providers differ in where they mount it, not in what it takes. A provider with a different wire format is a second class behind the same `StreamChunk` interface, which is the seam the ticket's assumption names. Rejected: a provider SDK. Every one reads keys and proxies from the environment in its own way, and at least one would be a new dependency making calls this module cannot see.

**Why every failure falls back, and why they are told apart.** The ticket requires that the user is never blocked, and a free local model is always there. The reasons are separated because each has a different fix, and only one of them is a statement about the network. "The network is unavailable" is correct only when the user asked for something that needs it. The proxy's 502 is the one place Askwell can say it truthfully, because the proxy tried the destination and failed. A provider's response body is never repeated in the message. Providers echo keys back in error text, and a message reaches the trace, the audit log and the screen. The body is dropped here rather than redacted later by `M8-KEY-BE-173`.

**Why a mid-answer failure withdraws and restarts, rather than finishing locally.** Continuing locally from half an online answer would give one answer from two models, with citations resolved against a sentence neither wrote whole. Keeping the half and stopping would fail the turn, which the ticket forbids. So the half is withdrawn, and the local answer is the whole answer. The live interface does not yet act on `answer_reset` (#733). The stored message is correct regardless.

**Why one request per tunnel.** A fresh `httpx` client per call, closed after it, means one `CONNECT` per provider request. The proxy's per-conversation count, the only number the user can read, is then also the request count, and #731's question is answered by construction rather than by a second counter. It costs one TLS handshake per answer, which is noise beside a generation. A test pins it: two calls produce two `CONNECT`s. If a future caller pools, it must count requests itself.

**Why the ticket's "never holds a key" line is not followed.** It was written for the credit model, which #255 dropped on 2026-09-13: the user now brings their own key. This ticket adds no key field and stores nothing, so "there is no field anywhere for a third-party key" is still true after it. `OnlineTarget.api_key` is how the backend presents a key (`Authorization: Bearer`), which is the assumption `M8-KEY-BE-173` states ("the provider abstraction already knows how to present it"). Nothing passes one until that ticket lands. A real provider therefore refuses, and the turn answers locally saying the provider refused. That is the true state of an install with no key, not a stub pretending otherwise.

**Why an online answer's `model_identity` changes, and why the local model's name moved.** `messages.model_identity` drives the unvalidated-model marker (`M7-SET-FE-146a`), and it describes the local model file. On an online answer that marker would say a file wrote something it did not, so an online answer's identity becomes `{source: "online", display_name: <model>}`. The interface's marker checks for `user_supplied` only, so it shows nothing for an online answer. Found while verifying live: every local record named its model `model`. That value is the stem of `inference_model_path`, a host-supervisor setting this container is never given. The turn's model is now the display name captured at question time, with the old stem only as a fallback. Without this, "the backend and model on every turn" was not true for the local half.

**Consequences.** A fresh install still cannot go online: destination and model are both empty by default. The throughput figure in Settings excludes online turns, because a provider sends no llama.cpp timings and `generation` stays `None`. The `/network` counts still attribute each online answer to its conversation. The SSE stream has a new event, `answer_reset`, and a new step kind, `backend`. `M8-KEY-BE-173` supplies the key through `provider.target(..., api_key=...)` inside `askwell.ask._online_client`, and replaces the destination setting. #730 (Redis has no authentication) is re-owned to land before `M8-KEY-BE-173` closes, because that is when online AI becomes usable against a real provider. This ticket does not make it so.

**Refs.** `docs/backlog/M8-the-paid-upgrade.md` `M8-ONLINE-BE-170`; issues #255, #730, #731, #733, #734; `api/src/askwell/inference/provider.py`, `api/src/askwell/ask.py` (`_online_client`, `_backend_record`, the compose loop), `api/src/askwell/online.py` (`configured`), `api/src/askwell/config.py`; `api/tests/test_inference_provider.py`, `api/tests/test_ask_online.py`.

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M8-ONLINE-SEC-169`: a conversation's online-AI authorisation is bound to a credential, not only a destination; the proxy's grant is the authority and every disagreement resolves to local; in-flight tunnels are cut; the destination is configuration until `M8-KEY-BE-173`

**Decision.** Enabling online AI for a conversation (`POST /conversations/{id}/online`, `askwell.online.enable`) mints a random credential for that conversation, and opens a third egress grant shape, `askwell:egress:conversation:<id>`, naming one `host:port` and holding the credential's SHA-256, with a Redis expiry of `Settings.online_ai_authorisation_ttl_seconds` (4 hours). The proxy forwards to that destination only for a `CONNECT` that presents the credential in a standard `Proxy-Authorization: Basic` header. The credential itself is held only in the API process's memory. The proxy counts each such connection in the global permitted counter *and* against `"<conversation>\t<destination>"` in `askwell:egress:permitted_by_conversation`, which `GET /network` returns with the live authorisations. `conversations.ai_backend` records the state; the grant decides it. Every read reconciles the two towards local. Disabling (`DELETE`) closes the grant before touching the database. An open tunnel re-reads its grant every second and is cut when the grant is gone or was replaced under a new credential. The API at startup, and the proxy at startup, close every conversation grant; the API also sets every `online` conversation back to `local`. Enabling and every ending (`disabled`, `lapsed`, `restart`) are decisions records naming the conversation and the destination. The destination is `ASKWELL_ONLINE_AI_DESTINATION`, empty by default, which makes online AI unavailable rather than pointed somewhere.

**Why a credential, when the two existing grants have none.** The ticket's first acceptance criterion is that no other conversation gains access. The proxy sees connections, not conversations: every conversation's traffic comes from the same `api` container, so neither the destination nor the source address can tell a local conversation from an online one. The turn grant (`M6.5-WEB-SEC-187`) and the update-check permit (`M7-UPDATE-BE-161`) accept that, because each is open for seconds around one call Askwell itself makes. A conversation grant is open for hours, and for that long any dependency or any other conversation reaching the same host would have gone out with it. Some claim had to travel with the request, and `Proxy-Authorization` is the standard place a proxy client already puts one — `httpx` sends it from the userinfo of the proxy URL, so `M8-ONLINE-BE-170` needs no custom transport. This is not hand-rolled authentication in the sense `AGENTS.md` warns against: it is a `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)` capability compared by `hmac.compare_digest` over SHA-256 digests, with nothing invented. Its limit is written down in `askwell.online`'s docstring rather than overclaimed: Redis has no authentication on the internal network, so a compromised container could write a grant of its own. The credential scopes the door against accidental use, not against an attacker who already has the database. That gap is older than this ticket, since it covers every grant shape, and is filed as #730 with Redis ACLs as the recommended fix. Rejected: the decision the M6.5 entry below left open, reusing the turn grant with a long TTL. It would have met "one destination" and "time-bound" and failed "no other conversation".

**Why the credential lives only in memory.** It makes "a restart does not silently restore an authorisation" true twice over. Redis here is `appendonly`, so a grant does survive a crash. Startup closes it, and even before startup gets there it is unusable, because the process that could present the credential is gone. Persisting the credential, encrypted or not, would give up that property and buy nothing, because a restart revokes anyway.

**Why reconcile towards local.** The two stores can disagree: the grant can lapse on its TTL, a proxy restart can clear it, or a commit can fail after it opened. Resolving towards online would mean re-opening a door nobody just asked for, which is the drift C1 forbids. So a conversation marked online with no usable grant is put back to local, with a `lapsed` record. A grant with no online conversation behind it is closed. A failed commit after enabling closes the grant it just opened. The `lapsed` record is written when the lapse is noticed, not at the moment of expiry. Its payload says what ended, not when. That was accepted in place of a background sweeper for an event with no user-visible consequence until the next read.

**Why in-flight is *cancelled*, not *completed*.** The ticket asked for one or the other, stated. "Completed" sounds gentler, but an HTTP client pools its `CONNECT` tunnel and sends later requests down it. A proxy that checked only at `CONNECT` would let a pooled connection keep talking for as long as the client kept it open after the user said stop. The proxy re-reads the grant every `CONVERSATION_RECHECK_SECONDS` (1 s) and aborts both sides when it is gone or its credential changed. The one-second window is the honest bound on "immediately". A request already fully sent in that second has left the machine, and cancelling cannot unsend it. Pub/sub revocation would close the window further. It was rejected as a second moving part for a gain under a second.

**Why "the conversation ends" is defined rather than built.** Askwell has no ended state for a conversation: no close, archive or delete. Inventing one here would be a product decision under a security ticket. The authorisation ends when the user disables it, when the time bound lapses, when Askwell or its proxy restarts, or when a reset deletes the conversation (`askwell.reset` now revokes all). If a per-conversation delete is ever built, it has to call `online.disable` first.

**Why the destination is configuration for now.** `M8-KEY-BE-173` owns which provider the user's key belongs to, and so which destination is authorised. Choosing a provider here would make that ticket's decision under this one's number. Empty by default means a fresh install cannot enable online AI at all, and says why (`409`, `NOT_CONFIGURED`), which is the honest state until a provider exists. `test_there_is_no_allowlist_to_configure` still holds: this setting names one destination that a conversation may be granted. It opens nothing by itself.

**Consequences.** `askwell.egress` now has three authorisation shapes, one per scope: install-wide sticky (`PERMITTED_HOST_KEY`), turn (`GRANT_KEY_PREFIX`) and conversation (`CONVERSATION_GRANT_KEY_PREFIX`). Only the conversation shape is bound to a caller. The proxy reads `CONNECT` headers only when a conversation grant names the requested destination, so every other refusal still happens on the request line alone. `M8-ONLINE-BE-170` must route its provider calls through `askwell.online.proxy_credentials(conversation_id)`. A request without them is refused by design, and that refusal is the proof the local-conversation edge case asks for. The settings screen lists permitted connections by conversation and marks one whose authorisation is standing. The per-conversation counts survive a reset, as the refusal counter does. There is no UI to enable online AI yet. That is `M8-ONLINE-FE-171` and `M8-KEY-FE-174`.

**Refs.** `docs/backlog/M8-the-paid-upgrade.md` `M8-ONLINE-SEC-169`; issues #255, #730; `docs/architecture.md` §5 and §5.1; the `M6.5-WEB-SEC-187` entry below; `api/src/askwell/online.py`, `api/src/askwell/egress.py`, `api/src/askwell/network.py`, `api/src/askwell/reset.py`; `api/tests/test_online.py`, `api/tests/test_egress.py`, `api/tests/test_network.py`.

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M7-FIX-FE-170`: conflict positions are laid out client-side from the answer text, not sent as a new field; positions above the conflict line are accepted; fewer than two cited positions stays prose; the cards drop "Added"

**Decision.** The answer text still carries the conflict. `layoutConflict` (`web/lib/answer-annotations.ts`) splits a conflict answer's cleaned text into the prose before the positions, the positions, uncited text among them, and the prose after. Each position is one cited sentence under the claim ordinal `segmentClaims` gives it over the whole text. Positions are taken from the paragraph after the "Conflicting sources on …:" line. When that paragraph has no cited sentence, they are taken from the run of lines directly above the line, provided each of those lines is blank or ends in a cited sentence. Fewer than two positions renders as ordinary prose. Records are ordered by `sortByDateAndSupersession`, the order the margin already uses. The date on each record, and on each conflict card, is `documentDateLabel`: the document's own date to its stated precision, with where it came from, or "Date unknown". The cards no longer show the added date.

**Why.** A structured `positions` field on the `done` event was rejected. The server has nothing more reliable than this same text to build it from, since detection is prompt-driven (`askwell.agent.conflict`'s docstring). A second copy would also have to agree with `split_conflict_answer` and with stored answers written before it existed. Reading the text keeps every stored conversation renderable. Accepting positions *above* the line is not a guess about some other model. The model this machine runs writes the positions first and the conflict line last. The live answer #643 was filed against has exactly that shape, and a below-only layout left it as the run-on the ticket exists to remove. That fallback applies only when the paragraph below has no cited sentence. A model that followed the prompt therefore never has an ordinary cited sentence above the line turned into a position. The two-position floor is there because one cited sentence beside a memory claim written as prose (the prompt's memory-versus-document case) is not two parallel records. Boxing only the cited half would make the document look preferred, which is the thing C4 and `docs/ux/ask.md` §5 forbid. Sentences, not lines, are the unit so that a model that omits the line break still gets two boxes. A bullet inside a claim's match starts a new position, so an uncited bullet with no full stop is shown as uncited text and never absorbed into a cited box. Ordering by date rather than by the model's order follows the §5 rule already applied to the cards. It is the one ordering a reader can check. "Added" was dropped from the cards rather than shown next to the document date because two dates on one card invites reading the wrong one. The added date is still on the document's own page.

**Consequences.** A conflict answer whose positions are neither directly below nor directly above the line still renders as prose, which is the earlier behaviour. The general whitespace loss between cited sentences in `AnswerProse` is not fixed here (#726). The conflict records avoid it because each claim is its own block. Placeholder text the model echoes (#663) still appears around the records. The upward fallback runs across blank lines, so when the conflict line comes last, an unrelated cited paragraph above the positions is boxed as a position too (#727). It cannot stop at a blank line, because the live answer puts blank lines between its positions. The fix is a prompt contract that puts the line first.

**Refs.** `M7-FIX-FE-170`; #643, #704, #726, #663, #727; `docs/ux/ask.md` §5; `AGENTS.md` §3 C4.

---
