## 2026-09-24 — `M7-FIX-BE-170a`: a document's own date is three columns (date, precision, source); modified before created; OOXML core properties read from the zip, not the Office libraries; a future date is ignored from either source; the API sends the date truncated to its precision

**Decision.** `documents` gains `document_date` (the first day of the period), `document_date_precision` (`day` | `month` | `year`) and `document_date_source` (`metadata` | `filename`), null together or set together by a check constraint. `askwell.document_date` fills them at the end of the `extract` stage: format metadata first — PDF `/ModDate` then `/CreationDate`, read from the document `extract_pdf` already has open; OOXML `dcterms:modified` then `dcterms:created` from `docProps/core.xml` — then the filename, recognising only `YYYY`, `YYYY-MM` and `YYYY-MM-DD` as a whole digit run with one consistent `-` or `_` separator. With two filename dates the more specific wins; two equally specific and different gives null. A date later than today, at its own precision, is ignored from either source. Metadata before 1990 is ignored; filenames go back to 1900. Every extraction writes all three columns, including back to null. `GET /documents/{id}` returns `document_date` as `2026`, `2026-03` or `2026-03-15`, never the padded stored value, beside `added_at`. `sortByDateAndSupersession` orders by it and treats an undated document as it treats a not-yet-loaded one. Existing rows are dated on their next re-index; the migration does not backfill.

**Why.** The only date Askwell held was `added_at`, and the conflicting-sources state was ordering two versions of a handbook by the order they were added in. Filename-only was rejected because `Handbook.pdf` with a correct `/ModDate` is the common real case. Metadata-only was rejected because the fixture corpus carries no `/Info` at all, so the walkthrough `M7-FIX-FE-170` depends on would read "date unknown" twice. Precision is a column because `store_hours_2026.pdf` names a year, and "1 January 2026" is a wrong fact that looks right. It is enforced at the API as well as stored, by truncating the string, because a caller that forgets to read a precision field would otherwise render the padding. Source is a column because file metadata is a claimed date: a 2019 document re-exported in 2026 says 2026, and the interface has to be able to say where a date came from. **Modified is read before created** because the usual way a second version comes to exist is opening the first and saving it again. That keeps the original's creation date and moves only the modification date, so created-first would date the 2026 handbook to 2025, which is the mistake this ticket exists to remove. The cost is that a trivial re-save moves the date; the source label is what states that gap. **OOXML is read from the zip directly** because `python-docx` and `python-pptx` substitute default core properties when the part is missing, and `openpyxl`'s default `created` is the moment the workbook was opened: the ingest date again, now labelled as metadata. The part is size-capped and parsed with the standard library, which resolves no external entities; the bundled expat (2.8) refuses entity-expansion bombs, which a test proves. Adding `defusedxml` was rejected as a dependency for what the installed parser already refuses. **A future filename date is ignored**, although the ticket names only future metadata: `budget_2027.xlsx` is about 2027 but was not written then, and the question the date answers is which version is newer, not what period it covers. The filename pattern is narrow on purpose (no compact `YYYYMMDD`, no `2025-26` spans, no mixed separators). A missed date reads as "Date unknown", which is honest; a wrong one reads as a fact. The 1990 floor for metadata exists because PDF dates from 1993 and OOXML from 2006; anything earlier is a zeroed or defaulted clock. The backfill comes from re-index rather than the migration because the date comes from reading the file, and a migration that opens every file on a user's disk would be both slow and outside what a schema change should do.

**Consequences.** A re-index re-dates every document it reaches. Two open defects affect whether it reaches them: cited documents fail at `chunk` (#719), after `extract` has already written the date, and documents with no `ingest_jobs` row stay `queued` (#720). `M7-FIX-FE-170` can now render conflict dates. It must render `document_date` by `document_date_precision`, qualify it by `document_date_source`, and show "Date unknown" for null, never `added_at`. A user-editable date and dates found in document text are not built.

**Refs.** `M7-FIX-BE-170a`; #704, #643, #719, #720; `docs/ux/ask.md` §5; `AGENTS.md` §3 C4.

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M7-QA-TEST-168`: one release checklist over the existing gates; everything is pass or fail; the constraint gates have no acceptance route; release evidence is committed; the walkthrough uses only the installed artefact

**Decision.** `docs/release-checklist.md` lists twelve gates (G1–G12), each with a pass condition, how to run it, and where its evidence lives. It *references* the existing gate documents (offline, restore, security review, notices) rather than restating them. `docs/release-walkthrough.md` is the manual regression script, ordered as a first session runs, each section tagged with the milestone whose headline path it walks. `docs/release-log.md` holds one entry per release; eval, performance and walkthrough evidence that no other log holds is committed under `docs/release-evidence/<version>/`. `docs/release-procedure.md` gains step 3d: no checksums until the log reads `Decision: release` for that exact version. Results are `PASS`, `FAIL`, `BLOCKED` or `ACCEPTED`. `BLOCKED` holds the release. An intermittent failure is `FAIL`. `ACCEPTED` needs an issue, a reason and a follow-up, and is never available for SQL safety, web escalation or abstention, the offline gate, the restore gate, or a security finding against C1–C10. The walkthrough runs nothing a repository checkout has and an installed machine lacks: the OS's own port listing for W1.2, and `askwell-verify` from the install prefix for W9.8. The outbound count is read where a user reads it, Settings → Network activity. G3's scored categories are compared by hand for now. G1 and each release-log entry record the release's schema revision, and the walkthrough reads Settings → About → Crash reports, where a report written during the walkthrough is a `FAIL`.

**Why.** The four gates that existed each had their own document, and nothing said "all of them", so a release could pass the ones someone remembered. Restating each gate's steps was rejected: two copies of a procedure drift, and the copy the maintainer happens to read wins. The checklist points at the gate document, and the pass condition is the log entry that document already produces. The pass-or-fail rule comes from the ticket's own edge case: on a ten-task suite, 0.9 reads as nearly fine and is a failure. The ticket's validation rule, "pass or explicitly accepted", taken literally would allow accepting a SQL safety failure, which contradicts that category's "no exceptions"; so acceptance is closed for the gates that protect a constraint the product exists to keep, and open, visibly, for a missed performance budget or an uncovered platform. Committing evidence rather than leaving it in `eval/results/` (gitignored for development runs) is what makes "recorded per release with evidence attached" still true after the release machine is gone, and keeps it readable offline beside the log that cites it. The abstention row keeps `docs/build-plan.md`'s bar exactly; a stricter worst-of-3 bar was considered and not added, because a checklist inventing a bar is how it and the quality gate start to disagree. Making `eval/bench.py` return a verdict for scored suites (#710) was left out: it is eval tooling with its own tests, and an untested verdict command on the release's critical path is worse than a hand comparison the checklist spells out. An earlier draft of W1.2 and W9.8 ran `scripts/verify-localhost-binding.sh` and a bare `podman compose exec`; on the clean machine the walkthrough requires, those either become `BLOCKED` (holding a release on a script defect) or get run from a dev checkout (testing the wrong thing), so both were rewritten (#717). Shipping those scripts in the bundle was rejected as new shipped surface for a maintainer check. The schema revision sits in the release record because `docs/rollback-and-incidents.md` §1.1 rolls back to "the revision the older release names", and release notes are prose nobody tests. The log's format is covered by the guard tests and is where the next person looks. The crash-report read is in the walkthrough because a crash the product survived leaves no other trace a person would see. A step can look fine while a background task fails behind it, and the local report list is the only place that shows it.

**Consequences.** `api/tests/test_release_checklist.py` fails if the eval table disagrees with `eval/suites/*.json` or the build plan, if a strict row states a number, if a gate loses a column, if the walkthrough stops reaching a landed milestone or a `docs/ux/` screen or starts running a repository script, if `/network` is read with a bare `curl`, or if a named path stops existing. The first release run is expected to be held: #619, the missed answer-latency budget, #625, #689, #559, #698, #699, #712, #590 and #592. The checklist's **Known holds** table names the ones that are not already another gate's own log entry, and a row leaves in the change that closes its issue. M8's walkthrough steps are appended when M8 lands. If a release needs more than two working days of hands-on time, the checklist is split and the split recorded here.

**Refs.** `M7-QA-TEST-168`; #689, #698, #699, #702, #710, #712, #717; `docs/rollback-and-incidents.md` §1.1, §3; `docs/release-procedure.md` §3d.

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M7-OPS-DOC-165`: crash reports are an allow-list, kept locally, never sent; rollback downgrades with the newer build; the ticket's "update delivery is blocked" premise was out of date

**Decision.** A crash report is built from a fixed allow-list of fields: version, component, platform, profile, route template, exception type names and `(location, function, line)` frames. It is written to `/var/lib/askwell/crash-reports`, and the only way off the machine is a person downloading it from Settings → About and attaching it. There is no send path. Rollback is written as two paths. Path A downgrades the schema **with the newer build still installed**, then installs the older release over it. Path B uses an empty database and a backup made by the older version. Each release now records its schema revision. The incident procedure treats "publish a fixed release under a new patch number" as the only in-product signal. The ticket's premise that update delivery is blocked is corrected: `docs/decisions.md` 2026-09-21 unblocked it, and `M7-UPDATE-BE-161` built it.

**Why.** *Allow-list, not scrubbing.* The ticket's edge case is "a crash report containing a filename or a question", and its validation rule is that no report is ever transmitted without the person attaching it. A scrubber that removes file names and questions from a traceback after the fact has to recognise every shape they come in: exception messages, `repr()`s of locals, SQL text, a path parameter, an f-string three frames down. It fails open on the one it was not written for. Building the report only from fields that cannot carry the person's material fails closed. It costs the error message, which is the most useful single line to a maintainer. That was accepted, because a report someone has to read and redact before attaching is a report most people will not attach, and one that leaks a client's name into a public issue is worse than none. Frame paths get the same treatment: only Askwell's own files, `site-packages` and the standard library are named, and anything else is `<other>`. The request's **route template** is kept and its path is not, because `/test-crash/{name}` tells a maintainer which handler failed while `/…/Harlow-v-Pemberton.pdf` tells the world whose file it was.

*Rejected:* Sentry-style automatic reporting, even as an opt-in. The ticket forbids it, and C1's two named exceptions do not include it. Writing the report into the audit log instead of a file was also rejected: the audit log is append-only and hash-chained, so a report could never be deleted after the person decided not to send it, and the log is not something a person attaches to an issue.

*Downgrade with the newer build.* Alembic can only undo a migration it has the file for. The older build does not have the newer migrations, so it cannot roll them back, and it has no startup schema check that would refuse a newer schema. Running the downgrade after installing the older version is therefore impossible, and running the older version against the newer schema without a downgrade is undefined. The order in §1.1 is the only one that works. The downgrade runs as the owner role, from the install's own `.env`. The app role keeps no DDL grant (the same boundary `M7-BACKUP-BE-158` recorded), so the ticket did not widen C6's grant separation.

*Premise corrected.* The ticket says update delivery is blocked and asks the procedure to name the resulting limit. It is no longer blocked. The real limits are narrower and more specific: people who declined the check cannot be reached at all, the check can only say "newer", never "broken", and the feed is currently `main/VERSION`. Documenting the stale "blocked" limit would have been wrong in both directions, overstating one gap and hiding the actual ones. `M7-SET-FE-149` corrected the same stale premise in its own ticket.

**Consequences.** A maintainer gets a stack shape without the message, and may have to ask the reporter for the trace, which `SUPPORT.md` already asks for and tells the person to review first. Three defects found while writing the procedure are filed, not fixed here. #698: installers never migrate, which blocks both rollback paths on every real install and is release-blocking in its own right. #699: the feed tracks `main`. #700: purge leaves volumes and breaks the next install's credentials. The per-platform rollback rehearsal the ticket asks for has run only as a Linux schema round trip. The full walkthrough waits on #698, two published releases, and macOS/Windows hardware (#590, #592). `docs/manual-tests/M7-OPS-DOC-165.md` §B says so rather than recording a pass; #711 owns the rest.

*Background tasks (#715).* The three original hooks miss a task that fails with nobody awaiting it, which is the shape a startup failure on one platform takes. A loop exception handler (`install_loop_handler`) covers every unretrieved task, and reads only `context["exception"]`, never the free-text `context["message"]`. It is not enough on its own: the lifespan keeps `reapply_task` and `discovery_task` and awaits them after `cancel()` at shutdown, which counts as retrieving the exception, so those two also get a done-callback (`report_task_failure`). A failed arq job is still not a crash, because it already has its own surfaced failure. *Restore (#714).* The rehearsal's `POST /restore` answered 500. `inspect_artefact` parsed the manifest outside its `try`, so a manifest that was not JSON, was a list, lacked a key or had a malformed version escaped unhandled. The whole parse now sits inside it and becomes `ArtefactInvalid` (422), naming the error type rather than the message, for the same reason the crash report leaves messages out.

**Refs:** `M7-OPS-DOC-165`; `docs/rollback-and-incidents.md`; `api/src/askwell/crash_report.py`, `api/tests/test_crash_report.py`, `api/src/askwell/restore.py` (`inspect_artefact`), `api/tests/test_restore.py`; issues #711, #714, #715; `web/lib/crash-reports.ts`, `web/components/settings/about.tsx`; `docs/release-procedure.md` §1; issues #698, #699, #700; `docs/decisions.md` 2026-09-21 (update checks), 2026-09-22 (`M7-BACKUP-BE-158`).

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M7-UPDATE-FE-162`: the newer-version line shows when Askwell found the version, dismissal is stored server-side against a named version, and the running version lives in a file, not the database

**Decision.** A known newer version is one line under Version in Settings → About (`web/components/settings/about.tsx`, `web/lib/about.ts`). Its date is when Askwell first found that version (`update_check_latest_known_since`, set only when the found version changes). Dismissing it stores the version the line showed (`update_check_dismissed_version`, `POST /settings/update-check/dismiss`). The line comes back when a newer version than that one is found. "How to upgrade" says what happens to the person's data first, then gives the releases address. "Check now" (issue #693) sits with update checking. An applied upgrade is an `upgrade_applied` decisions record (`version_changed` for a move to an older version). It is written at startup by `askwell.update_check.record_running_version`, which compares this build's version against a file on the state volume (`Settings.running_version_path`, `/var/lib/askwell/running_version`). A first start writes only the file.

**Why.** *The date.* The ticket asks for "the version and its date". The feed is the repository's own `VERSION` file (2026-09-21 feed decision), and it carries no release date. Two ways to get one were rejected. Switching to GitHub's releases API gets a real date, but it is an endpoint with server-side logic, which `docs/ux/settings.md` §7 rules out by name. Adding a date line to `VERSION` breaks `AGENTS.md` §7's rule that the file holds one plain `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH`, and every reader of it. The found date is honest, and it is what the ticket's own scenario describes: "1.2.0 arrived three weeks ago" is from the user's point of view. The label says "Found", never "Released". It moves only when the version changes, so a weekly check that finds the same version again does not make it look new. A version found by a build before this one has no stored date and is shown without one; a date is never made up.

*Dismissal.* It is stored in `settings`, because the ticket says it must hold "until the next version, not until the next launch", and browser storage does not survive a reset of the desktop shell's webview. The request names the version the line showed rather than the server dismissing "whatever is newest". Otherwise a check that finds 1.3.0 between the page loading and the click would dismiss a version the person never saw.

*The running version in a file.* The first attempt at this ticket wrote a baseline `version_first_started` row to `audit_decisions` on first start. Issue #697 found that this forks the chain on every clean-machine restore. The fresh machine's genesis row and the backup's genesis row both chain to `GENESIS`, so `askwell.audit.verify` reports `FORKED` and `chain_verified` is false on the restore release test's golden path. A `settings` key was rejected for two reasons. It would trip restore's existing-data refusal, the same false positive issue #597 describes for `session_secret`. And a restore that brings the backup's own row for that key would collide on the primary key. A file on the `askwell-state` volume is outside everything restore reads and writes. So a first start leaves the database empty, and a machine restored from an older backup records no upgrade it never ran. The record commits before the file is rewritten, so a failed file write repeats the record on the next start rather than losing it. Tolerating a local genesis before a restored one in `verify` (#697's option 3) was rejected, because it would weaken C6's fork detection to hide a row Askwell wrote itself.

*The data-safety statement is one sentence, not one per platform.* The ticket allows a single sentence only if every platform's installer keeps data across an upgrade. `deploy/linux/install.sh`, `deploy/macos/install.sh` and `deploy/windows/install.ps1` each detect a previous install and say "Its data is left untouched; upgrading application files in place." `web/lib/about.test.ts` reads that line from all three, so an installer that stops doing it fails the test that would otherwise leave the statement untrue.

**Consequences.** An install upgraded to this version from an earlier one has no file yet. Its first start at this version writes the baseline and records nothing, so the first `upgrade_applied` record appears at the upgrade after this one. That gap is accepted, because nothing on disk says which version ran before. The line lives in About, the last section of Settings. "Nowhere else" is a test (`updateMarker` may be used only in `components/settings/about.tsx`), not a convention. `ASKWELL_RUNNING_VERSION_PATH` is in `.env.example`. Tests that start the app against a real database point it at `tmp_path`.

**Refs:** `M7-UPDATE-FE-162`; issues #693, #696, #697, #597; `api/src/askwell/update_check.py` (`dismiss`, `record_running_version`, `LATEST_KNOWN_SINCE_KEY`), `api/src/askwell/app.py` (`lifespan`), `api/src/askwell/config.py`; `web/lib/about.ts`, `web/components/settings/about.tsx`; `api/tests/test_update_check.py`, `api/tests/test_restore.py` (`test_a_restore_onto_a_machine_that_has_started_keeps_one_chain`), `web/lib/about.test.ts`; `docs/ux/settings.md` §7; `docs/decisions.md` 2026-09-21 (the feed).

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M7-FIX-BE-172`: the term floor judges each use in its passage, not the word; a term that fails it is dropped, not capped; and the missing conflict was a detector gap, not a ranking one

**Decision.** `askwell.clarify._detect_abbreviations` now puts every all-caps term through a floor before it can become a question. A use of the term is *resolved* when its own passage proves a common-English reading (`_CONTEXT_READINGS`: "AM"/"PM" directly after a clock time). A term is *defined* when some passage in the source spells it out in brackets either side of it, or with "stands for / is short for / means", and the spelling is checked by initials (`_defined_inline`). Only unresolved uses count, both for materiality (still two or more) and for which passages are cited as evidence. A term that is defined, or has no unresolved use, fails `cannot_determine` and is **dropped**. It is not written to memory, not capped, and appears only as a `clarification_dropped` decisions record that says why. Separately, `_detect_contradictions` gained `_TIME_FACT_PATTERN` (`<subject> at|by|until <clock time> AM|PM`). The ranking was not changed, because contradictions were already first.

**Why.** The ticket framed this as a ranking fault: a term definition beat a live conflict drawn from the same two lines. Reading the code showed the ranking was already right (`_TRIGGER_PRIORITY` puts contradictions at 0 and abbreviations at 3, and a test pinned it). The conflict was never detected: `_FACT_PATTERN` only sees `is <number>`, and "stores close at 8 PM" has no "is". So the fix for the second scope line was wiring detection, which the ticket's own Assumption made part of this ticket. A new ranking test pins the order again under the ticket's name.

For the floor, two designs were rejected. **A stoplist entry for "PM"** would have been one line. But the ticket's hardest edge case is a common word used as jargon, and a stoplist silences "the PM signs off" together with "8 PM". The existing `_COMMON_ABBREVIATIONS` comment already warns that an over-eager stoplist launders a real domain term into "known", and that it is the costlier mistake. So the floor looks at each use in its context, and the same token can be dropped in one corpus and asked in another. **Inferring a memory fact from an inline definition** (writing "RFQ = Request for Quotation" at low confidence) was also rejected for now. The ticket says a candidate below the floor is dropped, the definition is already retrievable from the passage that holds it, and a copy in memory would be a second place for it to go stale.

"Dropped rather than deferred" is literal on purpose. A capped candidate still becomes a `memory` row saying "Not asked — ranked N, below the cap", which records it as a question worth asking. A term the source already answers is not one, and recording it as a near-miss would inflate the capped disclosure (`docs/ux/clarifications.md` §5) with things that were never real questions.

The ticket warns against raising the floor until the queue is empty. On `eval/fixtures/corpus` the queue after this change holds one item, the store-hours conflict. It is not empty, and both halves are pinned. The floor is also narrow by construction: only AM/PM have a context reading, and a definition must spell the term by initials. A genuine code (`STCD` in document text, and `st_cd` columns through `askwell.schema_introspect`, which this floor does not touch) is still asked, and a test checks that beside a resolved "PM".

**Consequences.** The heuristic will be wrong at the edges. A bracketed phrase whose initials happen to spell the term ("send to the finance queue (FQ)") counts as a definition. A context reading requires whitespace before the token ("8 PM"), because "8PM" is never counted as a use in the first place; counting it as resolved would cancel a jargon "PM" in the same passage (caught in audit, pinned by a test). Only AM/PM have context readings, so other common-English all-caps words still fall through to being asked until a reading is added for them. Adding one is a one-line change and should come with a test showing the jargon use of the same token is still asked. Value detection for contradictions is still digits-only. The fixture's `conflict_2025/2026` pair disagrees on eight facts written in words, and none is raised: issue #705.

**Refs:** `M7-FIX-BE-172`; `api/src/askwell/clarify.py` (`_CONTEXT_READINGS`, `_defined_inline`, `_unresolved_occurrences`, `_TIME_FACT_PATTERN`); `api/tests/test_clarify.py` (the `M7-FIX-BE-172` section); `docs/memory-and-clarification.md` §1; issue #705.

---

## 2026-09-24 — `M7-SET-FE-150`: the pre-launch online AI section describes the person's own key, not the ticket's credit tier; the switch answers a click rather than ignoring it

**Decision.** Settings → Online AI (`web/components/settings/online-ai.tsx`, wording in `web/lib/online-ai.ts`) is the second section, visible and inert. It says what the feature will be (a larger cloud model for a hard question, and that Askwell works completely without it), that it is chosen per conversation, that it will use an API key from a provider the person already pays with Askwell selling nothing and taking no part in the cost, and that exactly what leaves the machine will be stated before anything is sent. A switch reads "Off. Not available yet." It is `aria-disabled`, not `disabled`: pressing it shows a plain not-available statement, with no waiting list and no email field, and it never reaches "on". `docs/ux/settings.md` §3 is rewritten to the key model to match.

**Why.** The ticket was written before the 2026-09-23 decision that cancelled the credit tier, and two of its acceptance lines now say the opposite of the product: "paid by credit" and "states that no third-party API key is ever requested". `docs/PRD.md` §6 was amended to "if you want a larger model, you bring your own key". Shipping the ticket's words would put a promise on screen that M8 is already scheduled to break (`M8-KEY-FE-174` fills this very section with a key field). That is worse than no section, because the section exists to set expectations honestly. So the ticket's intent is kept — visible, inert, explains the shape, per conversation, no price, nothing collected, payload stated before sending — and its stale sentences are replaced with the decided model. The key statement also says that nothing on the screen asks for a key *today*, which is the true part of the old promise. The rejected option was to stop and ask. The decision is recorded, the PRD already carries it, and the owner made the call. Asking again would hand back a question that was already answered.

`aria-disabled` over `disabled` because the ticket's edge case is "a user trying to enable it — a plain statement". A `disabled` button cannot be clicked, so the attempt would get silence. The Storage section's export-and-prune button uses `disabled`, which is right there: nothing needs to be said beyond the text beside it. Here the attempt itself is the case the ticket names.

Rewriting §3 went past the ticket's scope into issue #658, which recommended waiting for M8's key-storage design. That was reasonable while nothing rendered §3. Now this ticket renders it, and building the section against a spec that describes a cancelled feature would have been building against the wrong assumption on purpose. The rewrite describes only the shape: the key is set or not set, never shown again, and can be replaced or removed. Where the key is entered and how it is masked stay with `M8-KEY-FE-174`.

**Consequences.** `M8-KEY-FE-174` fills this section in, replacing the placeholder switch and payload sentence. `web/lib/online-ai.test.ts` fails if the wording drifts back to credits, purchase, balance, a spending limit or a price. It also fails if the component gains an input, textarea, select, form, `fetch(` or an absolute URL, or if the section moves from second place. The new wording is owner-facing copy and should be read as such.

**Refs:** `M7-SET-FE-150`; issue #658; `docs/ux/settings.md` §3, §8, §9; `docs/PRD.md` §6; `docs/decisions.md` 2026-09-23 (the credit system is dropped); `docs/backlog/M8-the-paid-upgrade.md` `M8-KEY-FE-174`.
