## 2026-09-23 — `M7-PACK-FE-143`: the supervision surface reads the shell's own loop state, not the OS service state; manual control is a channel into the same thread, not a second lock

**Decision:** the supervision/repair surface (`web/src-tauri/shell-assets/supervision.html`, a second bundled window analogous to `starting.html`) shows exactly what `web/src-tauri/src/supervisor.rs`'s own in-process loop (`M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-183`) currently believes about the stack and the inference process — `Handle::status()`, a snapshot read of two `Mutex<ComponentStatus>` the loop writes once per tick — rather than querying `systemctl --user`/`launchctl`/`Get-ScheduledTaskInfo` for the OS-level services `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142` separately registers. Manual Start/Stop/Restart (`Handle::control`) is likewise not acted on directly by the Tauri command handler: it is queued through an `mpsc::Sender<(Target, ControlAction)>` and drained at the top of the loop's own tick, on the one thread that already owns `RestartPolicy`, the child `Process` handle, and every other piece of mutable state the loop touches.

**Why reading the loop's state answers issue #607 rather than reopening it.** `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142`'s own decision (the entry directly below this one) took option 2 on #607: macOS's `LaunchAgent` `KeepAlive` has no configurable restart cap, so it never reaches a distinct OS-level "failed" state the way the Linux unit or the Windows Scheduled Task does — and the chosen fix was to let `state.json`'s heartbeat age be the *one* detection path every platform's repair surface reads, since the shell's own adoption logic (`inference_heartbeat_fresh`) and `api/src/askwell/health.py`'s inference probe already read exactly that file. This surface does read it, but indirectly: `inference_status_now`'s `failed` state comes from the shell's own `RestartPolicy` capping at five attempts, and that policy's `reset()` only fires once `inference_confirmed_ready` — itself a fresh, `ready`-state `state.json` read — has proven a restart. A LaunchAgent-restarted process that keeps crash-looping past what the shell's own five-step backoff would tolerate is, by construction, also failing the shell's own confirmation check, so it reaches this surface's `failed` state on the same evidence the OS-level detection would have used, without a second, macOS-specific reader of the same file. Issue #607 is resolved by this entry (closed, not re-owned further) rather than left pointing at a ticket that has now shipped.

**Why the OS-level services were not queried instead, or in addition.** `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142`'s two extra services exist specifically so the stack and inference start with the session whether or not the desktop shell is ever opened — meaning there are real conditions where they are the only thing running (shell closed, `state.json` heartbeat still fresh from their own foreground process) and conditions where both the OS service and the shell's adopted process are, functionally, the same running thing reported twice. Querying three different platform service managers from Rust (parsing `systemctl` text output, calling into `launchctl`/`ServiceManagement`, or `Get-ScheduledTaskInfo` via a `powershell.exe` subprocess) to reconcile against a loop that already has its own opinion — informed by the same heartbeat file — would have doubled the sources of truth this surface has to agree with itself, for platforms this ticket has no way to test (the same `pkg-config`/`libsoup-3.0` gap, issue #497, that has blocked every Tauri ticket in this milestone from an actual build). The accepted cost: if a user starts Askwell purely via the OS service (shell never opened) and then opens the shell later, the surface reports what the shell's own adoption sees at that moment (`inference_heartbeat_fresh`/`stack_reachable`), which is correct once adopted but has no history of restarts the OS service already made before the shell existed — `restarts: 0` on first open even if the OS service already retried twice. Acceptable: the ticket's own stated need is "what is wrong right now and can I fix it", not a full restart history.

**Why manual control is a channel into the loop rather than a `Mutex<SupervisorState>` the Tauri command locks directly.** The loop already mutates `stack_policy`, `inference_policy`, and the `Option<Child>` slot on its own thread, unguarded by any lock, by design (`M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-183`'s own decision entry below). Adding a second writer (the Tauri command's own async-runtime thread) would have required wrapping all of that in a `Mutex` too, turning every 1.5s poll tick into a lock acquisition for state that is, in practice, read by nobody but that same thread. A channel keeps the loop the sole owner of everything it already owned, and reuses the exact shape `stopping: Arc<AtomicBool>` already established for the one piece of cross-thread signalling this file needed before (`Handle::stop_and_wait`) — a `Stop` command still runs `compose_down`/`terminate_child` on the loop's own thread, never from the command handler, so a manual stop and the loop's own shutdown path share one implementation rather than two.

**Why a native application menu, not a system tray icon, for "reachable from the desktop entry point".** A tray icon needs an OS-level background-process affordance this shell does not otherwise have (`tray-icon` is an optional Cargo feature, unused elsewhere in this crate) and behaves inconsistently across desktop environments in ways a menu bar item does not. A single "Askwell" application menu with one "Supervision…" item, built with `tauri::menu` (already part of the base `tauri` dependency, no new crate), is present the instant the window exists — whether it is still showing `starting.html` or has handed off — which is what "even when the API is down" actually requires: window-chrome-level UI, not page content.

**Why the log-location command says Linux and Windows do not write a file there yet, instead of inventing one.** `$DATA_DIR/logs` is created by every platform's installer (`M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142`), but only macOS's `LaunchAgent`s (`StandardOutPath`/`StandardErrorPath`) actually redirect output into it — Linux's `systemd --user` units and Windows' Scheduled Tasks write to their own platform journal instead. Silently pointing at an empty directory would be exactly the kind of overclaim C6 already warns against for the audit log; adding new log-file redirection for two more platforms is deploy-scripting work outside this ticket's own stated scope (a supervision UI, not a logging pipeline), so the honest, narrower answer — name the folder, say what is and is not in it yet, and give the `journalctl`/Task Scheduler equivalent — is what shipped, with the gap logged as a follow-up rather than left implicit.

**Consequences:** `web/src-tauri/capabilities/supervision.json` is a new capability file scoped to the `supervision` window label alone, granting exactly three commands (`supervision_status`, `supervision_control`, `supervision_log_location`) that `main` never receives; `capabilities/default.json` gains one more (`allow-open-supervision-window`), reachable from both `starting.html` (a repair button, shown only once a cause is named) and the real web app's `StatusBanner` (`web/components/shell/status-banner.tsx`, gated on `window.__TAURI_INTERNALS__` so a plain-browser visit renders nothing). No `api/` change was needed — this ticket's own stated API touchpoint was "supervision state; log paths", both already answerable from the shell process alone. Issue #607 is closed by this entry; #606 (Windows restart-semantics verification) remains open and unrelated, re-owned by `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142`'s own entry, not touched here. A follow-up issue is filed for Linux/Windows not yet writing a log file into `$DATA_DIR/logs` for the stack/inference services, since the honest log-location note above only *names* that gap rather than closing it.

**Refs:** `M7-PACK-FE-143`; `web/src-tauri/src/supervisor.rs`, `src/main.rs`, `shell-assets/supervision.html`, `shell-assets/starting.html`, `capabilities/default.json`, `capabilities/supervision.json`; `web/components/shell/status-banner.tsx`; issue #607 (closed by this entry).

## 2026-09-23 — `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142`: the stack and inference get their own OS service units, separate from the shell's; macOS's restart cap gap is accepted rather than patched

**Decision:** each platform's installer now registers *two more* long-running services beyond the existing shell one — one for the container stack, one for the native inference supervisor — using the platform's own service mechanism (`systemd --user` on Linux, `LaunchAgent` on macOS, a `Scheduled Task` on Windows), independent of whether the desktop shell (`M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-183`'s own in-process supervisor) is ever opened. `podman compose up -d` and a background inference process would leave each platform's failure-detection mechanism (`Restart=on-failure`, `KeepAlive`, `RestartCount`) nothing to watch, since the command that starts them returns immediately — so all three run their process in the *foreground* instead: `podman compose ... up --abort-on-container-exit` turns "a container died" into "the supervised process exited non-zero", which is what each platform's restart mechanism actually needs.

**Why two services and not one, and why separate from the shell's own supervisor.** The ticket's own framing ("the shell supervises while it is running; this ticket is the platform half") makes the split explicit: `M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-183` already spawns and restarts both halves correctly, but only for as long as the shell process is alive, and it starts *because a user opened it* — nothing brings the stack up if the shell is never opened, or keeps it up after the shell closes. Reading `state.json`'s heartbeat (the same file the shell's own adoption logic already reads) means the two supervisors never race: whichever comes up first is what the other adopts, never re-spawns. Two units rather than one per platform so "the two failure causes are reported separately" (this ticket's own Acceptance Criteria) stays true at the OS level too — a stack that hits its restart cap and an inference process that hits its own are independently visible (`systemctl --user status askwell-stack.service askwell-inference.service`; two separate LaunchAgent labels; two separate Scheduled Task names), not folded into one "Askwell is broken" unit.

**Why nothing changed in `api/src/askwell/health.py` or the audit stores.** `GET /health`'s existing per-component TCP probes (`docs/decisions.md`'s own design principle: five separate states, no aggregate boolean) already report a component that failed to come back up as `unreachable` with a reason, regardless of *why* it is down — a systemd unit that gave up after its restart cap looks identical, from the health endpoint's point of view, to a container that was never started, which is the correct behaviour: the health surface's job is "is it reachable", not "why did the supervisor give up", and the latter is answered by each platform's own service state (`systemctl --user status`, `launchctl list`, `Get-ScheduledTaskInfo`) — this ticket's stated Audit/Logging requirement ("supervision events are logged with cause") is satisfied by each mechanism's own journal (systemd's journal captures start/stop/restart automatically; the LaunchAgents write to `$DATA_DIR/logs/*.log`) rather than a new logging path this ticket would have had to invent and keep in sync with three platforms' native ones.

**The macOS restart-cap gap (issue #607) is accepted, not patched, and the decision is made here rather than deferred to `M7-PACK-FE-143`.** Unlike `systemd`'s `StartLimitIntervalSec`/`StartLimitBurst` (verified against `man systemd.unit` on this session's systemd 258 — the rate-limit keys live in `[Unit]`, confirmed rather than assumed) and Windows' `RestartCount`, `launchd`'s `KeepAlive` has no configurable cap: a permanently failing LaunchAgent retries indefinitely, throttled only by launchd's own undocumented internal back-off, and never reaches a distinct "gave up" state the way the other two platforms do. Issue #607 offered three options; this decision takes **option 2** — accept the asymmetry, and let `state.json`'s heartbeat age (already read by both the health probe and the shell's adoption logic) be the *one* detection path every platform's future repair surface reads, rather than adding a macOS-only wrapper script that would have to duplicate the backoff-and-give-up logic `deploy/inference/askwell-inference` already implements for the model-loading case. The rejected alternative (a thin per-platform wrapper script that counts its own failures and self-removes past a cap) was rejected specifically because it multiplies moving parts for one platform to reach a state the shared file already encodes well enough to detect from: a heartbeat that has gone stale for several minutes *is* "gave up trying", whether or not the OS itself has a word for it.

**Windows verification gap (issue #606) is re-owned, unchanged.** Same root cause as the prior `M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-183` attempt and this session: no `pwsh` on this build host and no passwordless `sudo` to install one, so `Register-ScheduledTask`'s exact restart semantics (does a non-zero *action process* exit count as the task failure `RestartCount` reacts to, or only a failure Task Scheduler itself judges at the task level) were verified against documentation, not exercised. `-ExecutionTimeLimit ([TimeSpan]::Zero)` is included specifically because Task Scheduler's *undisputed*, well-documented default (a 72-hour execution limit that kills a still-running task) would otherwise silently cap a session-long service — that part is not in question; the restart-on-failure interaction is what stays unverified.

**Consequences:** all three platforms' `install.sh`/`install.ps1` now register three services total (shell, stack, inference) instead of one; all three `uninstall.sh`/`uninstall.ps1` stop and remove the two new ones before the existing `podman compose down` fallback, so uninstalling still leaves nothing orphaned. No `api/` or `web/` change was needed — this ticket is deploy-scripting only, matching its own stated API touchpoint ("health surface", read-only). Issues #606 and #607 both stay open, explicitly re-owned by this entry rather than closed on an unverified assumption; #607's disposition (option 2) is now the standing answer for any future macOS supervision work, including `M7-PACK-FE-143`'s repair surface.

**Refs:** `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142`; `deploy/linux/lib.sh`, `install.sh`, `uninstall.sh`; `deploy/macos/lib.sh`, `install.sh`, `uninstall.sh`; `deploy/windows/lib.ps1`, `install.ps1`, `uninstall.ps1`; `deploy/inference/askwell-inference`; `web/src-tauri/src/supervisor.rs`; issues #606, #607 (both re-owned, not closed).

---

## 2026-09-23 — `M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-183`: the shell reads `state.json` rather than a socket to decide adoption, and reports its two new causes by pushing JS into its own starting page rather than adding a Tauri command

**Decision:** `web/src-tauri/src/supervisor.rs` supervises the container stack (`podman compose up -d`/`down`, polling `{api}/health` for reachability) and the native inference process (spawning `<install-prefix>/askwell-inference`, the same file every platform's installer already places) while the shell is open, with a `RestartPolicy` mirroring `deploy/inference/askwell-inference`'s own five-step backoff (`1, 2, 5, 15, 30`s, then give up with the last reason retained). Two design choices carry the actual risk:

Adoption — "the stack is already running, the shell attaches" and "the shell was killed, the next launch adopts or cleans up" — is decided by reading `<run_dir>/state.json`'s `updated_at` heartbeat (fresh within 35s, mirroring `STALE_AFTER_SECONDS` in `api/src/askwell/inference/state.py`) rather than by connecting to the inference Unix socket. And the two new shell-level unavailability causes (`runtime_missing`, `stack_down`) are pushed into `starting.html` via `WebviewWindow::eval("window.askwellReportCause(...)")` rather than exposed through a new Tauri command.

**Why:** this ticket had a real, documented predecessor attempt — an earlier build-runner session wrote and audited a version of this file, which failed audit and was never merged, leaving four open issues naming it directly: #600 (adoption checked a Unix socket, which does not exist on Windows, so it never worked there), #601 (`RestartPolicy::reset()` fired on every poll where the process was merely still alive, rather than only once it had proven itself ready — the backoff-cap acceptance criterion was consequently unreachable in practice), #602 (a `spawn()` failure — the antivirus-quarantine scenario this ticket names explicitly — was logged and then silently dropped, never retried, never reported), and #581 (the "warn before stopping if ingestion is running" edge case needs a global ingestion-status aggregate that does not exist in the API yet, which is a backend change outside this ticket's own stated API touchpoints).

Reading `state.json` instead of the socket is what fixes #600 directly: it is the one file every platform's `askwell-inference` supervisor already writes and heartbeats, so "is a live supervisor already answering" is answered identically on Linux, macOS and Windows, with no platform-specific IPC branch to get wrong or leave unimplemented on two of three platforms. It doubles as the fix for #601: `RestartPolicy::reset()` is now called only when `state.json` reports `state == "ready"` with a fresh heartbeat — confirmed readiness — never merely because `Child::try_wait()` returned `None` on the next poll tick. #602 is fixed by routing a `spawn()` failure through the same `RestartPolicy::record_failure` a crash-after-starting takes, so it retries with backoff and eventually reaches the same capped, reason-retained state rather than a silent drop. #581 is not attempted here — it is out of this ticket's own stated scope (a backend aggregate query, not a shell change) and remains open, re-confirmed rather than silently dropped a second time.

Choosing `eval` over a Tauri command for the two starting-page causes keeps `capabilities/default.json` at its current five commands (`M7-TAURI-FE-182`'s own narrow C7 boundary) rather than adding a sixth, read-only one for a status line that `M7-PACK-FE-143`'s repair surface (explicitly out of scope here) will likely want to expose properly — with buttons — anyway. `starting.html` is the shell's own bundled asset, not retrieved content, so pushing JS into it from Rust crosses no trust boundary C7 cares about.

Install-root resolution (`compose.yaml`, `.env`, `askwell-inference`) reads the same `ASKWELL_INSTALL_PREFIX` environment variable and platform defaults `deploy/linux/lib.sh`, `deploy/macos/lib.sh` and `deploy/windows/lib.ps1` already use, rather than deriving a path from `current_exe()` — confirmed necessary by reading `deploy/macos/install.sh`: the built `.app`'s executable lives at `Contents/MacOS/askwell-shell`, a different directory entirely from `$INSTALL_PREFIX`, so an exe-relative guess would have been wrong on that one platform specifically.

**Consequences:** the shell now genuinely starts and stops both halves rather than assuming a user (or `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-142`'s not-yet-built session-start service) already had. Issues #600, #601 and #602 are closed by this change; #581 stays open, unchanged, and is not this ticket's to close. Shutdown always runs `podman compose down` regardless of who started the stack, satisfying "leaves nothing orphaned" for the container half unconditionally; the inference half only sends its own owned child process a SIGTERM (with a hard-kill fallback after 10s) — a purely *adopted* process (this shell never spawned it) is left running on quit, since the shell has no PID for it and killing a process it never started risks stopping one the user began deliberately from the command line. That asymmetry is accepted rather than fixed here: real orphan cleanup of an adopted-but-now-stale process is a smaller, separable piece of work, left to a follow-up rather than folded into a ticket already at its 4–6 hour estimate.

**Verification gap, disclosed rather than worked around:** this session's build could not run `cargo build`/`cargo check`/`cargo test` to completion — the host has the WebKitGTK/GTK3 *runtime* packages but none of the matching `-devel` packages (`gdk-3.0.pc`, `gdk-pixbuf-2.0.pc`, `cairo-gobject.pc` and others are all missing), and there is no `sudo` in this session to install them. This is the same gap `M7-TAURI-FE-182` hit and filed as issue #497, still open; this session added a comment there rather than re-filing. The nine unit tests covering `RestartPolicy`, `resolve_install_root_from`, `looks_like_runtime_down`, `read_env_file`, `inference_heartbeat_fresh` and `inference_confirmed_ready` were instead run in a throwaway scratch crate (a verbatim copy of the pure logic, no Tauri dependency, deleted after the run) — one real bug was caught and fixed this way (`looks_like_runtime_down`'s own test used a phrasing, "Is the podman machine running?", that did not actually contain the substring the detector checks for). That is real verification of the highest-risk logic, but it is not the same as the shell actually compiling, linking, starting a stack, and opening a window — that walkthrough still needs a host with the four `-devel` packages present.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` `M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-183`; `web/src-tauri/src/supervisor.rs`, `main.rs`, `build.rs`, `shell-assets/starting.html`, `Cargo.toml`; `deploy/inference/askwell-inference`; `api/src/askwell/inference/state.py`; `api/src/askwell/health.py`; issues #600, #601, #602 (closed by this change), #581 and #497 (still open).

## 2026-09-23 — The restore release gate is a documented walkthrough with a retained log, not a script, and its first real run failed on the citation check

**Decision:** `M7-BACKUP-TEST-159` ships `docs/restore-release-test.md` (the gate procedure and
checklist, run once per release before `docs/release-procedure.md` step 4) and
`docs/restore-test-log.md` (the append-only, retained pass/fail record the ticket's own
Acceptance Criteria asks for). `docs/release-procedure.md` gained a new step 3 wiring the gate
in as a hard blocker, renumbering what follows. The procedure was proven by actually running it
once against the real stack rather than only written and left unexercised — `docs/manual-tests/M7-BACKUP-TEST-159.md`
has the full account, and the run's own result is `fail`, recorded honestly in the log rather
than smoothed into a pass.

**Why it failed, and why that is the correct outcome to record.** The ticket's Acceptance
Criteria require citations to reproduce through a restore and be checked; this run's corpus
never produced a citation on either side of the restore, so the check cannot be reported as
passed. The cause is issue #220 (pre-existing, open): the local `Qwen3.5-4B` model's `<think>`
block is never stripped and, on this CPU-bound host's `generation_max_tokens` budget (1024),
regularly exhausts the whole budget before emitting a delimited answer — the turn is then
scored as abstained. This is a generation-quality defect, not a backup/restore one: `chunks`,
`documents`, `memory`, `sources` and the audit chain all restored correctly and were verified at
the data layer instead. Recording the run as `fail` rather than a qualified pass follows the
gate's own stated rule (`docs/restore-release-test.md` §4: "any unchecked box fails the run —
do not average partial success into a pass") — a rule written into the procedure specifically
so a future run under time pressure cannot talk itself into a pass it did not earn.

**A second, previously-unknown defect the run found live: issue #597.** `askwell.restore._existing_data_present`
checks the `settings` table along with everything else `backup.TABLES` covers, and
`askwell.session.secret` writes a `session_secret` row into `settings` the moment the wiped API
serves its first request — which on a real installer flow is the interface's own first load,
before a user does anything. A restore's "refuse to merge silently" check
(`docs/decisions.md`'s original restore design, and issue #574's neighbouring discussion of the
same refusal path) therefore fires on every genuinely clean machine in practice, not only on one
with real prior activity, and the gate's own procedure had to route around it with
`replace_existing: true` — the same flag meant to mean "I am knowingly overwriting real data."
Recorded as its own issue rather than folded into this one, since fixing it is `restore.py`
logic, not documentation, and outside `M7-BACKUP-TEST-159`'s own scope (a procedure and a
checklist, not a code change to the system under test).

**Issue #574 (a `replace_existing` restore reporting `chain_verified: false` against real prior
audit activity, with no UI explanation) stays open, re-owned rather than closed by this ticket**:
its own recommended home is a future restore settings-screen surface, which does not exist yet
— there is nothing to wire the explanation into. `docs/restore-release-test.md` §4.5 names the
expected-`false` case explicitly so a gate run does not mistake it for a defect in the meantime.

**Consequences:** `VERSION` → `0.7.11` (`CHANGELOG.md` same date). `docs/restore-test-log.md`
carries the `0.7.10` entry as `fail`; no real release should be represented as gate-passed until
either #220 is fixed or a working citation is produced through some other means and the gate is
re-run. Issues #220 (re-owned), #574 (re-owned), #597 (new), #598 (new, no second-platform
machine to run the gate cross-platform — the restore-specific counterpart to #590/#592's install
gap) all stay open. A small doc bug also fixed in the same change: `docs/manual-tests/M7-BACKUP-BE-158.md`'s
own commands used `scripts/dev.sh db psql`, which is not a valid invocation (`db` is
alembic-only) — corrected to `scripts/dev.sh psql` there and in the new procedure, meaning
`-158`'s manual test was never actually runnable verbatim as written.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` (`M7-BACKUP-TEST-159`),
`docs/restore-release-test.md`, `docs/restore-test-log.md`, `docs/manual-tests/M7-BACKUP-TEST-159.md`,
`docs/release-procedure.md`, issues #220, #574, #597, #598.

## 2026-09-23 — Release checksums and procedure ship; the ticket's own Acceptance Criteria described the wrong (signed) product and were corrected at the source

**Decision:** `M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-184` is implemented as `scripts/release-checksums.sh` (generates
`SHA256SUMS` for a release directory, deterministic, refuses an empty directory),
`docs/release-procedure.md` (assemble → checksum → publish → verify from outside the build →
cold-start walkthrough), and `docs/release-notes-template.md` (puts the `docs/installing.md`
link above the artefact list, matching that page's own ordering). No signing, notarisation, or
credential handling was added — none of it is this ticket's scope.

**Why the ticket text itself was edited, not just disregarded.** The backlog entry's own
Acceptance Criteria, Real-World Example Scenarios, Dependencies and Testing Notes described a
*signed, notarised* artefact — "signing credentials exist only as environment secrets",
"notarisation rejected for a nested binary" — directly contradicting the same ticket's Scope
("Code signing and Apple notarisation — deferred to `M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-184a`") and its own header
note ("Signing is deferred, not dropped"). That premise was already settled wrong once before:
the 2026-08-26 entry below chose unsigned distribution specifically because no certificates
exist (issue #42), and this same file's own 2026-09-23 macOS entry (above) had to flag the
identical stale text reappearing in `M7-PACK-DEPLOY-141`'s dependencies. Correcting the source
document rather than only working around it in code is the point of that earlier note — leaving
the wrong AC in place would guarantee a third session hits the same trap. `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md`'s AC/Scenarios/Dependencies/Testing Notes for `184` now
describe checksums and the release procedure, matching what Scope always said.

**Why checksum generation ships without a real packaging pipeline behind it.** Issue #559 (open)
tracks that no CI job or script produces an actual installable bundle per platform yet —
`tauri.conf.json` still has `bundle.active: false`, and `deploy/linux/install.sh`'s own header
says today's release artefact is a trimmed repository tarball, assembled by hand. Building that
pipeline is explicitly out of #559's own scope note ("inventing one is out of this ticket's
scope") and therefore out of this one too — `184`'s job is what happens to an artefact once it
exists (checksum it, publish it, verify it), not producing the artefact. `docs/release-procedure.md`
says this directly rather than implying a pipeline exists. The corollary: the ticket's own
"verification on each platform that a freshly downloaded artefact installs" cannot be run for
real yet, for the same reason `docs/manual-tests/M7-PACK-DEPLOY-140.md`/`141.md` couldn't
(issues #590, #592) — tracked there, not re-invented here or silently marked done.

**Consequences:** `VERSION` → `0.7.10` (`CHANGELOG.md` same date). Issues #559, #590, #592 stay
open and are the honest blockers on end-to-end verification; this ticket does not close any of
them, and #592 specifically is confirmed independent of `184` (it was never actually blocked on
it — the backlog text's own dependency wording was the same stale-signing artefact this entry
corrects). Once #559 lands, `docs/release-procedure.md` §2 gets a real command in place of "by
hand or by a future CI job."

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` (`M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-184`),
`scripts/release-checksums.sh`, `scripts/release-checksums.test.sh`, `docs/release-procedure.md`,
`docs/release-notes-template.md`, `docs/installing.md`, issues #42, #559, #590, #592.

## 2026-09-23 — The macOS installer creates and starts its own Podman machine, and names the Podman-machine mount window rather than enforcing it

**Decision:** `deploy/macos/install.sh`/`lib.sh`/`uninstall.sh`/`install.test.sh`
(`M7-PACK-DEPLOY-141`) mirror the Linux and Windows installers' shape — same artefact layout,
same refuse-before-copy discipline, same `.env` secrets fix as issue #584 — with macOS's own
two problems named. First, Podman has no native macOS container runtime; every container runs
inside a small VM ("the Podman machine"), the Mac analogue of Windows' WSL2. `check_runtime`
creates one (`podman machine init`) and starts it (`podman machine start`) if neither already
exists, detected via `podman machine list --noheading`'s table output rather than
`--format`'s Go-template field names — those names (`Running`, `Starting`, …) could not be
verified against a real running machine from this build host (no Mac; Podman's own machine
subcommand does exist on Linux too, and was actually exercised here, but `--format`'s exact
JSON schema for the installed version was not confirmed), and the visible table's literal
`Currently running` string is the honest choice over guessing one (`AGENTS.md` §4). This was
run for real against this build host's own Podman: `podman machine init && podman machine
start` correctly exercised `has_podman_machine`/`podman_machine_running`'s detection before
failing on a host-specific missing `gvproxy` binary unrelated to the installer, proving the
detection logic against real output rather than only a synthetic test double.

Second, that machine only sees paths under the installing account's home directory by default.
`ASKWELL_ROOTS_MOUNT` (`docs/decisions.md`, 2026-08-27, "Nominated folders are mounted at their
own paths") already accepted `not_mounted` as a recorded, explained state rather than a defect
— "no platform Askwell supports can add a mount to a running container." macOS adds a
platform-specific *cause* for that state: a folder outside `$HOME` needs the machine recreated
with an explicit `--volume` before it becomes reachable at all, which install time is not the
right moment to attempt (recreating a machine destroys and rebuilds it, and it may already be
running other work). `check_roots_mount` prints this once, informationally, rather than
enforcing or verifying it — a deliberately smaller claim than could have been made, because
verifying it precisely means the same unverified `podman machine inspect` JSON schema problem
`check_runtime` already declined to guess at.

**Why unsigned distribution is treated as settled, not raised again.** The ticket's own
Context/Background and Assumptions describe `M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-184` as delivering signed,
notarised artefacts to be "wired in" here, and name an unavailable signing certificate as a
blocking issue to raise. That premise is stale: the 2026-08-26 entry below already settled
this — Askwell ships **unsigned**, real signing survives only as the deferred
`M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-184a` (blocked on cost, not engineering), and `deploy/windows/lib.ps1` already
treats this as fact (`Get-AskwellQuarantineMessage`: "Askwell's ... unsigned desktop shell").
Re-raising a already-settled decision as if newly discovered would be its own mistake; instead
`install.sh` never checks or claims a signature, and `launch()` names the
`docs/installing.md` bypass path if `codesign` reports none, rather than asserting the ticket's
"runs without disabling security features" criterion is met when the accepted product decision
means it provably is not for a *downloaded* release (only for a same-machine local build, which
carries no Gatekeeper quarantine attribute).

**A second, smaller gap surfaced and was not absorbed into this ticket.** This ticket's own
"folder access permission requests ... explained" scope line overlaps `M7-TAURI-FE-182`'s
acceptance criterion, so it was checked against the tree rather than trusted. The
application-level explanation genuinely works — `folders.tsx` calls `isMacOS()` and renders
Askwell's line beside the native picker, `docs/ux/add-source.md` §7 has the matching copy, both
confirmed present in `M7-TAURI-FE-182`'s real merge (`f0ea93ae`). What is still missing:
`web/src-tauri/Info.plist` and `tauri.conf.json`'s `bundle.macOS.infoPlist` key, which would
carry `NSDesktopFolderUsageDescription`/etc. so macOS's own system dialog shows
Askwell-authored wording instead of generic OS text — a refinement to the system prompt's own
copy, not the "an explanation appears" behaviour, which already ships. Filed as issue #593
(corrected mid-session once the overstatement was caught) rather than authored here — that
remaining content is `M7-TAURI-FE-182`'s stated scope, not this installer script's, which
places whatever bundle exists and does not author its permission-prompt content.

**Rejected:** stripping the quarantine attribute (`xattr -d com.apple.quarantine`)
automatically after copying a bundle — this is exactly the bypass `docs/installing.md`
deliberately sequences *after* checksum verification, and scripting it unconditionally would
remove the one real safeguard that page names. Verifying `podman machine inspect`'s actual
current mounts programmatically — considered and rejected for this ticket, since the field
names could not be confirmed against a real instance and a wrong guess is worse than an
honest, informational message (same reasoning `check_runtime` already applied to `--format`).

**Consequences:** `deploy/macos/install.test.sh` (36/36, run on this Linux build host — every
function under test is pure string handling with no macOS-only syscall) proves `lib.sh`'s
logic; the real cold-start walkthrough remains unrun (issue #592, same shape as #590 for
Windows). `docs/manual-tests/M7-PACK-DEPLOY-141.md` records both what was verified for real
(the runtime-detection step, against this host's own Podman) and what was not.

**Refs:** `deploy/macos/`; `docs/manual-tests/M7-PACK-DEPLOY-141.md`; issues #559, #590, #592,
#593, #501; this file, 2026-08-26 ("No trademark, unsigned distribution, and Apache-2.0
stays") and 2026-08-27 ("Nominated folders are mounted at their own paths").

## 2026-09-23 — The Windows installer checks virtualisation before Podman, and verifies its own copies rather than trusting them

**Decision:** `deploy/windows/install.ps1`/`lib.ps1`/`uninstall.ps1`/`install.test.ps1`
(`M7-PACK-DEPLOY-140`) mirror `deploy/linux/install.sh`'s shape — same artefact layout, same
refuse-before-copy discipline, same `.env` secrets fix as issue #584 — with two things Linux's
installer never has to do. First, virtualisation is checked *before* Podman: Podman on Windows
only runs containers inside a WSL2 virtual machine, so "is the runtime installed" has to answer
"can a runtime exist on this machine at all" first. That check parses `systeminfo`'s own
"Hyper-V Requirements" text block rather than a `Get-ComputerInfo` CIM property — the block's
two wordings (`Virtualization Enabled In Firmware: Yes/No`, or `A hypervisor has been
detected...` once one is already running) are documented, stable `systeminfo` output across
Windows versions; the CIM property names this file would otherwise depend on (`HyperVisorPresent`,
`HyperVRequirementVirtualizationFirmwareEnabled`) could not be verified against an installed
Windows machine from this build host, and AGENTS.md's registry-verification rule (§4) reads the
same for an unverifiable API as for an unverifiable model name — don't assert it, use what can
be checked. Second, every file this installer copies is checked with `Test-Path` immediately
after the copy, not assumed to have landed: a plain `Copy-Item` returning without error and an
antivirus product lifting the file a moment later look identical from the installer's own
control flow, and the ticket's own acceptance criterion — a quarantined binary "produces a
message naming the file and the likely cause" — cannot be met by trusting the copy call. Disk
space, path-length (Windows' 260-character `MAX_PATH`) and admin-rights checks follow the same
refuse-before-copying discipline `install.sh` already established. Registered-root path
handling across the WSL2 boundary — a changed drive letter reporting a root unavailable, a path
past `MAX_PATH` failing per file — is **not** new code: `api/src/askwell/roots.py`'s `probe()`
and `extract_common`'s per-file `OSError` handling were written platform-agnostically in
`M1-ADD-ING-021` and already produce the right behaviour; this ticket exercises them rather
than duplicating them, and `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §3 now names the Windows-specific
triggers for both rows explicitly rather than leaving them implicit.

**Why:** the alternative for virtualisation detection was `Get-ComputerInfo`'s `HyperV*`
properties, which would have been shorter code but rested on property names recalled rather
than verified — exactly the failure mode AGENTS.md §4 names by its own history ("correct model
names ... replaced with older ones," issues #24/#25). `systeminfo` text parsing is more code
and one more subprocess call, but every word of it is checkable against Microsoft's own
long-stable output format, and the unit tests (`install.test.ps1`) assert against fixture text
rather than a live machine either way — so the extra robustness cost nothing at test time and
removed a real risk at install time. For the post-copy verification: the cheaper alternative
was to only check quarantine reactively, after a user reports a launch failure — rejected
because the ticket's acceptance criterion is explicitly about *this* installer producing *this*
message at *install* time, not about a later support conversation.

**Consequences:** `deploy/windows/lib.ps1`'s `Test-AskwellVirtualizationEnabled` takes a
`systeminfo` string rather than a live probe object, which is what makes it unit-testable with
fixture text on this Linux build host — the same reason `deploy/linux/lib.sh`'s
`parse_podman_version` takes a raw version string rather than shelling out itself. The real
end-to-end walkthrough (virtualisation-disabled refusal, quarantine message, WSL2-boundary
citation, drive-letter-changed unavailable state, all against a real Windows machine) is
unverified from this build host — issue #590, filed the same way #559 already tracks the
identical gap for Linux plus the still-missing `askwell-shell.exe` release pipeline that blocks
both platforms' installers from a full cold start. PowerShell 5.1 (Windows' default) versus
PowerShell 7 (what this suite ran under) is an open compatibility question, folded into #590
rather than filed separately since it needs the same Windows machine to resolve.

**Refs:** `deploy/windows/install.ps1`, `lib.ps1`, `uninstall.ps1`, `install.test.ps1`;
`docs/manual-tests/M7-PACK-DEPLOY-140.md`; `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §3; issue #584 (the
placeholder-password fix, extended to Windows here); issue #559 (the missing release pipeline,
unchanged, now also named as blocking Windows); issue #590 (this ticket's own walkthrough gap).

## 2026-09-23 — The Linux installer is a rebuild of a closed PR, carrying its fixes forward and generating real database passwords

**Decision:** `deploy/linux/install.sh`/`lib.sh`/`uninstall.sh`/`install.test.sh`
(`M7-PACK-DEPLOY-139`) are rebuilt from scratch off current `main`, carrying forward every fix
already made in the earlier, unmerged attempt (PR #560, closed 2026-09-22 for a stale merge
base rather than a code defect): the `have_admin_path` fix for issue #503 (real `sudo`, not
`sudo -n true`) and the `askwell-shell` filename fix. New in this pass: `lib.sh`'s
`generate_env_passwords`, called from `install.sh`'s `place_files` immediately after a *fresh*
`.env` is written (never against an existing one), replaces every `POSTGRES_*`/`SANDBOX_*`
placeholder with a random 256-bit value from `random_hex` (`openssl rand -hex`, falling back to
reading `/dev/urandom` directly if `openssl` is not on `PATH`). `SANDBOX_OWNER_PASSWORD`/
`ASKWELL_SANDBOX_OWNER_PASSWORD` and `SANDBOX_READONLY_PASSWORD`/
`ASKWELL_SANDBOX_READONLY_PASSWORD` are generated once each and written to both variable names,
since `.env.example` names the same credential twice (Compose builds the app-side value from
the Compose-side one) and a mismatch there would leave the sandbox roles Compose creates and
the passwords Askwell connects with out of sync.

**Why:** issue #584, filed against the closed attempt's own audit, found that `place_files`
copied `.env.example` to `.env` verbatim — the file's own first line says "then set the three
passwords", but the target user this ticket is for (someone who has never used container
tooling) will never open `.env` in an editor to do that, so every fresh install ran Postgres
and both sandbox roles on the same well-known, publicly-documented placeholder credentials.
Three options existed: (1) generate random values with no user interaction, (2) prompt
interactively for the passwords during install, (3) leave it and document the gap. (2) was
rejected as inconsistent with the ticket's own "no terminal, no systems task" framing — this
installer already never asks a question it can answer itself, e.g. the disk-space and
runtime-version checks. (3) was rejected because shipping a known placeholder credential by
default is a worse outcome than a slightly more complex `place_files`, and nothing about
generating a password needs review or user judgement the way, say, choosing a data directory
does. (1) shipped. The two paired variables were generated once and shared, not generated
independently, because they are not two credentials — `.env.example`'s own comments state
plainly that the `ASKWELL_SANDBOX_*` values are read by the application while the
`SANDBOX_OWNER_PASSWORD`/`SANDBOX_READONLY_PASSWORD` values are read by Compose to create the
matching Postgres roles; generating them independently would silently lock Askwell out of its
own sandbox on every fresh install.

**Consequences:** a fresh install's `.env` now carries six distinct, unguessable values (four
independent, two shared pairs) instead of six known placeholders; an upgrade over an existing
install still never touches `.env`, so this changes nothing about the "never touching its data"
acceptance criterion already in place. Verified end to end against this checkout with a
stand-in shell binary: a fresh install writes no `change-me` string anywhere in `.env`, the two
paired credentials match each other, all six stored values are pairwise distinct, and a second
`install.sh -y` run over the same prefix (an upgrade) leaves the already-generated
`POSTGRES_PASSWORD` unchanged. Issue #559 (no release pipeline produces the Tauri shell binary
or an installable bundle) is unaffected by this rebuild and stays open, re-owned rather than
fixed here — it is release-packaging work with its own shape, out of a 4–6 hour
installer-logic ticket's scope, exactly as the closed attempt's own decision entry (superseded
by this one; see git history for PR #560) already reasoned. Issue #584 is closed by this
change.

**Refs:** `deploy/linux/install.sh`, `deploy/linux/lib.sh`, `deploy/linux/uninstall.sh`,
`deploy/linux/install.test.sh`; `docs/manual-tests/M7-PACK-DEPLOY-139.md`; issue #503 (fixed,
carried forward); issue #584 (fixed here); issue #559 (re-owned, unaffected, still open); PR
#560 (closed, superseded by this rebuild).

**The *Why* should be longer than the *Decision*.** What was built is readable from the code. What was rejected, and the trade-off accepted, is not, and is exactly what gets lost. Name the rejected option.

Template:

```markdown
## YYYY-MM-DD — Title

**Decision:** one or two sentences.

**Why:** the reasoning. What alternatives were considered and why they lost. What trade-off is being accepted knowingly.

**Consequences:** what this now forces, forbids, or costs. What would have to change to reverse it.

**Refs:** PRD sections, issues, commits, files.
```

## 2026-09-22 — `M7-TAURI-FE-182`: the IPC layer scopes its own filesystem reads, and `native.ts` talks to `globalThis` rather than `@tauri-apps/api`

**Decision:** `web/src-tauri/src/main.rs` gains `AllowedRoots` — a `Mutex<HashSet<PathBuf>>` of every path a dialog (`pick_folder`/`pick_file`/`pick_files`) has actually returned this running session. `list_dir` and `read_head` both refuse (`"That path is outside every folder or file you chose."`) any path that does not canonicalise to one of those roots or a descendant of one, checked on the Rust side before any `std::fs` call runs — not left to the frontend to call these commands only with paths it happens to have. `web/lib/native.ts`'s wrappers call `globalThis.__TAURI_INTERNALS__.invoke` directly rather than importing `@tauri-apps/api/core`'s own `invoke`/`isTauri`: that package's `invoke()` reads `window` unconditionally, which does not exist under this repo's `node --test` runner (`AGENTS.md` §6, no jsdom), so every test would need a browser shim just to import the module under test. `globalThis` is the same object as `window` inside the real webview, so the production path is identical either way — only what a unit test has to fake differs.

**Why:** this ticket's own Validation Rule states "a path outside every registered root is still never read" as a guarantee, not an aspiration, and an earlier, discarded attempt at this same ticket shipped `list_dir`/`read_head` with no scoping at all — any path the webview happened to send was read, which a passing test suite could not catch because the happy path only ever calls these with dialog-derived paths (issue #580, filed against that attempt during its audit, which failed and whose code never reached `main`). Two designs existed to close it: check every incoming path against the *roots registry* (an API round-trip, and wrong regardless — a picked file for relocation is never itself a registered root, only somewhere under one eventually), or track what a dialog has *actually handed back this session* and check ancestry against that. The second is what the ticket's own edge cases describe ("a path that cannot be mounted... refused at registration," never "refused because the shell forgot it chose this") and needs no round trip: the shell already has the path the instant the dialog returns it. For `native.ts`, the alternative (`@tauri-apps/api`) is the documented, "prefer what exists" choice (`AGENTS.md` §4) and was tried first — it is already present in this project's pnpm store from the discarded prior attempt. It was dropped once `node --test`ing `pickFolder()` against it threw `ReferenceError: window is not defined` before any mock could intercept the call: `core.js`'s `invoke()` hardcodes `window.__TAURI_INTERNALS__.invoke(...)`, and Node's test runner (per this repo's own convention, `add-source.test.ts`'s comment: "Node 22 runs TypeScript directly, so this needs no bundler, no jsdom") has no `window` at all. Reaching for `mock.module()` (Node 22's `--experimental-test-module-mocks`) would have worked but means every future contributor testing anything that touches Tauri IPC needs to know that flag exists; targeting `globalThis` directly needs nothing beyond what every other test file in `web/lib/` already does — set a global, call the function, delete the global in `afterEach`.

A related, separate finding from the same audit (issue #499, also against the discarded attempt) held: `path_string()` refuses (rather than `to_string_lossy()`-mangles) a path that is not valid UTF-8, at the point a dialog returns it, rather than letting a corrupted string fail confusingly later at `list_dir`/`read_head`. `pick_folder`/`pick_file`/`pick_files` therefore return `Result<_, String>` rather than a bare `Option`, and `list_dir_impl` skips (rather than mangles) any directory entry whose name cannot be represented, in favour of an honestly incomplete listing over a silently wrong one.

**Consequences:** `@tauri-apps/api` is not a dependency of `web/package.json` — a future ticket that wants one of its other modules (the dialog/fs plugins, `@tauri-apps/api/event`, etc.) starts from zero, not from this ticket's choice, and should re-evaluate rather than assume the precedent extends. `AllowedRoots` is per-process, in-memory, and reset on every relaunch — by design, since the whole point is "what has this session's dialogs actually returned," not a persistent grant; a future command that needs to read a path across a relaunch (none does today) cannot rely on it and would need its own mechanism. `list_dir`/`read_head`'s Rust logic is tested (`native_command_tests` in `main.rs`) but **not run** in the session that wrote it — no system webview libraries and no `sudo` to install them, the same gap issue #497 already named; confidence instead comes from `rustc --edition 2021 --emit=metadata`, which parses the whole file with zero syntax errors and only the expected unresolved-crate errors. `capabilities/default.json`'s `remote.urls` grants `http://127.0.0.1:*` only, dropping the `http://localhost:*` entry #580 also flagged as unused (`api_origin()` never constructs that hostname) — free tightening, done in this same change rather than deferred.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog` ticket `M7-TAURI-FE-182`; issues #580 (the scoping gap this fixes), #579 (the fabricated test-coverage claim this ticket's own real `native.test.ts`/`selection.test.ts` resolve), #499 (the non-UTF-8 path-refusal design), #497 (the unbuildable-session gap, still open), #498 (Windows/macOS path translation, still unverified — untouched by this decision); `web/src-tauri/src/main.rs` (`AllowedRoots`, `allow_root`, `is_within_allowed`, `path_string`, `native_command_tests`); `web/lib/native.ts`; `web/src-tauri/capabilities/default.json`; `docs/ux/add-source.md` §7.

## 2026-09-22 — `M7-PERF-TEST-167`: every budget timing is this harness's own wall clock; the per-stage breakdown is the one exception

**Decision:** `eval/answer_latency.py` times `first_step`/`first_token`/`full_answer` off its own `time.monotonic()` reads as each named SSE event arrives from `POST /ask` — not the server's own stage timings, the opposite choice from `eval/voice_latency.py`, which reads `stage_breakdown_ms` exclusively and never times anything itself. The per-stage retrieve/compose breakdown *is* read from the server (`GET /ask/{message_id}/trace`), and `message_id` is read off the `done` event's own payload rather than queried from the database by "whichever row committed most recently" (an earlier draft this session did exactly that, against `messages.created_at`, before message_id turned out to be present on every event already). A `done` event is not treated as success — `status` is read out of its payload and anything other than `"completed"` is a measured failure, with `reason` carried as the error text.

**Why:** `docs/ux/ask.md` §7 and this ticket's own acceptance criteria phrase every one of the four budgets in terms of what happens "of submit" or "within Ns" — wall-clock, from a person's perspective, not a component's internal duration. Voice's own budget is phrased "end of speech → first audio" and voice deliberately avoids folding this harness's own network/scheduling jitter into a number meant to describe the voice path; the answer path's budget is phrased the other way round, and copying voice's choice here would time something the product never promised. The per-stage breakdown is the genuine exception: attributing *where* time went needs the server's own stage boundaries (`askwell.ask`'s `retrieve`/`compose` trace steps), which no amount of client-side SSE timestamping can recover — a harness watching from outside cannot see inside a single event's own processing. Building the `done`-is-not-success check and the crash-first-on-total-failure ordering from this script's first commit, rather than retrofitting them, is a direct response to issue #572, filed against a since-discarded uncommitted draft of this same harness that shipped both bugs; this version was run for real against a genuine `status: "failed"` turn (a composed prompt exceeding the native inference process's 8192-token context window on this machine's accumulated corpus) and correctly reported it as a failure with the real reason rather than a silent success with an empty error.

**Consequences:** a clean, fully-measured run of this ticket's own four budgets on a realistic corpus was **not achieved this session** — this specific shared dev machine's accumulated state (a locked content-encryption passphrase from an earlier, unrelated session; an already-ingested 19-source corpus large enough to overflow the configured context window) blocked both a fresh ingestion and a query against existing content. A still-valid prior measurement exists on this same machine and profile from before it accumulated that state (`eval/results/answer-latency-standard-20260921T101607Z.json`, 2026-09-21, from a since-discarded harness version — real numbers, different schema) — cited in `docs/BRAIN.md` rather than reproduced as this session's own, since it was not generated by the code now in the repository. Issue #576 covers both blockers and recommends a disposable environment for future perf runs, so unrelated tickets' manual testing state never contaminates a latency measurement again.

**Refs:** `AGENTS.md` §3 (none), `docs/ux/ask.md` §7, issue #491 (harness never run), #572 (the two bugs fixed here), #576 (this session's environment blockers), `eval/answer_latency.py`, `eval/voice_latency.py` (the contrasting choice).

## 2026-09-22 — `M7-BACKUP-BE-158`: restore cannot run its own migrations, cannot clear the audit tables, and must not write to them before the restored chain does

**Decision:** `askwell.restore` refuses to run against a database not at Alembic head (`_check_schema_current`, naming `scripts/dev.sh db upgrade head`) rather than calling `alembic upgrade head` itself. `replace_existing` clears every table it restores with `DELETE`, never `TRUNCATE`, and never touches `audit_decisions`/`audit_interactions` at all — no refusal check on their existing rows, no clear, ever. `enqueue` writes no decisions record; only `run_job`, at the very end, after the restored chain is the only thing in those two tables besides it. A passphrase-protected backup now carries `install_secret_wrapped` (`askwell.backup`, manifest field, `backup_jobs` column) — the install secret itself, encrypted with a key derived from the passphrase alone (`crypto.derive_key(b"", passphrase)`), so restore can write the *source* machine's install secret back and the already-encrypted `chunks.content`/`sources.config_encrypted` decrypt exactly as they did there.

**Why:** all four are the same discovery, made while actually building the ticket rather than assumed from its Acceptance Criteria text. `askwell_app` — what the API and worker processes connect as (`compose.yaml`) — owns nothing and holds `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE` on ordinary tables and only `SELECT, INSERT` on the audit tables (`2026-08-27 — Askwell does not own its own tables`, below); it has no `TRUNCATE` grant on anything and no DDL privilege at all. Running `alembic upgrade head` from inside `run_job`, as originally planned and as issue #570 assumed was merely the more expensive of two options, is not just expensive — it is a command this process cannot authenticate to perform. Clearing the audit tables on `replace_existing` is the same shape of impossible, not a missing feature: no credential this process holds can ever empty them, and that is C6 working, not a gap. Writing `enqueue`'s own decisions record immediately, matching `askwell.backup.enqueue`'s pattern exactly, was tried first and broke the ticket's own golden-path test (`docs/backlog` "Run log verification and confirm the chain is intact"): a target machine's `audit_decisions` is never actually empty by the time table-restore runs, because enqueueing itself just wrote a row with `prev_hash IS NULL` — a second root next to the historical chain's own first row, which `askwell.audit.verify` correctly reports as broken. Deferring the one decisions record to the end, after the historical rows, makes a clean machine's post-restore chain a single line again. The install-secret-wrapping mechanism exists because without it, a passphrase-protected backup is permanently unrestorable on any machine other than the one it came from — the passphrase alone was never sufficient, since the key content is encrypted under also depends on the 32-byte install secret, which lives outside the database by design (C8) and is never included in the dump.

**Consequences:** an operator restoring onto a database that has never been migrated gets a clear refusal naming the exact command, not a partial restore or an opaque permission error three tables in — this is the resolution to issue #570; the ticket's own Acceptance Criteria wording ("applies migrations if the version differs") should be read as "requires them applied," not "applies them." Restoring onto a machine with genuine prior local audit activity produces an honestly-reported broken chain (`chain_verified: false`) rather than a silently wrong one — two independently-hash-chained histories were concatenated, and C6 exists to make that detectable. `askwell.backup.enqueue` gained a required-when-protected `backup_passphrase` parameter — a backward-incompatible change to a ticket that shipped one session earlier, made because a passphrase-protected backup with no wrapped secret was an artefact `askwell.restore` could never have used regardless of when the gap was found.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` `M7-BACKUP-BE-158`; `docs/ux/settings.md` §9 item 2; issues #570, #571, #569, #518; `api/src/askwell/restore.py`, `api/src/askwell/backup.py`, `api/src/askwell/db/models.py`; migration `20260922_e8b3f61a92d4_restore_jobs.py`; `api/tests/test_restore.py`. The `2026-08-27 — Askwell does not own its own tables` entry below (C6's grants, named exactly).

## 2026-09-22 — `M7-LOG-FE-156`: the log verifier is a synchronous read, not a job table

**Decision:** `GET /log-verify` (`askwell.log_verify`) calls `askwell.audit.verify` for both stores in one request and answers once. No `verify_jobs` table, no `arq` dispatch, no polling — unlike export, backup and prune, which all follow the same job-table-plus-`arq` shape because each of them writes something (a file, a set of deleted rows) that must survive a crash and be resumable. Verification writes nothing until the walk is already done, and what it writes then (the `log_verification_run` decisions record) is a report, not partial progress — there is no state a crash mid-walk would leave that is worth resuming rather than simply re-running. `askwell.audit.VerificationResult` gained `broken_at`, the broken record's own `occurred_at`, because "which record" without "which date" is half of what the ticket's own acceptance criteria ask the report to name.

**Why:** the alternative, matching the shape every other "large, progress-shown, interruptible" operation in this codebase uses (`askwell.log_export`, `askwell.log_prune`, `askwell.backup`), was a `verify_jobs` table with a worker-side runner reporting incremental progress and a cancel flag the frontend polls. Rejected for scope: this ticket's own Granularity note calls it "one action and two reports," and `askwell.audit.verify`'s chain walk already loads the whole result set into memory before it can determine anything about the chain's shape at all (it has to — a record's position in the chain is only knowable by following `prev_hash` links, not by any column a keyset query could page on), so a job table would not actually buy incremental progress without first rewriting `verify` itself to checkpoint mid-walk — a change to `askwell.audit` this ticket's own dependencies (`M7-LOG-BE-155`, `M0-DATA-OBS-015`) do not ask for and that touches the one module every other audit ticket also depends on. "Interruptible" is honoured instead as `AbortController` on the client: a read that has committed nothing has nothing to undo by stopping early, so aborting the wait *is* the honest meaning of "interruptible" for this specific operation, not a lesser version of it. "Progress shown" is an elapsed-time counter rather than a fabricated percentage — the walk has no natural midpoint to report against, and a progress bar implying granular knowledge the endpoint does not have would be a smaller version of the same dishonesty C5 exists to forbid elsewhere.

**Consequences:** a verification against a genuinely enormous log (years of interactions, never observed in practice — this is a single-user local product) blocks the request for as long as the walk takes, with no way to report finer-grained progress than "still checking" without first reworking `askwell.audit.verify`'s own walk to checkpoint. If that ever becomes a real complaint rather than a hypothetical one, the fix is to give `verify` an optional progress callback invoked periodically during its chain walk and build the job table around that — not to fake progress against the synchronous version. Reversing this decision means adding the job table; nothing here blocks that later.

**Refs:** `docs/ux/settings.md` §6/§8, `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §6, `api/src/askwell/log_verify.py`, `web/components/settings/verify-log.tsx`, issue #565 (a real broken chain found on this machine's own dev stack while verifying this ticket, unrelated to and not caused by it).

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M7-LOG-BE-154`: a prune records its own boundary rather than reusing `M7-LOG-BE-155`'s windowed-export mechanism, and `askwell.audit.verify` is taught to recognise it

**Decision:** `askwell.log_prune` deletes the oldest run of `audit_interactions` rows (older than `askwell.log_budget`'s retention window) inside the same transaction that writes an `interactions_pruned` decisions record naming the range and, critically, `boundary_hash` — the hash of the last record deleted, which is also the `prev_hash` every surviving record still chains to. `askwell.audit.verify` gained an optional `prune_boundaries` argument: when a chain does not start at `GENESIS`, it now checks the actual start against every recorded prune boundary before concluding `MISSING_GENESIS`, and reports the chain intact with an explanatory note if one matches. `askwell-verify` (`audit.main`) reads these boundaries from the decisions store itself, so the command needs no new flag to use them. Refusals: `PruneNotExported` unless a completed, unfiltered (`since IS NULL`) log export already covers the window, and `PruneRequiresConfirmation` if the window would remove ≥90% of what exists, mirroring `askwell.log_export.ExportNotAcknowledged`'s "state it once, then allow it" shape. Resolves issue #516, filed against this exact gap while `M7-LOG-BE-155` was being built.

**Why:** the alternative — recorded in #516's own options list — was re-anchoring genesis at prune time with a new marker record the pruned chain then verifies from instead of `GENESIS`. Rejected: a marker record is, by itself, indistinguishable from a forged one without more machinery (anyone could insert a record claiming to be a "chain restarts here" marker), whereas checking the *actual* surviving chain's start against a hash Askwell computed and stored the moment it deleted the predecessor is a fact nobody but this code path could have produced correctly — forging it would require also forging the SHA-256 preimage of a real, otherwise-deleted record. This keeps `verify`'s existing four-value `Break` taxonomy untouched (`ALTERED`/`UNLINKED`/`FORKED`/`MISSING_GENESIS`) rather than adding a fifth case that only ever fires for one store. A second question was where "nothing exported yet" should be checked: against the exact range about to be pruned (matching `since`/`until` to the interaction data's own bounds) or against a simpler "any completed, unfiltered export reaching at least the cutoff" test. Took the simpler one — `docs/audit-log.md`'s own Validation Rule is "export before prune, always," not "export precisely what you are about to prune" — because a windowed export proves nothing about records outside its own window and chaining that logic into `_has_covering_export` would let a narrow, unrelated export silently satisfy a prune of a totally different range.

**Consequences:** `askwell.audit.verify`'s signature is not backward-incompatible (the new argument defaults to `()`), but any future caller of `verify` against `Store.INTERACTIONS` that does not pass `prune_boundaries` will see `MISSING_GENESIS` after a real prune — `main()`/`askwell-verify` is updated here; `M7-LOG-FE-156`'s own settings-screen verifier (not yet built) will need to do the same, and is flagged in #516 rather than silently assumed. A prune's `cutoff` is frozen onto `prune_jobs` at enqueue time rather than recomputed on retry, so a retention-window change made between enqueue and a crash-retry does not change what an in-flight prune removes — consistent with `askwell.log_export`'s own snapshot-at-start reasoning for `until`.

**Refs:** `M7-LOG-BE-154`, `M7-LOG-BE-155`, issue #516, `docs/audit-log.md` §4/§8, `api/src/askwell/log_prune.py`, `api/src/askwell/audit.py` (`verify`, `prune_boundaries`), `api/tests/test_log_prune.py`, `api/tests/test_audit_chain.py`.
