## 2026-09-22 — `M7-SET-BE-145a`: shipped-vs-user-supplied is written by `askwell.model_select`, never inferred from a loaded filename

**Decision:** `messages.model_identity`'s `source` field (`"shipped"` / `"user_supplied"` / `"none"` / `"unknown"`) is read back purely from a `settings` key, `model.active_source`, that only `askwell.model_select` ever writes — at the moment a swap actually succeeds or fails, both on a live selection (`select_user_model`) and on the startup reapply of a persisted one (`reapply_user_model`). `active_model_identity` never compares the inference supervisor's reported `state.model` filename against the configured override path to decide the category; it only ever reads `model.active_source` and, when that says `user_supplied`, reports the display name from `model.user_model_path` alongside it. The ticket's own Validation Rule says this outright ("never guessed from the file name"), but a filename comparison would have been the cheaper implementation, so the rejection is recorded here rather than only in a docstring.

**Why:** a filename check (`Path(user_model_path).name == state.model`) is simpler — no second setting key, no need to update it on every swap outcome — and was the first design considered, because it needs nothing new: the supervisor already reports the loaded file's name in `state.json`. It fails in exactly the case that matters most: a user selects a model, the API restarts, `reapply_user_model` tries to reload it and fails (file moved, disk full, out of memory) and the supervisor falls back to keep serving whatever was already loaded — the shipped default from cold boot. At that point `model.user_model_path` is still set (the user's intent has not changed) but the *loaded* model is shipped. A filename comparison has nothing to compare against that tells it this happened; only an explicit fact updated at the moment of the actual outcome does. `model.active_source` is exactly that fact, written in the same transaction as the audit decision (`model_reapply_failed` sets it back to `shipped`), so a message stamped afterward is never wrong about which model actually answered it — which is the entire point of C4/the ticket's own acceptance criterion, not a nice-to-have.

**Consequences:** every code path that changes what the generation role is actually running (`select_user_model`, `reapply_user_model` — currently the only two) must update `model.active_source` itself; a third future path that swaps the model without going through one of these two would silently desynchronise the setting from reality, and there is no test that would catch it except one written against that new path directly. `active_model_identity` therefore has exactly two truthful inputs it trusts — `state.usable` (is anything loaded at all) and `model.active_source` (what kind it is) — and adding a third state (a "downloading" or "verifying" model, say) means extending both, not just one.

**Refs:** `api/src/askwell/model_select.py` (`active_model_identity`, `select_user_model`, `reapply_user_model`, `SETTING_ACTIVE_SOURCE`); `api/src/askwell/db/models.py` (`Message.model_identity`); migration `20260922_a1c2e5f6b3d4_messages_model_identity.py`; `deploy/inference/askwell-inference` (`Supervisor.swap_model`); `docs/audit-log.md` §2 (settings changes are a decisions record).

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-WEB-OBS-193`: "fetched" in the trace means what the provider returned, not a separately fetched page

**Decision:** `web_search_trace_steps` (`api/src/askwell/websearch.py`) treats every `WebSearchResult` the provider actually returned as the thing to name and flag in the trace — a `web_search_fetch` step per kept result (URL, source, title, retrieval date, and `flag_injection_text` run over its `passage`, the identical heuristic a document candidate or tool result already goes through), and a new `WebSearchDrop`/`WebSearchOutcome.dropped` for every result the provider returned but `escalate_web_search` never kept (today, always `"no usable passage"`). It does **not** call `askwell.webfetch.fetch_pages`, and no `web_search_drop` step ever names one of that module's own byte/count/timeout caps. `_record_escalation_trace` merges these steps onto the turn's existing `messages.trace`, after its own `retrieve`/`abstain` step, using the same two-write shape `askwell.ask._run_generation` already uses (`TraceRing.write`, fail-open, then `messages.trace` inside the caller's transaction) — imported with a deferred `from askwell.ask import ...` inside the function, because `askwell.ask` already transitively imports `askwell.websearch` (via `askwell.agent.conflict` → `askwell.agent.compose` → `WebSearchResult`), so a module-level import the other way is circular, confirmed by actually triggering `ImportError: cannot import name 'WebSearchResult' from partially initialized module` before adding the deferred import.

**Why:** the ticket's own text ("which pages were fetched," "a page dropped by a cap") reads as if `fetch_pages` is already wired into the live escalation path. It is not — the 2026-09-22 `M6.5-WEB-BE-195` entry below (chronologically earlier in this file, written the same day) already decided that the live path composes from `ddgs`'s own search snippets alone, because fetching real result pages needs a destination-list egress grant that does not exist yet (issue #551). Two ways to close that gap existed: (a) treat this ticket as blocked on #551 and stop, per `AGENTS.md`'s "say so and stop" instruction for a dependency that has not been built; or (b) read "fetched" as "what the provider returned," since a `WebSearchResult` already carries every field the trace's own Acceptance Criteria ask for (URL, retrieval date, a passage to flag) and the ticket's Dependencies list `M6.5-WEB-BE-188` (built) rather than the still-unwired call site. (b) was taken: stopping outright would leave the trace with no escalation detail at all for the whole current system, contradicting the ticket's own stated purpose ("the one opaque moment in this product is not opaque") for every escalation a real user can run today, over a documentation mismatch between when this ticket was written and when `188`'s wiring was later deferred. The drop reason is honest about the gap rather than papering over it: `WebSearchDrop`'s own docstring states plainly that a cap-driven drop is not reachable until `#551` lands, so a future reader who only ever sees `"no usable passage"` knows why, instead of assuming the caps are broken.

**Consequences:** once `#551`'s egress redesign lands and a caller starts fetching real pages behind result URLs, `escalate_web_search`'s drop-building code and `WebSearchDrop.reason`'s docstring both need a second pass — today's single reason string is not designed to also carry `webfetch`'s byte/count/timeout/non-text reasons, only extended enough to be honest about not doing so yet. `web_search_trace_steps` and `_record_escalation_trace` have no test coverage that stands in for that future path; `api/tests/test_websearch.py`/`test_websearch_api.py`'s new tests all exercise the fixture-returned-snippet shape, which is what production actually does today. The circular-import constraint (`askwell.ask` importing this module transitively) is now load-bearing for any future function in `websearch.py` that wants an `askwell.ask` helper — the deferred-import pattern here is the second instance of it in this file (`compose_and_generate_web_answer`'s own deferred `askwell.agent.compose` import is the first), so a third case should reach for the same pattern rather than rediscovering the failure.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M6.5-it-can-look-outside.md` ticket `M6.5-WEB-OBS-193`; issue #549 (declined-offer edge case, resolved as working-as-intended, backlog doc corrected in the same change); issue #551 (the deferred egress redesign this decision leans on); `api/src/askwell/websearch.py` (`web_search_trace_steps`, `WebSearchDrop`, `_record_escalation_trace`); `api/src/askwell/ask.py` (`_bound_trace_steps`, `_trim_rotated_traces`, `TraceRing`); the 2026-09-22 `M6.5-WEB-BE-195` entry below.

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-WEB-BE-195`: `ddgs` pinned to one backend; page-fetching deferred, not inherited from the 2026-08-26 `188` entry

**Decision:** `DDGSWebSearchProvider` (`api/src/askwell/websearch.py`) implements `WebSearchProvider` by calling `ddgs.DDGS.text(question, backend="duckduckgo")` — the single `duckduckgo` backend, never `ddgs`'s `"auto"` — via `asyncio.to_thread` (`ddgs` is synchronous), and maps its `title`/`href`/`body` fields straight into `WebSearchResult.title`/`url`/`passage`. It does **not** call `askwell.webfetch.fetch_pages` on the result URLs. The proxy is passed to `ddgs.DDGS(proxy=...)` explicitly, built from `Settings.egress_proxy_host`/`_port`, rather than left to rely on `ddgs`/`primp` reading `HTTP_PROXY`/`HTTPS_PROXY` — `ddgs` only ever consults its own `DDGS_PROXY` env var or constructor argument, confirmed by reading `ddgs` 9.16.0's own source rather than assumed (`AGENTS.md` §4).

**Why:** the 2026-08-26 entry for `M6.5-WEB-BE-188` (above) stated as a **Consequence** that "`M6.5-WEB-BE-195` must call `fetch_pages` on the URLs behind whatever `ddgs` returns, open its egress grant around exactly that fetch (not just the search call)." That plan does not survive contact with either `ddgs`'s actual shape or this ticket's own, later-written acceptance criteria. Two things changed it: first, `ddgs`'s `"auto"` mode fans a search across several backend engines — DuckDuckGo, Bing, Brave, Yandex, Wikipedia, and others — each its own host, which the egress grant (`M6.5-WEB-SEC-187`, `askwell.egress.open_grant`) cannot express; it opens exactly one `destination` string per turn and `EgressProxy._grant_permits` checks for exact equality. Fetching the *result* URLs is the same problem one layer further out: those are arbitrary third-party hosts chosen by the search results themselves, unbounded and unknowable in advance, not a fixed small set a grant could name. Widening the grant mechanism to a destination *list* is a real change to C1's sole enforcement point and belongs in its own security-labelled ticket, not folded into a `backend` ticket sized at 3–4 hours. Second, `M6.5-WEB-BE-195`'s own ticket text — written after the `188` entry — scopes this ticket to "the provider... question in, results with domain, title, URL, passage and retrieval time out" and states the acceptance criterion as "no change to the answer path" and "**the** destination is authorised per question" (singular), which only holds if the provider itself already returns a usable passage, matching what `ddgs.text()` actually returns (a search snippet in `body`) without an extra fetch. Pinning to `duckduckgo` rather than `auto` was the fix that made the single-destination design still correct: that backend's `search_url` is `https://html.duckduckgo.com/html/`, which is exactly `Settings.web_search_destination_host`'s existing value — confirmed by reading `ddgs/engines/duckduckgo.py`, not assumed.

**Consequences:** `docs/decisions.md`'s 2026-08-26 `188` entry is superseded on this one point — fetching real page content behind a web result stays possible later, but needs its own ticket that either (a) redesigns `askwell.egress`'s grant to a small named destination list scoped per turn, or (b) routes result-page fetches through a different mechanism entirely (e.g. a dedicated single-purpose fetch proxy). Filed as issue (see `AGENTS.md` §8) rather than silently dropped. Until then, an escalated answer is composed from `ddgs` snippets alone — shorter and less complete than a fetched page would give, a real quality cost, but the honest one available without an unreviewed C1 change. `askwell.webfetch.fetch_pages` (`M6.5-WEB-BE-188`) remains built, tested, and uncalled outside its own tests, exactly as its own docstring already states — this decision does not change that module, only who calls it and when.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M6.5-it-can-look-outside.md` ticket `M6.5-WEB-BE-195`; the 2026-08-26 `188` entry above; `api/src/askwell/websearch.py` (`DDGSWebSearchProvider`); `api/src/askwell/egress.py` (`open_grant`, `_grant_permits`); `api/src/askwell/webfetch.py`.

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-EVAL-TEST-194`: web escalation discipline is measured on the egress proxy's counter, not on the answer text

**Decision:** `eval/web_escalation.py` adds the quality gate's eighth category, `mode: "web_escalation"`, 10 tasks (`eval/suites/web_escalation.v1.json`), pass bar 1.00, wired into `eval/bench.py` and `.github/workflows/eval.yml`'s suite loop. Every task drives one or more real `askwell.ask` turns against the fixture corpus, the same way `eval.abstain` does, but the run's score is decided by `askwell.network.read_activity`'s `permitted` counter (`askwell.egress`'s honest, proxy-side count) taken before and after — a nonzero delta fails the run outright, before the turn's own text is even considered. `Task` gained a `web_escalation_setup` field (`plain`, `repeat`, `after_accept`, `after_stop`, `retrieval_error`) naming which of the ticket's enumerated fallback shapes a task exercises; `after_accept` is the only scenario that calls `askwell.websearch.escalate_web_search` at all, and it does so explicitly, scoped to a *prior* turn, to set up the state the *following*, automatic turn is tested against — never as the thing under test itself.

**Why:** a text-only check ("does the answer look like an abstention") cannot tell "never reached the web" apart from "reached a fixture that happened not to touch a socket" — `FixtureWebSearchProvider` (the only provider this environment can run without breaking C1) never makes a real connection either way, so the only artifact that can actually distinguish an automatic escalation from none at all is the proxy's own counter, which is exactly what `askwell.network`'s own module docstring says it exists to be ("the application saying 'I did not make any outbound requests' is exactly the assertion the proxy exists to replace with evidence"). Five scenarios, not one, because the ticket's own edge cases name specific ways a fallback creeps in that a single near-miss prompt cannot exercise: `repeat` and `after_accept` need a real second turn in the *same* conversation (`eval.grounded._ask_one` always opens a fresh one, so this module adds its own `_ask_turn` against an existing `conversation_id`); `after_stop` needed a prior turn on the record as `stopped`, written directly rather than by racing a real cancellation into `_generate`, since `askwell.ask`'s own tests already cover that race and reproducing it here would test the stop mechanism, not escalation discipline; `retrieval_error` monkeypatches `askwell.ask.retrieve` for one call, because the module docstring's own claim — "nothing in `askwell.ask` imports this module" — already makes an automatic escalation on a broken retrieval path structurally impossible, so what is actually worth measuring is that the failure surfaces as a failed turn rather than silently swallowed into some other answer. Registration in the release checklist (`M7-QA-TEST-168`) is this ticket's own dependency, but that ticket is Phase 7 work and does not exist yet — nothing to register into — so it is left for whoever builds that checklist to find this category in `docs/build-plan.md`'s quality gate table, which already lists it.

**Consequences:** this suite needs the egress proxy's Redis-backed counters reachable, which is why it runs on `scripts/dev.sh eval`'s stack-joined network rather than `--network=none` (the same "local machine talking to itself" exception `db`/`psql`/`test-db` already use, not a new hole in C1). If the counters are ever unreadable mid-run, the suite raises `HarnessError` rather than reporting a score — `askwell.network`'s own rule, "unreadable is never reported as zero," extended to this harness rather than silently treated as a pass. A future real web search provider (`M6.5-WEB-BE-195`) changes nothing here: this suite never builds one, and `FixtureWebSearchProvider`'s own docstring already states it must never be handed anything that could reach the network, so the suite's own honesty does not depend on which provider configuration selects.

**Refs:** `docs/build-plan.md`'s quality gate table, `docs/backlog/M6.5-it-can-look-outside.md` ticket `M6.5-EVAL-TEST-194` — `eval/web_escalation.py`, `eval/suite.py`, `eval/suites/web_escalation.v1.json`, `eval/bench.py`, `.github/workflows/eval.yml`, `eval/tests/test_web_escalation.py`.

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-WEB-FE-191`: the escalation's answer is a second, narrower generation call, not a re-run of the turn's own

**Decision:** `POST /ask/{message_id}/escalate/web` now generates the escalation's own answer once `escalate_web_search` returns usable results — `askwell.websearch.compose_and_generate_web_answer`, a new function, not a change to `askwell.ask._run_generation`. It calls `askwell.agent.compose.compose_web_answer` (new), which reuses the existing `answer_composition.v1.md` system prompt and its `<web-content>` section (already written for this by `M6.5-WEB-BE-188`, never previously called) with **no `<retrieved-content>` block at all** — the turn's own document candidates are not recombined. Claim ordinals inside `compose_and_generate_web_answer` are local to that call (1-based, numbered by `askwell.agent.claims.segment_claims` same as every other generation); the endpoint offsets them by `len(segment_claims(<the turn's current stored content>))` before writing `web_citations` and returning them to the browser, and appends the generated text to `messages.content` (`UPDATE ... SET content = content || :suffix`) so a second escalation on the same turn reads back an already-larger `content` and continues the same shared ordinal space rather than colliding with the first.

**Why:** the turn that already abstained or answered partially is gone by the time an escalation happens — its `Candidate` list lived in that request's own `_run_generation` call and was never persisted, so "one prompt with both `<retrieved-content>` and `<web-content>`" (which the prompt file's own wording reads as anticipating) is not reachable without re-running retrieval and re-generating the *entire* answer from scratch, discarding citations and margin cards a user may already be reading. A second, narrower call was chosen instead: cheaper, leaves the already-rendered document half completely untouched (`ask-state.tsx`'s `webAnswer` field is deliberately separate from `answer`, so `isAbstained` and the partial-answer parsing are unaffected), and matches the ticket's own stated fallback for exactly this gap — "if composition cannot attribute reliably [across two kinds of source in one call], keep the halves separate in the answer text rather than guess." Local-then-offset ordinals were chosen over asking the model to continue a global numbering scheme (which it cannot be told about — a second call has no memory of the first) or over minting a disjoint key namespace (`web:1`, `web:2`) for web claims, which would have needed a second code path through `ClaimSpan`'s hover-pairing (`ask-screen.tsx`) instead of the existing one just taking a computed offset. Appending to `messages.content` rather than only returning it to the browser was chosen so the offset computation has one source of truth (the stored row) instead of the endpoint and the browser each tracking "how many claims exist so far" separately and needing to agree without ever comparing notes.

**Consequences:** an escalation's own answer can never cite something from the turn's original document retrieval, and a claim contradicted or agreed with by both a document and a web page is presented as `M2-PARTIAL-BE-059`'s conflict shape only when the *document* half's own generation already detected it — this generation has no visibility into that. If the model is unreachable, `compose_and_generate_web_answer` returns `None` and the endpoint degrades to `answer_text: null`/`citations: []` (the ticket's own "search unavailable" cost is still shown; `M6.5-WEB-FE-192` owns narrating this state further) rather than failing the whole escalation, since the fetch, the grant and the audit record already succeeded by that point. `web_citations.claim_ordinal` for a given row is only meaningful relative to the turn's `messages.content` at write time, not a fixed value — reading it back without also reading `content` as it stood then would misattribute it, which is an existing property of `content`-relative claim numbering (`askwell.agent.claims`) extended here rather than a new one.

**Refs:** `docs/ux/web-search.md` §3–4, `docs/web-search.md` §4, `docs/backlog/M6.5-it-can-look-outside.md` ticket `M6.5-WEB-FE-191` — `api/src/askwell/websearch.py`, `api/src/askwell/agent/compose.py`, `web/components/ask/ask-state.tsx`, `web/components/ask/ask-screen.tsx`, `web/components/ask/web-pairing.tsx`, `web/components/ask/web-result.tsx`, `web/lib/web-citations.ts`, `web/lib/web-search.ts`, `api/tests/test_compose.py`, `api/tests/test_websearch.py`, `api/tests/test_websearch_api.py`.

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-WEB-FE-186`: the server verifies abstention/partial before escalating, not only the offer's own rendering

**Decision:** `POST /ask/{message_id}/escalate/web` (new, `askwell.websearch.register_web_search`) reads the named turn's `messages.content`/`trace` and refuses with 409 unless it actually abstained (`content == ''` and `trace ->> 'reason'` set — the same test `lib/ask.ts`'s `isAbstained` uses) or answered partially (`trace ->> 'partial_coverage'` true). The frontend's `EscalationOffer` only ever renders in those two places, but the check lives on the endpoint, ahead of the call into `escalate_web_search` (`M6.5-WEB-BE-185`), rather than trusting the caller. Acceptance is recorded separately, to `Store.DECISIONS` as `web_search_escalation_accepted`, distinct from `escalate_web_search`'s own `Store.INTERACTIONS` record of what the provider call produced.

**Why:** issue #532 named this gap before the endpoint existed: `test_the_full_answer_path_never_calls_the_provider` (`test_websearch.py`) already treats "the offer is the only route to a search" (C10) as a structural property of the codebase, not a UI convention, and a server that trusted the frontend to only ever ask when appropriate would make that guarantee true by accident rather than by construction — a second client, a replayed request, or a future screen reusing this endpoint could otherwise escalate a fully-grounded turn with nothing to stop it. Checking client-side only was rejected for the same reason `M6.5-WEB-SEC-187`'s egress grant checks `accepted` at the mechanism rather than trusting the caller's docstring: a guarantee that lives only in rendering logic is not a guarantee. Recording acceptance separately from outcome was chosen over folding it into the existing `WEB_SEARCH_ESCALATED` interaction record because they answer different questions under C6 — "did the user authorise this" survives even a failed or unavailable provider call, and a decisions-store entry is the class of record C6 defines for exactly that kind of act (content leaving the machine), not an interaction log entry that a provider outage could make read as "nothing happened."

**Consequences:** a caller of this endpoint (today, only `EscalationOffer`) gets a 409 rather than a search if it is ever pointed at a fully-grounded turn — including by a bug in the frontend gating, which is now a second line of defence rather than the only one. `GET /settings/web-search` (new, same module) is a second small surface this ticket added: the offer needs to know before a click whether there is anywhere to send one, per its own edge case ("provider unavailable or unconfigured — the web option states that plainly rather than disappearing"), and reading `Settings.web_search_provider` through a dedicated endpoint was chosen over extending the existing `/health`/`/network` shapes, which describe component reachability and egress activity respectively, not configuration.

**Refs:** `docs/ux/web-search.md` §2, `docs/backlog/M6.5-it-can-look-outside.md` ticket `M6.5-WEB-FE-186`, issues #532, #534, #535, #536, #542 — `api/src/askwell/websearch.py`, `api/src/askwell/agent/citation_check.py`, `web/components/ask/ask-screen.tsx`, `web/lib/web-search.ts`, `api/tests/test_websearch_api.py`, `api/tests/test_citation_check.py`, `web/lib/web-search.test.ts`.

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-WEB-BE-189`: `web_citations` is a new table, and nothing writes to it from the live answer path yet

**Decision:** A web result used in an answer is stored in a new table, `web_citations` (`message_id`, `claim_ordinal`, `domain`, `title`, `url`, `passage`, `retrieved_at NOT NULL`) — never as a nullable `chunk_id` plus a `kind` flag on the existing `citations` table. `askwell.websearch` gains `WebCitationRecord`, `web_citation_record()` (builds one from a cited `WebSearchResult` plus the claim ordinal that used it) and `record_web_citations()` (writes them, in whatever transaction the caller is already in). Like `askwell.webfetch.fetch_pages` (`M6.5-WEB-BE-188`), nothing in `askwell.ask`'s live generation loop calls `record_web_citations` yet.

**Why:** `citations.chunk_id` is a `NOT NULL` foreign key into `chunks`, and a web result was never a chunk — making it nullable to hold a web result would weaken that guarantee for every existing document citation, and a `kind` discriminator column would leave every future reader (the trace screen, the margin card, export) to check it before trusting `chunk_id`, which is exactly the shared-record-shape risk C10 exists to rule out (`docs/backlog/M6.5-it-can-look-outside.md` ticket `M6.5-WEB-BE-189`'s own Assumption). A second table makes "is this a document citation or a web one" a fact about which table a row is in, not a value someone could get wrong.

Not wiring a caller was the more debatable call and was made for the same reason `188` made it: nothing currently produces a `(WebSearchResult, claim_ordinal)` pair to hand this function. `escalate_web_search` (`185`) returns raw results with no claim attached, and threading a web result into `_cite_claim`'s own index numbering — so the model can cite `[N]` the way it already does for documents and memory facts — is a change to `askwell.ask`'s compose loop that touches how citations are rendered end-to-end (`M6.5-WEB-FE-191`, "mixed answers: each claim points at its own kind of source", is the ticket that names this problem explicitly and is not this one). Building that wiring here, ahead of `191` deciding how a mixed answer actually numbers and renders its sources, risked guessing a shape `191` would then have to unwind. Shipping the storage primitive now — tested directly, exactly as `185`/`187`/`188` each shipped their own isolated piece — lets `191` (or whichever ticket ends up owning the compose-loop change) call a finished, proven function rather than build one from scratch under its own budget.

**Consequences:** `grep -rn web_citation_record\|record_web_citations` outside `api/src/askwell/websearch.py` and its own tests finds nothing today — expected, not a bug, matching `188`'s own precedent. The ticket that wires web results into the answer (citing them by index, deciding where `claim_ordinal` comes from for a web-sourced claim, and calling `record_web_citations` inside `ask.py`'s existing citation-writing transaction alongside `citations`/`fact_usage`) inherits this function rather than inventing its own. `docs/architecture.md` §7 and `api/tests/test_models.py`'s `EXPECTED_TABLES` both now list `web_citations`; `askwell.backup._TABLES` carries it so a backup (and any future conversation export) includes it without a separate change.

**Refs:** `docs/web-search.md` §4, `docs/architecture.md` §7, `api/src/askwell/websearch.py`, `api/src/askwell/db/models.py`, `api/src/askwell/db/migrations/versions/20260922_d3e6b1a94f02_web_citations.py`, `api/tests/test_websearch.py`, `api/tests/test_models.py`.

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-WEB-BE-188`: fetch caps live in a standalone `askwell.webfetch`, not inside `escalate_web_search`

**Decision:** The three fetch caps (result count, per-page size, per-page timeout) and the drop-whole-never-truncate rule live in a new module, `askwell.webfetch.fetch_pages`, that nothing in the live `/ask` or escalation path calls yet. `escalate_web_search` (`M6.5-WEB-BE-185`) is unchanged: it still calls `WebSearchProvider.search()` and treats whatever comes back as the finished result. The delimiter for fetched content (`delimit_web_result`, `<web-content>`) and the prompt file's extended C7 standing statement land in `askwell.agent.compose` the same way, exercised directly by their own tests rather than wired into `compose()`'s own call path.

**Why:** `FixtureWebSearchProvider` — the only `WebSearchProvider` implementation that exists — returns pre-recorded, already-finished results and must stay network-free; that is the property `test_websearch.py`'s zero-network eval guarantee (C1) depends on. Making `escalate_web_search` unconditionally fetch every result's URL would mean the fixture path starts making real HTTP requests during `scripts/dev.sh test` and the eval suite, breaking both. The real provider that will need capped fetching, `ddgs` (`M6.5-WEB-BE-195`), is not built — it is not a dependency of this ticket and has no destination-list decision yet (`M6.5-WEB-SEC-187`'s own "placeholder until 195" note). Wiring caps into a call path with no real caller to exercise it would be exactly the kind of speculative structure `AGENTS.md` §4 forbids. So this ticket ships the caps, the drop semantics, the delimiter, and the corpus-invariance guarantee as a tested, load-bearing unit that `195` takes a dependency on and wires in when it lands — the alternative (holding the whole ticket until `195`'s provider and destination-list decision exist) was rejected because the caps, the delimiter and the never-persist guarantee are independently useful and independently testable now, and `195` inheriting a half-built ticket is worse than `195` inheriting a finished one.

**Consequences:** Nothing currently calls `askwell.webfetch.fetch_pages` or `askwell.agent.compose.delimit_web_result` outside their own tests — a `grep` for either name outside `tests/` and their own module will find nothing today, and that is expected, not a bug. `M6.5-WEB-BE-195` must call `fetch_pages` on the URLs behind whatever `ddgs` returns, open its egress grant around exactly that fetch (not just the search call), and thread `delimit_web_result` into whatever composes the answer once results are stored with the turn (`M6.5-WEB-BE-189`). Reversing this would mean deciding the provider and destination-list question early, which `M6.5-WEB-BLOCKED-195` still has not answered.

**Refs:** `docs/web-search.md` §5, `docs/architecture.md` §5.2, `api/src/askwell/webfetch.py`, `api/src/askwell/agent/compose.py`, `api/tests/test_webfetch.py`.

---

## 2026-09-22 — `M6.5-WEB-SEC-187`: a second, turn-scoped grant primitive next to `PERMITTED_HOST_KEY`, not an extension of it

**Decision:** The per-question egress authorisation web search needs is a *new* mechanism in `askwell.egress` — `open_grant`/`close_grant`, one Redis key per turn (`askwell:egress:grant:{turn_id}`, hard-TTL'd) — rather than reusing or generalising `M7-UPDATE-BE-161`'s `PERMITTED_HOST_KEY`. The grant's lifetime is scoped to exactly the `escalate_web_search` call, opened immediately before the provider is invoked and closed in a `finally` immediately after, not to the whole `/ask` turn around it — the escalation is the only moment anything needs to leave the machine, so that is the shortest honest scope, and it is a strict subset of "closes with the turn" rather than a looser approximation of it.

**Why:** `PERMITTED_HOST_KEY` is a single value: one destination, permitted or not, for the whole proxy. That is correct for the update check, which is a background, non-conversational, effectively-singleton concern — at most one check is ever running. Web search is not: the ticket's own edge case requires "two escalations in quick succession — two grants, each scoped to its own turn, never one merged grant," which a single key cannot represent at all (the second `permit_destination` call would silently overwrite the first, and closing either would close both). Extending `PERMITTED_HOST_KEY` into a set was considered and rejected — it would have meant retrofitting turn-scoping and per-entry expiry onto a primitive whose whole design is "there is exactly one," entangling two callers with genuinely different lifetimes (one sticky-while-the-answer-is-yes destination vs. many short, concurrent, per-turn ones) into one piece of state. A second, narrower primitive keeps `M7-UPDATE-BE-161`'s mechanism exactly as simple as it needs to be and gives web search a shape that actually matches its own requirement.

The hard TTL (`Settings.web_search_grant_ttl_seconds`, `30.0`) is the answer to "a grant left open by a missed code path expires on its own" — the ticket's own edge case for a crashed or hung turn. Redis's own `EX` is the mechanism: it needed no polling, no sweeper process and no dependency on the API process being alive to close anything, which a database-row-plus-cron approach would have required. The `finally` block around the provider call is believed to make the TTL a pure backstop rather than the normal closing path — cancellation (the user pressing stop) delivers `asyncio.CancelledError` through the same `finally`, verified by `test_the_grant_closes_when_the_turn_is_cancelled_mid_search` — but the TTL exists precisely because "believed to" is not "proven for every future caller," and a grant is bounded even if some future code path bypasses the `try`/`finally` entirely.

`open_grant`'s `accepted` argument, checked before any Redis client is constructed, is the proxy-side enforcement of the ticket's own strongest claim: "since no grant exists unless a user accepted an offer, an automatic fetch is refused by the proxy even if application code attempts it." Today `escalate_web_search` always passes `accepted=True` because its own docstring already guarantees it is the sole call site reached from an accepted escalation (`M6.5-WEB-FE-186` has not landed yet, so nothing calls it at all outside tests) — the argument is deliberately not decorative: it is the seam a future caller with a real, possibly-wrong acceptance check plugs into, and it is refused-by-default rather than trusted-by-default the day that caller exists.

**Consequences:** `askwell.egress` now has two authorisation primitives with different shapes (`PERMITTED_HOST_KEY`: one destination, sticky until revoked; the grant keyspace: many destinations, each turn-scoped and self-expiring) rather than one generalised one — a reader has to know which caller they are looking at. `EgressProxy.handle` checks both on every `CONNECT`, so the "no allowlist" test (`test_there_is_no_allowlist_to_configure`) had to keep meaning what it says with a second door in the code; it still passes because neither mechanism is a static config value. `M8-ONLINE-SEC-169` (per-conversation authorisation for online AI) is a third shape again — conversation-scoped, not turn-scoped — and should not be forced into either of these two; if it turns out to need turn-like semantics, that is a decision for that ticket to make explicitly, not inherited by default. The destination this grant names (`Settings.web_search_destination_host`/`_port`, `html.duckduckgo.com:443`) is an explicitly provisional placeholder, not a verified `ddgs` endpoint — `ddgs` fans out across several backend engines rather than one fixed host, and `M6.5-WEB-BE-195` inherits the job of replacing this value (and, if `ddgs` needs more than one destination, widening the grant to a small named list) without touching the grant mechanism itself.

**Refs:** `docs/architecture.md` §5.1, `docs/backlog/M6.5-it-can-look-outside.md` (`M6.5-WEB-SEC-187`), `docs/backlog/README.md` line 514, `api/src/askwell/egress.py`, `api/src/askwell/websearch.py`.

## 2026-09-22 — `M7-UPDATE-BE-161`: the release feed is this repository's own `VERSION` file; the egress proxy learns its first real permitted destination

**Decision:** The "static published document requiring no account and no key" the ticket's own assumption named is `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Rumeasiyan/askwell/main/VERSION` — this repository's own single source of truth for its version (`AGENTS.md` §7), served as a plain file by `raw.githubusercontent.com` rather than by any API. `askwell.update_check` stores the answer as `not_asked`/`yes`/`no` (`update_check_answer` in `settings`, absent key reads as `not_asked`), and `askwell.egress` gained its first real capability beyond refusing: `PERMITTED_HOST_KEY` in Redis, read fresh on every proxy connection, names the one `host:port` a `CONNECT` may be forwarded to, set by `set_answer` the instant the answer becomes `yes` and cleared the instant it stops being `yes`. A manual "check now" (`run_check(manual=True)`) works regardless of the stored answer by opening that same door for the one request and closing it again immediately afterward unless the standing answer already had it open — never leaving a `no` install with anything permitted once the call returns.

**Why:** Three feed candidates were considered. GitHub's Releases API (`api.github.com/repos/.../releases/latest`) was rejected outright — it is an endpoint with server-side logic and rate-limiting by identity, which is exactly what `docs/ux/settings.md` §7's own claim ("a static version file, not an endpoint... there is no server that could log who asked") says this is not. A dedicated `updates.askwell.dev`-style domain was rejected as inventing infrastructure this ticket has no mandate to stand up, host, or secure, for a value the repository already publishes as its own canonical artefact. Reusing `VERSION` itself means the comparison is against the same file `AGENTS.md` §7 already calls the one manually maintained version value — no second registry that could disagree with the first. On the proxy: extending `EgressProxy` for one caller, rather than building the general per-destination permit set issue #345 recommends, was the deliberate scope line — #345's own options list asks for revocation-on-source-deletion and a settings-screen count for *live connections*, neither of which this ticket's actor needs; building that now would be solving a different, larger ticket under this one's number. The mechanism built here (Redis-backed, one key, real `CONNECT` forwarding) is exactly #345's own Option 1 for a single destination, left as prior art rather than closing that issue, which still needs the multi-destination, revocable version. A cursor/"missed checks" design for the weekly timer was rejected in favour of comparing `now() - last_checked_at` against the interval directly — the ticket's own edge case ("machine offline a month — no backlog, one check when it next runs") falls out of that comparison for free and needs no counter to get right.

**Consequences:** issue #345 is commented on, not closed — it now has a concrete forwarding primitive (`askwell.egress.permit_destination`/`revoke_destination`, real `CONNECT` tunnelling in `EgressProxy._forward`) to build its own multi-destination permit set on top of, rather than starting from the proxy's original always-refuse shape. `test_there_is_no_allowlist_to_configure` (`api/tests/test_egress.py`) still passes and still means what it says: the permitted destination is never a static config value, only ever a Redis key a deliberate act opens and closes. `askwell.update_check.run_check` does not itself decide whether a fetched version counts as "newer" beyond a plain `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` tuple compare (`_version_gt`) — an unparseable feed value reads as "no update," never as an error the user has to dismiss, matching the ticket's own "release feed unreachable" edge case. `M7-UPDATE-FE-162` inherits `GET /settings/update-check`'s `update_available`/`latest_known_version` fields to build the presentation on.

**Refs:** `docs/ux/settings.md` §7 and §9; `docs/decisions.md`, 2026-09-21 ("Update checks are chosen at installation, never defaulted on"); issue #345. `api/src/askwell/update_check.py`, `api/src/askwell/egress.py`, `api/src/askwell/worker.py` (`run_update_check` cron), `api/src/askwell/config.py`; `api/tests/test_update_check.py`, `api/tests/test_egress.py`.

## 2026-09-22 — `M7-BACKUP-BE-157`: a backup is a snapshot transaction, not a refuse-during-ingestion rule; the re-embed cost is a stated, unmeasured estimate

**Decision:** `askwell.backup.run_job` gets its consistency from one `REPEATABLE READ` Postgres transaction that reads every included table, not from refusing a backup while ingestion is running — the ticket's own edge case allowed either. `content_encryption.assert_not_migrating` (built and left unused by `M7-SEC-BE-152` for exactly this caller) is that transaction's first statement, so the snapshot is only ever taken while the corpus's plaintext/encrypted state is uniform; a migration starting after that point is invisible to the rest of the run rather than something the run has to detect mid-flight. Excluded from the artefact: `chunks.embedding` and `schema_notes.embedding` (the vector index — regenerable by re-embedding `chunks.content`, which *is* carried), the trace ring buffer (files, never queried by this module at all) and model weights (never in the database). `chunks.content_tsv` is *not* excluded — since `M7-SEC-BE-152` it is a plain application-maintained column, not `GENERATED`, and it is text-sized, so none of the "tens of gigabytes" reasoning that justifies dropping the embedding applies to it. The re-embed cost shown at backup time (`GET /backup/estimate`, restated on the job row and the manifest) is `chunk_count / ESTIMATED_CHUNKS_PER_SECOND`, a deliberately conservative, explicitly unmeasured constant.

**Why:** refusing outright during ingestion was rejected because it would make "I'm about to replace my laptop" — the ticket's own actor — collide with "Askwell is indexing something I added an hour ago," a state a single-user install can be in for a long time on a large corpus, for no correctness gain a snapshot doesn't already give for free. A per-table `REPEATABLE READ` read, one table at a time with intermediate commits, was considered and rejected: Postgres fixes a `REPEATABLE READ` snapshot at the transaction's *first* statement, so committing between tables would take a new snapshot on the next one and reopen exactly the torn-read window a single long transaction closes. The passphrase-migration case is different in kind, not degree — a migration changes what a `chunks.content` byte string *means* row by row while it runs, which no snapshot isolation level can paper over, so that one case is refused (`409`, `MigrationInProgress`) rather than silently included half-migrated. On the estimate: no native inference process is available in this environment to benchmark embedding throughput against (the same gap `docs/BRAIN.md` has recorded since `M1-INDEX-ING-032`), and inventing a number precise enough to look measured would be a worse failure than stating a conservative, clearly-labelled one — the same posture `Settings.retrieval_score_threshold` already established (`0.65`, "unmeasured," `M2-ABSTAIN-RET-053`). A per-hardware-profile table of rates was considered and rejected as scope creep against this ticket's own "one job, one manifest, one exclusion rule — upper bound" granularity note; a single conservative constant, revisited once real measurement is possible, was judged the honest minimum rather than false precision.

**Consequences:** `M7-SEC-BE-152`'s own note — "a future backup/export feature must call `assert_not_migrating` before copying the database... the hook exists now, unused, because no such feature exists yet to call it" — is now closed by this ticket; that note's own `issue #(filed alongside this ticket)` was left as an unfilled placeholder in the original entry (below) rather than a real tracked issue, so there is nothing to close beyond updating this line. `ESTIMATED_CHUNKS_PER_SECOND` (`api/src/askwell/backup.py`) is a number nobody has verified on real hardware and will read as wrong on some machines in both directions until it is replaced with an actual measurement — filed as issue #518 rather than guessed away. `roots` and `document_pages` are included in the artefact (not named explicitly in the ticket's own table list, but "sources and their configuration" and "documents... extracted content" cover them respectively); `ingest_jobs`, `reapply_jobs` and `export_jobs` are deliberately *not* included — they are queue/bookkeeping state for jobs that either finished or were abandoned, not user data, and restoring one onto a machine where the worker that owned it no longer exists would resurrect work nobody asked for. Restore (`M7-BACKUP-BE-158`) inherits a defined table list and column-exclusion map (`_TABLES` in `api/src/askwell/backup.py`) to build against rather than having to re-derive one.

**Refs:** `docs/build-plan.md`; `docs/ux/settings.md` §6; `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` `M7-BACKUP-BE-157`. `api/src/askwell/backup.py`; `api/src/askwell/db/migrations/versions/20260922_c4a1f8d02e77_backup_jobs.py`; `api/tests/test_backup.py`. The 2026-09-21 `M7-SEC-BE-152` entry below (`assert_not_migrating`'s origin).

---

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-LOG-BE-155`: an export rewrites from scratch on restart; a windowed export chains from its own first record, not the genesis value

**Decision:** `askwell.log_export.run_job` gives a run no memory of a previous attempt at the same job — it deletes and re-creates its working directory outright, then re-streams both stores and re-zips, rather than resuming from a byte offset a crashed run left behind. Every file it writes is `<name>.tmp` until it is complete, then renamed into place; nothing under the real name is ever partial. `manifest.json` records, per store, the `prev_hash` its *first exported record* actually chains to, and `askwell.log_export_verifier`'s bundled `verify.py` walks from that recorded value rather than always expecting the universal genesis constant — the genesis value falls out of this automatically for an unfiltered export (the first real record's `prev_hash` *is* genesis), and a `since`-filtered export gets an honest, checkable starting point instead of a verifier that either refuses to run on a window or silently accepts an unrelated one.

**Why:** the ticket's own edge case ("export interrupted — restartable, and no partial file is presented as complete") has two ways to satisfy it: resume a partially-written file from a persisted cursor, or make redoing the whole write cheap enough that resuming isn't worth the extra state. Twelve months of interactions at the default retention (`docs/audit-log.md` §8) is kilobytes to low tens of megabytes of JSON Lines — cheap enough that a full rewrite on every retry is simpler and more obviously correct than tracking a resume point across a Redis-and-Postgres job whose own worker might restart mid-write. A resumable design would also have to answer "was the last line of the `.tmp` file actually flushed to disk, or torn by the crash" — the rewrite-from-scratch answer sidesteps that question rather than getting it right. On the verifier: the alternative was making a windowed export either refuse to verify (defeating the point of shipping one) or silently treat "does not chain to genesis" as `MISSING_GENESIS`, which is exactly the false-tamper report `askwell.audit.verify`'s own docstring already warns against for a different case (two records racing to the same predecessor). Recording the window's actual starting hash in the manifest and trusting it as the walk's root is honest about what a windowed export can and cannot prove — `verify.py` says so explicitly when `since`/`until` are set — rather than pretending a partial export is a complete one.

**Consequences:** a very large, near-limit export that crashes repeatedly near the end redoes real work each time — acceptable at the sizes this store actually reaches, and revisited only if retention or budget defaults grow by an order of magnitude. The manifest's `first_prev_hash` is not itself verified against anything outside the export — a person tampering with *both* the manifest and the first record consistently could forge a plausible-looking window, which is no worse than the honest limit `docs/audit-log.md` §4 already states for the in-database chain (tamper-evident, not tamper-proof) and is called out in `verify.py`'s own date-filtered note rather than left implicit.

**Refs:** `docs/audit-log.md` §5. `api/src/askwell/log_export.py`, `api/src/askwell/log_export_verifier.py`. `api/tests/test_log_export.py` (subprocess round-trip against the real bundled verifier, restart-after-interruption, date-filtered chaining).

---

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-OPS-DEPLOY-154a`: one volume for all of `/var/lib/askwell`, not one per subdirectory

**Decision:** `compose.yaml` mounts a single named volume, `askwell-state`, at `/var/lib/askwell` on both `api` and `worker`, covering `Settings.trace_dir` and (once `M7-LOG-BE-155` lands) `Settings.export_dir` at once, rather than a separate volume — or a separate bind mount — per subdirectory.

**Why:** the two known consumers of this path (`trace_dir`, `export_dir`) are both written by `worker` and read by `api`, and nothing about either one needs isolation from the other the way `sandbox-data` needs isolation from `postgres-data` (C3) — there is no cap or blast-radius reason to keep them apart. A mount per subdirectory would need a `compose.yaml` change every time a third thing under `/var/lib/askwell` showed up; one mount of the parent means it does not. The alternative in #489's own options list — streaming export bytes through Redis's result backend or a Postgres `bytea`/large-object column instead of a shared filesystem path — was rejected as disproportionate: `run_export` deliberately stays off-heap and streamed to disk for files that can run to a year of interaction history, and routing that through either datastore would mean holding the whole file in memory or in a table built for something else, for no gain over a filesystem both containers can already reach.

**Consequences:** any third writer under `/var/lib/askwell` is visible on both containers automatically, with no compose change — but also means neither `api` nor `worker` can be given a narrower filesystem view of just the subdirectory it actually needs; a bug in one that writes garbage under the other's expected subdirectory is not stopped by the mount boundary. Reversing this would mean splitting the volume per subdirectory, which is a `compose.yaml` and a `podman volume` migration, not a code change.

**Refs:** #486, #489. `compose.yaml` (`api`, `worker`, `volumes:`). `docs/manual-tests/M7-OPS-DEPLOY-154a.md`.

---

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-SEC-BE-152`: chunk content is encrypted; the search index and the embedding are not, and the documentation says so

**Decision:** `chunks.content` is encrypted with the same passphrase-derived key `M7-SEC-BE-151` already built, per row, with a `content_encrypted` flag saying which of plaintext or a Fernet token the row currently holds. `chunks.content_tsv` (full-text search) and `chunks.embedding` (dense retrieval) stay derived from **plaintext**, always — a generated `GENERATED ALWAYS AS (to_tsvector(content))` column cannot be kept, because Postgres would tokenise a Fernet token's own base64 bytes into a search index full of garbage; `content_tsv` becomes an application-maintained column instead (`ALTER COLUMN ... DROP EXPRESSION`, migration `b7e91a4c3f65`), computed by `askwell.chunk.run` from the plaintext chunk before it is ever encrypted. Migration between plaintext and encrypted corpora (`askwell.content_encryption.migrate_chunk_content`) is per-row and resumable **without persisting a cursor or any key material**: a batch's `WHERE content_encrypted != target` clause already excludes whatever a previous, interrupted run finished, so retrying the same `set_passphrase`/`change_passphrase`/`remove_passphrase` call with the same passphrase is an ordinary resume, not a special path.

**Why:** the alternative — encrypting `content_tsv` and `embedding` too — was rejected outright as the ticket's own Out of Scope line states: retrieval on a single local machine needs both readable, and pretending they could be encrypted without breaking search would be exactly the overclaim `docs/architecture.md` §3 (C6) already warns against for the audit log. A cursor-based resume (storing "last migrated chunk id" in `settings`) was considered and rejected as needless: because each row is self-describing (`content_encrypted`), the `WHERE` clause itself is the cursor, and a persisted one would just be a second source of truth that could disagree with the rows after a crash. Storing the in-flight key material to make an interrupted migration resume without re-entering the passphrase was rejected on the same grounds `M7-SEC-BE-151` already established for the unlock state itself — a durably stored key defeats the reason a passphrase exists — so resume instead relies on the caller supplying the same passphrase again, which every retry of a failed request already does naturally.

**Consequences:** a stolen, imaged disk with a passphrase set exposes document count, approximate document length, and every word that appears in any document (via `content_tsv`'s lexemes and `embedding`'s vectors) — but not the documents' actual text. This is the honest claim `docs/architecture.md` §7 now states in those terms, not "the corpus is encrypted." Ingestion (`askwell.chunk.run`, `askwell.embed.run`) reads the passphrase key through the same in-memory-only, per-process mechanism `M7-SEC-BE-151` built — which means the **worker** process (chunking and embedding both run there) inherits that ticket's own filed gap: a passphrase set through the API process does not unlock the worker, so new documents cannot finish indexing until the worker process itself restarts. This was a narrow, background-job-only gap before; it is now also the ordinary ingest path, so its severity has grown, and issue #(filed alongside this ticket) reflects that. A future backup/export feature must call `askwell.content_encryption.assert_not_migrating` before copying the database — the hook exists now, unused, because no such feature exists yet to call it (grep confirms no `backup`/`export` route in `api/src/askwell`).

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` `M7-SEC-BE-152`; `docs/architecture.md` §7; `docs/ux/settings.md` §4; `api/src/askwell/content_encryption.py`, `api/src/askwell/passphrase.py`, `api/src/askwell/chunk.py`, `api/src/askwell/embed.py`, `api/src/askwell/retrieve.py`, `api/src/askwell/clarify.py`, `api/src/askwell/suggestions.py`, `api/src/askwell/reapply.py`; migration `20260921_b7e91a4c3f65_chunk_content_encrypted.py`; the 2026-09-21 `M7-SEC-BE-151` entry below.

---

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-SEC-BE-151`: the passphrase's unlock is in-memory only, so it does not carry to the worker process

**Decision:** `askwell.passphrase` stores whether a passphrase is set as a verifier — a known plaintext encrypted with the derived key — in `settings`, and stores the *unlocked* key nowhere durable at all: a plain module-level variable, cleared to `None` on every process start. `set_passphrase`/`change_passphrase`/`remove_passphrase` re-encrypt every row in `sources.config_encrypted` in the same transaction as the change (decrypt with the old key, encrypt with the new one), which is what makes the ticket's own assumption — "the key derivation already built for credentials extends to include the passphrase without re-entering credentials" — literally true: nobody retypes a database password because they added a passphrase. `askwell.crypto`'s `Locked` (a new `CredentialsLocked` subclass) is what every one of the three existing decrypt call sites (`askwell.connections._load_connection_config`, `askwell.connections.create_connection_source`, `askwell.ask._connection_credentials`) now raises instead of successfully decrypting with the wrong key.

**Why:** the alternative — caching the unlocked key on the same run-directory bind mount the install secret itself already lives on (`M4-CONN-SEC-098`) — was rejected outright, not merely deprioritised: that mount is host-persisted, so a cached key surviving a container restart would make "restart prompts for the passphrase before anything decrypts" (this ticket's own acceptance criterion) false the moment it mattered most, on a stolen laptop that was asleep rather than powered off. In-memory-only is the only construction where a restart is genuinely a lock. The cost is real and is not hidden: `api` and `worker` are separate OS processes (`compose.yaml`), so the API process unlocking when the user types their passphrase does nothing for the worker process, which has no interactive surface to unlock through at all. A live connection's background introspection and health check (`askwell.connections.run_introspection`, `check_connection_health`) therefore fail `CredentialsLocked` for as long as a passphrase is set, permanently, regardless of what the API process's own state is — filed as its own issue rather than patched here, because sharing an unlocked key across processes safely is a change to C1's own security boundary (a local socket, or a signal through Redis) and does not fit inside a 3-4 hour ticket sized around three UI operations.

The verifier is a canary, not a stored hash: `crypto.decrypt` already distinguishes a wrong key from a right one via `CredentialsLocked`, so checking a candidate passphrase never needs the passphrase text compared to anything on disk, and a wrong passphrase and a wrong install secret report through the exact same exception path an operator already has to reason about.

**Consequences:** setting, changing or removing the passphrase is now the only way `sources.config_encrypted` ever gets re-encrypted, and each is a decisions record with no value (`passphrase_set`/`_changed`/`_removed`), per this ticket's own Audit requirement. `askwell.crypto`'s own docstring ("room for a passphrase to be folded in later without re-encrypting anything already stored") is about the *derivation* being extensible without a new scheme, not about skipping re-encryption of already-stored ciphertext — this entry is the clarification for the next reader who takes that sentence literally. Reversing the in-memory-only choice to fix the worker gap would need a genuinely new cross-process mechanism, not a config flag.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` `M7-SEC-BE-151`; `docs/ux/settings.md` §4 and §8; `api/src/askwell/passphrase.py`, `api/src/askwell/crypto.py`, `api/src/askwell/connections.py`, `api/src/askwell/ask.py`; the 2026-09-18 `M4-CONN-SEC-098` entry above.

## 2026-09-21 — Update checks are chosen at installation, never defaulted on

**Decision:** Askwell asks, once, during installation: may it check for new versions? The question states exactly what is sent — the current version, nothing else — and what comes back. Answer yes and it checks weekly against the published release feed. Answer no, or dismiss it, and it never makes the request; the setting stays visible and off, and the manual "check now" control works either way. Nothing is enabled by an upgrade, and an existing install that has never been asked is treated as "no" until it is. This unblocks the two tickets, now rewritten as `M7-UPDATE-BE-161` and `M7-UPDATE-FE-162`.

**Why:** Three shapes were on the table and the choice was the product owner's, not an engineering default — the ticket says so explicitly and forbids picking one. **Out-of-band only** (release notes on the repo, no network code at all) is the purest against C1 and was rejected because it is also the one where nobody learns a security fix exists: a free local install has no support channel and no update nag, so in practice the user finds out never. **Periodic opt-in, on by default** was what was asked for first, and it cannot be built: the ticket's own acceptance criterion is "the mechanism must be off by default", and C1 says an outbound call is a deliberate, per-unit act. Shipping it on by default would mean a product whose central promise is "disconnect the cable and it works identically" quietly making a weekly request nobody agreed to — the exact thing the constraint exists to prevent, and an audit failure on the ticket's own terms. **Asked at installation** gets what the on-by-default answer actually wanted — most people end up with update checks on, because most people say yes when asked plainly — without any install ever making a request its owner did not agree to. The trade-off accepted knowingly: one more question during install, on a product whose first ten minutes decide everything.

**Consequences:** The installers (`M7-PACK-DEPLOY-139`/`140`/`141`) each gain this prompt, and it must read identically on all three. The egress proxy's permitted destinations gain exactly one host, opened only when the answer was yes, and the `/network` count must still read zero on an install that said no — which is the test that proves this. Enabling or disabling the check is a decisions record. Reversing this means either dropping the prompt (back to out-of-band) or defaulting it on, and the second would need C1 itself rewritten, not just this entry.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` — `M7-UPDATE-BE-161`, `M7-UPDATE-FE-162`; `docs/ux/settings.md` §7 and §9; `AGENTS.md` §3 C1; product owner's answer, 2026-09-21.

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-SET-FE-148`: storage settles for approximate and honest over exact and silent

**Decision:** Three things this ticket needed did not exist and were built to the smallest shape that makes the acceptance criteria true without pretending more is built than is. First, `askwell.sources.storage_by_source` answers "index size per source" with `octet_length(chunk.content)` summed per source plus `chunk_count × Settings.embedding_dimensions × 4` (one float4 per embedding lane) — the pgvector column's on-disk shape, not `pg_total_relation_size` sliced by source, which would need a query heavy enough to revisit on every settings load, ruled out by the ticket's own Assumption. It reports `None` ("unknown"), never `0`, for a `connection`/`dump` source (queried in place, never chunked) and for a source still `queued`/`indexing` (nothing measurable exists yet). Second, issue #484 (effective log budget silently capped by 5% free disk, with no field saying so) is closed by adding `Usage.configured_bytes` alongside the existing `budget_bytes`, so a caller can compare the two rather than re-deriving `min()`'s own logic. Third, the retention window gets a real value (`log_budget.get_retention_months`/`set_retention_months`, a `settings` key plus a decisions record, the same shape `set_budget` already established) with **no prune behind it** — the prune that acts on the window is `M7-LOG-BE-154`, not yet built, and the settings screen says exactly that rather than implying the number already does something. Export and prune ships as a visible, disabled entry point stating it is not built (`M7-LOG-BE-155`/`M7-LOG-BE-154`), the same pattern `docs/ux/settings.md` §3 already uses for Online AI ahead of Phase 7.

**Why:** The ticket's own Dependencies line named only `M7-LOG-BE-153` and `M1-LIB-FE-050`, both complete, but its Scope asked for retention control and an export-and-prune entry point — both of which `docs/BRAIN.md`'s own record of `M7-LOG-BE-153` already named as separate, unbuilt tickets (`M7-LOG-BE-154`, `M7-LOG-BE-155`) in that ticket's own Out of Scope line. Stopping the whole ticket over that gap would have thrown away the three displays that *are* fully backed (index size, log budget, the at-the-limit statement) over the two that are not. Silently faking a working export button or a retention control that implies enforcement was rejected outright — `AGENTS.md`'s own instruction is "a stub that looks finished is worse than an honest halt" — so the retention *value* was built (it is genuinely useful on its own: it is a real decisions record today, and `M7-LOG-BE-154` will read it rather than invent its own setting), while the *button* for export/prune, which has no meaningful partial version, states plainly that it is not there yet rather than being wired to an endpoint that does not exist.

**Consequences:** `M7-LOG-BE-154`, when built, must read `interaction_retention_months` rather than adding a second retention setting — one already exists and is user-facing. Per-source index size undercounts what the chunks table actually occupies on disk (row overhead, the HNSW graph, the GIN full-text index are all excluded) — acceptable per the ticket's own "approximate" framing and its stated known gap, but not a number to cite in a support conversation about actual disk usage. The settings screen's manual walkthrough could not be completed against this machine's own long-running compose stack: `GET /log-budget` reads free disk from `Settings.trace_dir` (`/var/lib/askwell/traces` by default), which has no volume in `compose.yaml` and does not exist in the running `api` container — the same pre-existing gap `M7-LOG-BE-153`'s own decision log entry already flagged as unexercised against this stack. Verified instead with a manually created directory inside the running container (not persisted, not a fix) and the full `pytest -m requires_db` suite (16 tests, `test_log_budget.py` + new `test_storage.py`) against a real Postgres.

**Refs:** `docs/ux/settings.md` §5; `docs/ux/library.md` §6; `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §6; `api/src/askwell/log_budget.py`; `api/src/askwell/sources.py` (`SourceStorage`, `storage_by_source`); `api/tests/test_log_budget.py`; `api/tests/test_storage.py`; `web/lib/storage.ts`; `web/components/settings/storage.tsx`; issue #484 (closed); `docs/BRAIN.md`'s `M7-LOG-BE-153` entry (names `M7-LOG-BE-154`/`M7-LOG-BE-155` as the deferred work).

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-LOG-BE-153`: the effective budget is recomputed every call, and checked after validation

**Decision:** `api/src/askwell/log_budget.py` measures the interaction store (`pg_total_relation_size('audit_interactions')`) and the trace ring buffer's own `total_bytes()`, and compares their sum against an *effective* budget — `min(configured cap, 5% of current free disk)` — recomputed fresh on every call rather than snapshotted once at install. The three add-source routes (`POST /sources`, `/sources/dump`, `/sources/connection`) call `enforce_ingestion_allowed` after their own validation runs, not before: `add()`/`add_dump()`/`add_connection()` refuse a malformed request (bad folder, blank host, unsupported engine) on those terms alone, several of them without ever touching the database, and only once a request is otherwise going to proceed does the hard-limit check run and — if it fires — roll the whole transaction back with it.

**Why:** The ticket's own edge case says the staging must apply "the same" whether Askwell's own writes filled the disk or something else did — recomputing `min(cap, 5%·free)` on every measurement gets that for free: as free disk shrinks from any cause, the effective budget shrinks with it, with no separate "disk filled externally" code path to keep in sync with the ordinary one. Snapshotting the 5% figure once at install was the alternative and was rejected because it cannot express that edge case at all — a laptop that fills up nine months in would keep whatever number was true on day one. Checking the hard limit after validation rather than before was forced by the existing test suite (`test_sources_api.py`), which already asserts several refusals — a relative folder, a blank connection host, an unsupported engine — happen with **no database configured on the client at all**; checking budget first would have turned every one of those into an `OperationalError` instead of the intended 400. Putting the check last, inside the same transaction as the write it is guarding, means a refused batch's rows never survive the rollback — there is no separate "undo" step.

**Consequences:** A source is refused for the same underlying condition (log storage full) with a 507, distinct from the 400 the same routes already use for a malformed request — the two must not be merged into one status later, because a client branching on the difference (retry after freeing space, vs. fix the request) needs them to stay distinct. `GET /log-budget`'s reading and an ingestion attempt's reading of "current stage" can disagree by the width of one Postgres write between the two calls; nothing here serialises them, and nothing needs to — the two matter for a notice and a refusal respectively, not a hard invariant. Free disk is read from `Settings.trace_dir`'s filesystem, not from wherever Postgres's own volume lives — the API and database run in separate containers with no shared mount this module could inspect, and the trace directory is a fair proxy: it is written to by this same product and, in the ordinary single-machine deployment, is on the same disk everything else is.

**Refs:** `docs/audit-log.md` §3, §8; `docs/backlog/M7-someone-else-can-install-it.md` `M7-LOG-BE-153`; `api/src/askwell/log_budget.py`; `api/src/askwell/sources.py`; `api/tests/test_log_budget.py`.

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-PROBE-FE-138`: two named warnings, one setting that both a probe and a person can write

**Decision:** `GET /setup`'s hardware profile (`api/src/askwell/setup.py`'s `_resolve_profile`) now prefers the real host probe's result over the M1 in-container reading whenever `askwell-probe` has ever run, so the welcome screen sees the same detection `GET /probe` does rather than a permanently-stale interim reading once the real probe exists. It also carries a new `probe_failed` flag distinct from `floor_met`: a genuine reading under the `light` floor still says so with concrete, per-GB numbers (`deploy/probe/askwell-probe`'s own `reason` string, unchanged from `M7-PROBE-DEPLOY-137`), while a detection failure — memory could not be measured at all — states that the `standard` profile is a stated fallback, not a measurement. The welcome screen (`StepMachineCheck`) renders these as two different sentences rather than one generic "it might be slow". Settings gains a profile override (`POST /probe/override`, `web/components/settings/hardware-profile.tsx`): it writes the same `hardware.profile` setting `askwell.probe.apply_probe_result` writes, so every downstream reader — model tier selection, voice's latency threshold — sees an override without a second seam, and it never refuses a profile the machine did not measure into, per the ticket's own "never refuses on hardware grounds" rule. Overriding and re-probing share one setting deliberately: a genuine reprobe (`sync_probe_result` seeing a newer `probed_at`) always overwrites a stale override, which is the "re-run on demand" behaviour naming its own reset rather than a second flag to keep in sync.

**Why:** The alternative to reusing `hardware.profile` for the override was a separate `hardware.profile_override` setting, checked everywhere the profile is read — rejected, because every current reader (`_resolve_profile`, voice's latency threshold, model tier defaults) already reads the one key, and a second key would need every one of them updated to check it first, with a real risk of one being missed and quietly reading the un-overridden value. Writing directly to the shared key costs exactly one property: an override and a probe cannot be told apart by inspecting `hardware.profile` alone, which is why `GET /probe` also returns `overridden` and `detected_profile` computed at read time (comparing the stored setting against what the probe file itself says) rather than persisting an `overridden` bit that could drift from the setting it describes. The below-floor and detection-failure warnings were kept as two separate conditions rather than folded into one "hardware trouble" banner — a user with 6GB and a user whose probe script hit an unrecognised platform are in different situations (the first can expect it to be slow forever; the second's next honest probe might land somewhere entirely different), and conflating them would make the below-floor warning's concrete GB numbers disappear into a vaguer sentence for no reason.

**Consequences:** `askwell.setup.PROFILE_SELECTED`'s `probed_tier`/`floor_met`/`probe_source` fields now come from `_resolve_profile` (real probe when available) rather than always from `askwell.hardware.probe()`, so a "why is this slow" audit read a year from now reflects what was actually detected, not the interim M1 reading, on any machine the real probe has run on. `askwell.probe.PROFILE_OVERRIDDEN` is a new hash-chained decision kind, recorded on every override regardless of whether it changes anything the profile can actually support — matching `PROFILE_PROBED`'s existing "every selection event, not just changes" rule. Nothing here changes what happens when an overridden profile's model fails to load; that failure path is `askwell.inference`'s to report, unchanged by this ticket.

**Refs:** `docs/ux/first-run.md` §2 step 2, §4; `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §6; `docs/ux/settings.md` §2; `api/src/askwell/probe.py`, `api/src/askwell/setup.py`, `web/components/welcome/welcome-screen.tsx`, `web/components/settings/hardware-profile.tsx`, `web/lib/probe.ts`.

---

## 2026-09-21 — `M7-PROBE-DEPLOY-137`: the host probe measures and selects; the API records; a file, not a socket, joins them

**Decision:** `deploy/probe/askwell-probe` is a standalone stdlib script — the same shape as `deploy/inference/askwell-inference` — that runs on the host, measures RAM/CPU/accelerator/free-disk, and selects one of the four `docs/architecture.md` §6 profiles itself, entirely in Python with no dependency on the `askwell` package. It writes its result as JSON to the same `${ASKWELL_RUN_DIR}:/run/askwell` bind mount the inference socket and install key already share. `askwell.probe` on the API side reads that file, applies it to `settings` and an `audit_decisions` record (`profile_probed`) whenever the file's `probed_at` is newer than the last one it recorded, and exposes both through `GET /probe`. `POST /probe/rerun` cannot invoke the probe itself — a container cannot start a host process, the entire reason this exists as a host script in the first place — so it touches a flag file next to the result, which `askwell-probe --watch` (also host-side, started by `scripts/dev.sh probe --watch`) polls for.

**Why:** The alternative to duplicating the four thresholds in two languages was to keep only raw measurements in the host script and do selection in importable, unit-tested Python — rejected, because the host script cannot import that package (stdlib-only, host Python is not ours to choose, `docs/decisions.md`'s existing inference entry), so "selection in Python" would still mean a second copy of the thresholds somewhere the host script *can* read, which is the same duplication with an extra indirection. Instead the host script owns selection outright and a test (`test_the_host_script_and_the_application_agree_on_the_profile_names`) asserts its four profile constants haven't drifted from `askwell.probe.PROFILES`, the same drift-guard pattern `test_inference.py` already uses for `ProcessState`. For "re-run on demand from settings" (this ticket's own scope line), the rejected alternative was wiring the request through the not-yet-built Tauri shell (`M7-TAURI-DEPLOY-184a`) so the settings UI could spawn the host binary directly — the correct long-term answer, but that shell doesn't exist yet, and blocking this ticket on it would leave "recording and re-running" undeliverable this phase. A poll-file watcher costs one small loop in a script that already has to run continuously for the watch case, and gives the settings screen a working `POST /probe/rerun` today; it should be revisited once the shell exists and can do this without polling. The M1 in-container `askwell.hardware.probe()` (`M1-LIB-FE-052`) stays as the pre-probe fallback exactly as its own docstring already promised — `askwell.probe.read_probe_result` returns `None` rather than inventing a second "unknown" answer, and the API falls back to the M1 reading one layer up.

**Consequences:** A decisions record is written on every successful probe, including a rerun that confirms the same profile — deliberate, matching `setup.PROFILE_SELECTED`'s existing rule that every selection event is worth recording, not just changes, because "why is this on light" needs an answer even when light was chosen twice. Audit payloads convert every measurement to fixed-unit integers (`ram_mb`, `vram_mb`, `disk_free_mb`) before writing, because `askwell.audit.canonical_payload` refuses floats outright. `Settings.profile` (`config.Profile`, `light`/`balanced`/`full`) remains a separate, older vocabulary from the four hardware tiers this ticket uses — already tracked as issue #466, commented on here rather than fixed, since renaming it is a breaking config change outside this ticket's scope.

**Refs:** `docs/architecture.md` §6, `docs/ux/settings.md` §2, `docs/ux/first-run.md` §2 step 2, `deploy/probe/askwell-probe`, `api/src/askwell/probe.py`, `api/tests/test_probe_host.py`, `api/tests/test_probe_records.py`, issue #466.
