## 2026-09-18 — `M4-SQL-VAL-104`: the gate walks the whole parsed tree, not just the top-level statement type

**Decision:** `askwell.sql.validate` rejects a candidate query unless `sqlglot` parses it to exactly one statement whose top level is `exp.Select` or `exp.SetOperation` (`UNION`/`INTERSECT`/`EXCEPT`) **and** no data-modifying, definition, locking, or write-marker node (`exp.Insert`/`Update`/`Delete`/`Create`/`Drop`/`Alter`/`TruncateTable`/`Merge`/`Grant`/`Revoke`/`Copy`/`Set`/`Pragma`/`Command`/`Into`/`Lock`) appears *anywhere* in the parsed tree, not only at the root. A short, explicitly incomplete denylist of side-effecting function names (`pg_terminate_backend`, `xp_cmdshell`, `LOAD_FILE`, and similar) is checked separately against `exp.Anonymous` calls, since `sqlglot` has no general way to classify a function call as side-effecting.

**Why:** Checking only the top-level node type was the first design considered, since it is what "single SELECT or WITH" naturally suggests, and it is wrong: PostgreSQL allows a data-modifying statement inside a CTE when the CTE ends in `RETURNING` — `WITH d AS (DELETE FROM orders RETURNING *) SELECT * FROM d` parses with `exp.Select` at the top and deletes every row in `orders` when executed. A top-level-only check would accept this outright. This is not a contrived case; it is the ticket's own "nesting to disguise a modification" edge case, verified concretely against `sqlglot` 30.18.0 rather than assumed. Walking the entire tree with `Expression.find_all` costs nothing extra a regex-based approach would also have to pay, and it is exactly the class of thing C2's own reasoning ("regex misses nested statements") already predicted — the fix is not a nesting-specific special case, it is refusing to trust the root node's type as a proxy for the whole statement's shape at all. The ticket's other named disguise vector, a comment hiding a second statement, needed no code at all: `sqlglot`'s tokenizer discards comments before they enter the parse tree, so `SELECT 1 -- ; DROP TABLE x` was already, unambiguously, one statement — the same is true for the real database executing it, which is why a SQL comment was never a plausible attack vector for a parser-based gate in the first place, only for a regex one operating on raw text.

The side-effect function denylist was considered as either exhaustive-and-trusted or omitted entirely, and both were rejected: omitting it silently passes `SELECT pg_terminate_backend(123)` as a "read" because nothing in `sqlglot`'s grammar distinguishes a side-effecting function call from a pure one — both parse as an ordinary function call node. Treating the list as exhaustive would overclaim coverage the module cannot actually provide; the module docstring and this entry both say plainly that an unlisted function, a same-named function on an engine not covered, or a side effect reached by a mechanism other than a function call, passes through unflagged. The read-only database role (`askwell.sql_execute`'s own layer 1, already built by `M4-SQL-DB-107`) is what actually stops most of what this list would miss — this module's honest job is to reject what it can recognise as a parsed shape, not to be the sole barrier.

**Consequences:** `askwell.sql` is a new package (`api/src/askwell/sql/`), matching the path `docs/architecture.md` §5 already named for C2 before any code existed there. `sqlglot>=25.0` (resolved 30.18.0, MIT-licensed, ships its own type stubs — no `mypy` override needed, unlike `pypdfium2`/`pytds`/`pytesseract`) is a new runtime dependency. `validate_query` is not reachable from `POST /ask` — the same "do not wire ahead of a dependency that does not exist yet" reasoning `M4-SQL-DB-107` and `M4-SQL-BE-103` both recorded about themselves, now stated for the ticket both of those depend on in reverse: limit injection (`M4-SQL-VAL-105`) and the `EXPLAIN` dry run (`M4-SQL-VAL-106`) are still missing layers, and wiring a generated query through to a real turn before all three safety layers exist would be exactly what C2 exists to prevent. `Settings.sql_validation_timeout_seconds` (default 2.0s) bounds `sqlglot` parsing time separately from `sql_statement_timeout_seconds`, which bounds the database — a hang in the parser and a runaway query at the database are different failure modes with different fixes, so they get different knobs.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-SQL-VAL-104`; `api/src/askwell/sql/validate.py`, `api/src/askwell/sql/__init__.py`; `api/tests/test_sql_validate.py`, `api/tests/test_sql_validate_db.py`; the `M4-SQL-BE-103` and `M4-SQL-DB-107` entries below (the "do not wire ahead of a dependency that does not exist yet" precedent this entry follows in reverse).

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-SQL-BE-103`: schema retrieval reuses `schema_notes` rather than a second representation; source disambiguation is presence-of-any-match, not a scored comparison; a cross-database question and an ambiguous one get the same response

**Decision:** Three choices in one ticket, all in the new `askwell.agent.sql_generate`. First: "relevance-based schema subset retrieval with notes and memory" is built entirely on the existing `askwell.memory.retrieve_relevant_facts` — table- and column-level `schema_notes` rows, already ranked by lexical relevance to a question and already excluding a stale note (`M4-SCHEMA-BE-102`) — called with a higher `note_limit` (40, not the document-answer default of 5) rather than a fresh bulk `SchemaInventory`-shaped context built from scratch. Second: choosing among several connected databases is presence, not a score — does *any* schema note for a candidate source match the question at all (`retrieve_relevant_facts` with `note_limit=1` per source), not a compared `ts_rank` magnitude across sources. Third: a question that needs two databases at once and a question that is merely ambiguous between two get the identical response — `SelectionReason.AMBIGUOUS`, naming the candidates rather than guessing — with no separate detection for "genuinely spans two" attempted.

**Why:** The first follows directly from `M4-DUMP-ING-088`'s own precedent, quoted almost verbatim in this ticket's module docstring: that ticket rejected building a second schema representation under a different name when the real one (`M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`–`102`'s `schema_notes`) already existed and already did the job, on the grounds that building it here would duplicate work a dependency ticket already did. The same reasoning applies more strongly here — `schema_notes` is not merely available, it is the *exact* plain-language table/column/PK/FK description this ticket's own Real-World Example ("using the user's own note about the status codes") assumes exists, and `retrieve_relevant_facts` is not merely available but already does bounded, relevance-ranked, stale-excluding retrieval over it — reimplementing that against a bulk `SchemaInventory` fetch would mean two things deciding "what is the schema subset" that could silently disagree.

The second: a compared-score approach ("source A's best match ranks higher than source B's") was considered and rejected because `ts_rank` is not calibrated to mean anything across two different documents' worth of text — a source with more tables produces more candidate rows and a wider score distribution than a source with few, so "A's top score beats B's" would as often reflect corpus size as actual relevance. Presence-of-a-match is coarser but honest: it only ever claims "this source's schema mentions something related to the question," never "this source is more related than that one," which is the only claim the underlying full-text search actually supports.

The third: detecting "the question genuinely needs two databases" separately from "the question could plausibly mean either" was considered — some heuristic over which named entities in the question map to which source's tables — and rejected as an unreliable second classifier layered on top of an already-approximate first one (presence-of-a-match), for two edge cases whose UI response is the same list-the-candidates-and-ask shape either way (this ticket's own Testing Notes test only the ambiguous case; the cross-database case is named in Known Gaps as accepted, not to be built around). Since this module also never sends more than one source's schema to the model in a single generation call, a real cross-database question cannot produce a technically-valid-but-wrong query even by accident — at worst it produces a query against whichever source happened to be selected, referencing only that source's own tables, which a person reading the disclosed SQL (`docs/ux/ask.md` §4, "Expand SQL") can see is incomplete. One mechanism, not two, covers both named edge cases without a heuristic that cannot be verified without two real, differently-shaped databases to test against.

**Consequences:** `agent.compose`'s C7-delimiting helpers (`delimit_memory_facts`, `delimit_schema_notes`, `confidence_label`, and the new `flag_injection_text`) moved from being private to `agent.conflict` to shared, public-within-the-package functions — `agent.conflict.compose_conflict`'s own output is unchanged (`test_conflict.py` passes with no edits), but a third prompt-composing module needing the same delimiting only had to import it, not reimplement it a second time; a fourth one (M5's multi-step reasoning, most likely) should do the same rather than add a third copy. `generate_candidate_query` is not reachable from `POST /ask` — the same "do not wire ahead of the validation that has to exist first" call `M4-SQL-DB-107` made about itself, now made about the ticket that depends on it too, so a cold-start walkthrough cannot yet observe a database answer through the product; only `M4-SQL-VAL-104` onward makes that true. A locked live-connection credential (`M4-CONN-SEC-098`) is silently excluded from `list_database_sources` rather than surfaced as its own failure reason — consistent with `askwell.connections.run_introspection` treating the same condition as "this source cannot be used right now," but it means a database question against a connection whose credential just became unreadable reports as if that source did not exist, rather than naming the lock. Filed as a known gap rather than built here, since `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §4 does not name this specific case and inventing UI copy for it was out of this ticket's scope.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-SQL-BE-103`; `api/src/askwell/agent/sql_generate.py`, `api/src/askwell/agent/prompts/sql_generation.v1.md`, `api/src/askwell/agent/compose.py`, `api/src/askwell/agent/conflict.py`; `api/tests/test_sql_generate.py`; the `M4-DUMP-ING-088` and `M4-SQL-DB-107` entries below (the "do not wire ahead of a dependency that does not exist yet" and "reuse rather than duplicate" precedents this entry follows).

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-SCHEMA-BE-102`: a stale schema note is excluded from generation outright, not caveated in place; an omitted table's name is now tracked, not just counted

**Decision:** Three related reversals of choices `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`/`-101` made, all inside the same ticket. First: `retrieve_relevant_facts` — the one retrieval path both document answers and database question-answering draw their schema-notes context from — now excludes a `stale` note with `AND NOT stale`, rather than including it with the `agent.conflict._delimit_schema_notes` caveat `M4-SCHEMA-ING-101` added ("column no longer found in the current schema"). `get_active_schema_notes` is unchanged and still returns a stale note, unfiltered, for the library and memory screens. Second: `SchemaInventory` gained `omitted_tables: frozenset[str] | None`, the Postgres-only set of relation names `has_table_privilege` found non-selectable — `_build_postgresql_inventory` already computed this internally as `class_rows` and threw the names away, keeping only `omitted_count`. Third: a stale note now carries `stale_reason` (`'dropped'` vs `'possibly_invisible'`, using the set above) and `reattach_suggestion` (the single unambiguous rename candidate, when a table lost exactly one column and gained exactly one in the same run) — two new nullable `schema_notes` columns (migration `20260918_3f7c1a9e5d20_schema_note_stale_reason.py`).

**Why:** The first is the one that most looks like reopening a settled decision, so it gets the most weight: `M4-SCHEMA-BE-102`'s own Acceptance Criteria and Validation Rules, written after `-101` shipped, say plainly "the note is not applied to query generation while stale" and "a stale note is never used in generation" — not "is caveated." A caveat delegated to a small local model reading a prompt is a real risk for exactly the fact class C5 exists to protect: the model can still cite a caveated-but-present note as if it settled the question, the same failure mode abstention-over-invention exists to prevent for retrieval generally. Excluding it outright removes that risk instead of trusting the model to read a footnote correctly every time. The caveat-rendering code in `_delimit_schema_notes` was not deleted, though: `retrieve_relevant_facts` is `compose_conflict`'s only caller for `retrieved_notes` today, so the `.stale` branch is unreachable in production right now, but a pure function should not assume every future caller filters the way today's one does, and keeping a currently-inert caveat correct costs nothing (a direct unit test, `test_a_stale_schema_note_carries_a_caveat_if_it_ever_reaches_this_prompt`, keeps it from silently rotting). The second: `-100`'s own test name, `test_postgresql_inventory_counts_but_never_names_an_invisible_table`, read as a deliberate choice, but nothing in `docs/decisions.md` recorded a reason for it, and the module docstring only ever claimed the *count* was the point — discarding the name was simply unneeded at the time, not a considered privacy or safety boundary (the names were already visible to the introspecting role via `pg_catalog`, which is exactly the metadata-is-public-regardless-of-`SELECT`-grants fact the original `has_table_privilege` decision rests on). `-102`'s own edge case — "a table temporarily invisible because of a permission change — reported as possibly invisible rather than definitely gone" — cannot be satisfied by a count alone, so the name has to be kept. The third is new rather than a reversal: fuzzy name matching for a rename suggestion was rejected per the ticket's own stated Assumption ("fuzzy matching risks attaching a note to the wrong column"); the single-removed/single-added-column-in-one-table rule is the only case where a rename is unambiguous without guessing, so it is the only case that gets a suggestion — anything with more than one candidate on either side gets none, silently, rather than a low-confidence guess.

**Consequences:** A person reading `M4-SCHEMA-ING-101`'s changelog entry or `docs/decisions.md` entry about the caveat and expecting to see it rendered in a live prompt will not, unless they trace as far as this entry — flagged here specifically so that gap is findable. Any future caller of `compose_conflict`/`_delimit_schema_notes` that does *not* pre-filter stale notes the way `retrieve_relevant_facts` does would reintroduce the caveat-not-exclusion behavior for its own notes; that is intentional defence in depth, not a bug, but worth knowing about before relying on it as the primary guarantee. `SchemaInventory.omitted_count` and `.omitted_tables` must be kept consistent by any future engine support (SQL Server, MySQL both still report `omitted_tables=None`, the same "real gap, not silently worked around" shape `omitted_count=None` already has for both). A source's `sources.status`/`last_error` can now be set by `schema_introspect.refresh_schema_attention` independently of `ingest.refresh_source`'s document-coverage reasons — the two are kept from fighting over the same row via the `Schema drifted:` `last_error` prefix, which is the only way `refresh_schema_attention` tells its own prior write apart from a dead-connection or locked-credentials reason set elsewhere; a third source of `sources.status` writes in the future will need the same discipline or risks two mechanisms flapping a source's status back and forth.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog` `M4-SCHEMA-BE-102`; issue #365 (closed by this), building on issue #355/`M4-SCHEMA-ING-101`; `api/src/askwell/schema_introspect.py` (`SchemaInventory.omitted_tables`, `refresh_schema_attention`, the rename-detection additions to `write_schema_inventory`); `api/src/askwell/memory.py` (`retrieve_relevant_facts`'s `AND NOT stale`, `reattach_schema_note`); `api/src/askwell/agent/conflict.py` (`_delimit_schema_notes`); `api/src/askwell/connections.py` (`_run_deep_introspection`); `api/src/askwell/db/models.py` (`SchemaNote.stale_reason`/`.reattach_suggestion`); migration `20260918_3f7c1a9e5d20_schema_note_stale_reason.py`.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-SCHEMA-ING-101`: a stale user note is flagged, never superseded, and "unguessable" is a name-shape heuristic, not a dictionary lookup

**Decision:** Two decisions in one ticket. First (closing issue #355): a `user`-origin `schema_notes` row whose table/column position a re-introspection no longer finds gets a new `stale` boolean set `true` — the row stays active (`superseded_by` untouched) rather than being superseded or deleted, and the flag clears again if the position reappears. Second: whether a column name is "unguessable" enough to raise a clarification is decided by `_is_unguessable_column_name` — split the name into letter-only tokens, flag it if any token is three characters or fewer and outside a small hardcoded allowlist of common short words (`id`, `at`, `key`, `url`, …) — rather than checking tokens against a real English dictionary or word-frequency list.

**Why:** For the first: superseding a `user`-origin note the way an `inferred` one is superseded-by-itself was rejected because supersession means "this is no longer the active answer," which is false here — the person's explanation of what the column meant is still correct, only the column itself is gone. Deleting it was rejected for the same reason C6 rejects deleting audit rows: the explanation is provenance a user might still want to see, not noise. A flag that survives independently of `superseded_by` was the only option that lets retrieval keep surfacing it (so a reappearing column instantly has its old note back, verified) while still letting the prompt carry a caveat rather than presenting a vanished column as current schema (C5's "abstention over invention" extends naturally to "never present a stale fact as current"). For the second: a real dictionary (`nltk`, `wordfreq`, or similar) was considered and rejected on `AGENTS.md` §4's "prefer what exists" — neither is already a dependency, both are large enough to be a real addition to a no-network-required install, and a name-shape heuristic gets the ticket's own worked example (`st_cd` unguessable, `student_status`/`created_at`/`email` guessable) right without one. The heuristic is deliberately conservative — it will occasionally flag a real but short word nobody would ask about (`dob`, `rfq`) and occasionally miss a long word that is still meaningless without domain context (`hlos`, `sfdc`) — accepted because the clarification cap and the "answer once, apply everywhere" loop make a false positive cheap (one extra question, answered once) and a false negative merely means the existing "the model reads the raw column name and type" baseline continues for that one column, not a regression.

**Consequences:** `SchemaNote` (both the ORM model in `db/models.py` and `askwell.memory`'s retrieval dataclass) permanently carries a `stale` field now; any future code constructing one directly (not through `get_active_schema_notes`/`retrieve_relevant_facts`) must set it deliberately rather than relying on a class default silently being correct. The allowlist in `_GUESSABLE_SHORT_TOKENS` is the kind of thing that will need occasional tuning against real schemas (a domain-specific short word nobody flagged yet); it is not meant to be exhaustive on day one. Two gaps were found while implementing this and filed rather than folded in here: `table_infer.py`'s own `table_column`/`date_format` clarification subjects are a compound string that never actually matches `reapply.resolve_dependencies`'s exact-match lookup, so CSV-source column clarifications appear to have never promoted a schema note (issue #361); and no bounded value-distribution query is wired up for MySQL or SQL Server yet, so unguessable-column clarifications are PostgreSQL-only for now (issue #362).

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-SCHEMA-ING-101`; issue #355 (closed by this), #361, #362; `api/src/askwell/schema_introspect.py` (`raise_unguessable_column_clarifications`, `_is_unguessable_column_name`, the `stale`-handling loops in `write_schema_inventory`); `api/src/askwell/db/models.py` (`SchemaNote.stale`); `api/src/askwell/memory.py` (`SchemaNote`, `get_active_schema_notes`, `retrieve_relevant_facts`); `api/src/askwell/agent/conflict.py` (`_delimit_schema_notes`); migration `20260918_1e6f3b9c4a72_schema_note_stale.py`.

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-SQL-DB-107`: the sandbox role check is a separate, later `try` in `worker.startup`, not folded into the existing one

**Decision:** `askwell.sandbox.verify_readonly_role` — the check that refuses to start the worker if `askwell_sandbox_readonly` carries a privilege a read-only role must not have — runs in its own `try`/`except` in `askwell.worker.startup`, placed *after* the existing block that calls `embed.check_dimension`, `ingest.resume` and `reapply.resume` against the main database, rather than as one more line inside that block's own `try`.

**Why:** the first version put it as the first line inside the existing `try`, on the reasoning that both are "startup checks that should run before anything else." That broke a real, pre-existing test — `test_embed_records.py::test_worker_startup_refuses_to_start_on_a_dimension_mismatch` — whose fixture deliberately points `sandbox_database_url` at an unroutable address, the same way most of this codebase's startup-check tests isolate the one thing under test from everything else. With the role check first, its own connection failure (an `OSError`/`psycopg.OperationalError`, not `SandboxRoleMisconfigured`) was caught by the same broad `except Exception: log.warning(...); return` that already exists to tolerate "Postgres is not up yet" — and that `return` happened before `embed.check_dimension` ever ran, silently swallowing the dimension mismatch the test exists to prove is fatal. The bug is not specific to the test fixture: on a real machine where the sandbox container is merely slower to come up than the main database — an ordinary boot-order race, not a misconfiguration — the same ordering would have skipped the main database's own resume and dimension check entirely, for a reason that has nothing to do with either. The two checks protect different things and have different reactions to "unreachable": a `SandboxRoleMisconfigured` is a fact about how the install is configured and must always be fatal, while the sandbox being merely not up yet must be exactly as tolerated as the main database being not up yet already was — but they cannot share one `except Exception` and get both right, since a generic connection failure to *either* database looks identical to that clause. Splitting the check into its own `try`, running after the first block has already either succeeded or returned, means a sandbox connection failure there logs a warning and continues (the worker still resumes ordinary ingestion against the main database, which is all that first block needs) while `SandboxRoleMisconfigured` alone still propagates out of `startup` and crashes the process, the same way `embed.EmbeddingDimensionMismatch` already does. The alternative considered — keep one `try`, add a second `except` clause for `OSError`/`psycopg.OperationalError` ahead of the broad one, to swallow only sandbox-connection failures — was rejected: it would require this module to know and enumerate every network-layer exception two different drivers (the main database's and the sandbox's, both `psycopg`, so actually the same type) can raise, which is exactly the kind of exception-type coupling `except Exception` exists to avoid needing, and it still would not fix the ordering problem of the role check running, and being able to short-circuit, before the main database's own resume.

**Consequences:** a slow-to-start sandbox container no longer delays or skips the main database's ingest resume and dimension check; only a genuine role misconfiguration blocks worker startup, verified live by misconfiguring `askwell_sandbox_readonly` on the running stack and watching the worker crash-loop on exactly that condition, then resume normally once reverted. Any future startup check that depends on a *second* database or process being reachable should default to this shape — its own `try`, placed after checks that do not depend on it, tolerant of "not up yet," fatal only on a named, specific exception — rather than assuming one shared `except Exception` correctly distinguishes every check's own meaning of "unreachable."

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-SQL-DB-107`; `api/src/askwell/worker.py` (`startup`), `api/src/askwell/sandbox.py` (`verify_readonly_role`, `SandboxRoleMisconfigured`); `api/tests/test_embed_records.py::test_worker_startup_refuses_to_start_on_a_dimension_mismatch` (the test that caught it).

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`: primary keys and foreign keys read from `pg_catalog`, never `information_schema`'s constraint views, once a real restricted role was tried

**Decision:** `askwell.schema_introspect._introspect_postgresql_blocking` gets a table/view/materialized-view's existence and visibility from `pg_class` joined to `has_table_privilege` (already the design, and already correct), but gets primary keys from `pg_index`/`pg_attribute` and foreign keys from `pg_constraint` (`contype = 'f'`) joined through `pg_attribute` on both sides, rather than `information_schema.table_constraints`/`key_column_usage`/`constraint_column_usage` as first written.

**Why:** the `information_schema` version was written first — it is the standard-SQL way to ask the question and reads naturally next to the `information_schema.columns` query beside it, which does work correctly under a restricted role. It was wrong: verified live against the real `askwell_sandbox_readonly` role on the real sandbox instance (`AGENTS.md` §4's "run it" rule, not a unit test with a mock cursor), a table's primary key silently disappeared — `has_table_privilege(..., 'SELECT')` was `true` for the role, `information_schema.columns` correctly listed both columns, and `information_schema.table_constraints` joined to `key_column_usage` still returned zero rows for the same table's `PRIMARY KEY` constraint. Postgres's own `information_schema` constraint views apply a privilege check beyond plain table-level `SELECT` before they will name a constraint at all — a gate this repo has no use for and that a role deliberately restricted to read-only querying (C2's whole premise for the connection path, and the whole reason `askwell_sandbox_readonly` exists rather than reusing the owner) will not generally clear. `pg_catalog.pg_index`/`pg_constraint` carry no such gate — table and constraint *metadata* is publicly readable in Postgres regardless of row-level `SELECT` grants, which is also what makes the separate `has_table_privilege` check the right and only way to detect an actually-invisible table (the ticket's own "omitted" edge case) rather than conflating "metadata I can't see" with "data I can't see". The alternative considered — granting the readonly role something beyond `SELECT` so `information_schema`'s gate clears — was rejected outright: it would mean loosening the one role C3's isolation and this ticket's own validation rule both depend on being read-only, to work around a metadata-visibility quirk that has a correct answer without touching privileges at all.

**Consequences:** every table-level `schema_notes` description this module writes now correctly names a primary key and foreign keys for a live PostgreSQL connection and a sandbox database alike; before this fix every such description silently read as if no table had a primary key. A second, independent bug this same live pass caught: `askwell.connections.record_introspection` (the shallow, names-only pass `M4-CONN-FE-096` built) had never been called more than once per source before this ticket made on-demand re-introspection possible, and repeat calls each inserted a second, competing active "Table X." placeholder note rather than superseding the first — fixed by skipping a table that already carries any active note (`api/src/askwell/connections.py`, `record_introspection`), verified by re-introspecting the same live connection twice in a row and confirming exactly one active note per table both times.

**Refs:** `docs/build-plan.md` phase 3, `docs/data-sources.md` §4/§5, issue #353 (the same class of live-untested gap, filed for MySQL/SQL Server rather than fixed blind); `api/src/askwell/schema_introspect.py`, `api/src/askwell/connections.py`, `api/tests/test_schema_introspect.py`.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-CONN-SEC-098`: the per-install secret lives on the run-directory bind mount, and a lost secret locks rather than regenerates silently

**Decision:** `sources.config_encrypted` is now encrypted with a Fernet key derived (HKDF-SHA256) from a 32-byte per-install secret. That secret is generated on first use and stored as a plain file, owner-only permissions, at `Settings.install_secret_path` — default `/run/askwell/install.key`, on the same host bind mount (`${ASKWELL_RUN_DIR}:/run/askwell`) `api` and `worker` already share for the inference socket. `askwell.crypto.load_or_create_install_secret` fills in a missing file but treats every other read failure (wrong length, permission denied) as `CredentialsLocked` rather than silently generating a replacement.

**Why:** the alternative locations considered were a new named Compose volume (rejected: another volume to document and back up correctly, for one 32-byte file, when a bind mount that already meets every requirement — host-persisted, outside `postgres-data`, shared identically between the two containers that need it — already exists) and storing the secret in the `settings` key/value table (rejected outright: it would make the secret travel with every database copy, which defeats the entire point — C8's "outside the database" assumption in `docs/architecture.md` §7 is load-bearing, not decorative). Regenerating a missing secret automatically was considered and rejected for the same reason `load_or_create_install_secret`'s docstring states directly: a install that silently makes itself a new key looks identical to a working one until the first decrypt, which is a worse failure than refusing plainly at the moment the loss is discovered — "fail loudly" (`AGENTS.md` §6) applies to a security boundary quietly going soft, not only to code paths that crash. HKDF (not a bare hash or the raw secret) was chosen because it is the standard construction for "derive a key from key material, with room to add more material later" — exactly what folding in the M7 passphrase needs, without a second derivation scheme to reconcile. Fernet (not raw AES-GCM) was chosen because it bundles authentication and versioning into one call, so `askwell.crypto` has one obvious way to use it rather than a nonce-management decision to get subtly wrong; `cryptography` (the library, not a hand-rolled scheme — `AGENTS.md`'s standing rule) provides both.

A distinct `credentials_locked` reason code is deliberately not folded into `host_unresolved` or the existing generic failure path: `run_introspection` now decrypts *before* ever calling `probe_connection`, so a locked credential never touches a socket, and reporting it as a reachability failure would send the user chasing a network problem that does not exist — the same "misleading failure" `docs/data-sources.md` §4 already warns against for a different case (#345's DNS-vs-egress ambiguity).

**Consequences:** a copied `postgres-data` volume, alone, no longer yields a usable credential — the key never lives in that volume. A backup restored on a second machine (M7 territory, but true today by construction) hits `credentials_locked` and asks for re-entry rather than reporting a wrong host or password, verified live against the running stack (`.run/install.key` removed, `run_introspection` re-run, source moved to `attention` with the locked message, credential never touched a socket). `create_connection_source` and `run_introspection` both now take `settings`, which is a signature change every call site had to follow (`api/src/askwell/sources.py`, `api/src/askwell/worker.py` unchanged since it already threads `settings` through). `cryptography>=44.0` is a new runtime dependency (`api/pyproject.toml`, `api/uv.lock`), verified against PyPI at 50.0.1 current.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-CONN-SEC-098`; `docs/architecture.md` §7 (`config_encrypted` note); `docs/data-sources.md` §4; issue #345 (the failure-misclassification precedent this entry's `credentials_locked` reasoning follows); `api/src/askwell/crypto.py`, `api/src/askwell/connections.py`, `api/src/askwell/config.py`, `.env.example`.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-CONN-SEC-097`: the write-permission probe detects by introspection, never by attempting a write, and fails closed on an unreadable credential

**Decision:** `askwell.connections.probe_connection` refuses a write-capable credential before either read check runs, for all three engines. Detection is privilege introspection only — `pg_roles.rolsuper` / `has_database_privilege(..., 'CREATE')` / `information_schema.table_privileges` for PostgreSQL, `SHOW GRANTS FOR CURRENT_USER()` for MySQL/MariaDB, `IS_SRVROLEMEMBER('sysadmin')` / database role membership / `sys.fn_my_permissions` for SQL Server — never an actual `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`CREATE` attempt against the user's database. A new `probe_unreliable` reason code refuses the connection outright whenever one of those introspection queries itself raises, on any of the three engines. There is no override anywhere in this path.

**Why:** the alternative to introspection — attempting a real write and rolling it back — is itself the thing this ticket exists to prevent Askwell from ever doing to a database it has not yet verified is safe to touch; a privilege check is the only method consistent with "Askwell does not hold credentials that can damage the user's database" applying from before the very first write is ever attempted, not after. Failing closed on `probe_unreliable` is a deliberate tightening past what this module already did for the read-only check it borrows structure from: `_probe_sqlserver_blocking`'s existing SELECT check assumes read access when `sys.fn_my_permissions` itself fails (a known permissiveness gap, left alone — it is `M4-CONN-FE-096`'s scope, not this ticket's, and changing it was not asked for). The write probe cannot inherit that assumption: this ticket's own AC is explicit that an unverifiable credential is refused, "rather than assumed safe," so the two checks now disagree on purpose where they used to agree by accident. Superuser/sysadmin and database-level `CREATE` are checked in addition to table-level grants because a write-capable role frequently is not one with an explicit `INSERT` grant on a specific table — it is one that owns the database, or can create objects in it — and the AC's "refused if they can write anywhere reachable" reads those as write-capable too.

**Consequences:** every refusal names the strongest permission found and the object it applies to (`_write_capable_outcome`, tested against a real Postgres in `api/tests/test_connections_write_probe_db.py` for table-level, database-level `CREATE`, and the read-only-passes case; MySQL grant parsing and SQL Server permission/role classification are pure functions tested without a database in `api/tests/test_connections.py`, since neither engine is part of this stack). A refusal is recorded as an `audit_decisions` row (`connection_write_refused`, naming engine/host/message, never the credential — `docs/decisions.md`'s own long-standing rule) and increments a local, never-transmitted Redis counter (`askwell:connections:write_probe_refused`) best-effort, mirroring `askwell.egress._record`'s tolerance for a Redis hiccup. `read_only_user_sql` returns copyable, engine-specific statements for a role named `askwell_reader`; Askwell never runs them — creating that user is the user's own action, per this ticket's "Out of Scope". `web/components/add/add-screen.tsx`'s "not wired up yet" copy for the write probe, and `web/components/settings/connections.tsx`'s "write permissions not yet checked" line, are both now false and have been corrected in the same change; issue #346's caveat recommendation for `docs/ux/add-source.md` §4 is moot for the same reason, since that document already describes this ticket's target state rather than the placeholder.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-CONN-SEC-097`; `docs/data-sources.md` §4 layer 1; `docs/ux/add-source.md` §4; `docs/states-and-edge-cases.md` §4 (edge case 118); issues #345, #346, #348 (the SQL Server SELECT-check asymmetry this entry names and leaves unfixed); `api/src/askwell/connections.py`, `api/src/askwell/sources.py`.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-CONN-FE-096`: the egress permit mechanism a live connection needs is not built here, and is filed as its own issue

**Decision:** the connection wizard (`api/src/askwell/connections.py`, `POST /sources/connection`, `web/components/add/add-screen.tsx`'s `ConnectionRoute`) is built in full — validation, a real connect attempt against PostgreSQL (`psycopg`), MySQL/MariaDB (`pymysql`) and SQL Server (`python-tds`), a minimal read check, table-name introspection, and five distinguishable failure reasons. What is **not** built is any change to `askwell.egress.EgressProxy` or the `internal` Compose network that would let one of those connection attempts actually reach an external database. That gap is filed as [#345](https://github.com/Rumeasiyan/askwell/issues/345) rather than built informally inside this ticket.

**Why:** this ticket's own C1 note (`docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md`) says the proxy "must be configured to permit exactly that destination and nothing else" — but no such mechanism exists anywhere in this codebase, for any destination. `docs/architecture.md` §5.1 and the 2026-08-26 entry below are explicit: the proxy in local mode has *no* allowed destinations, the two narrowly-scoped exceptions it names (online AI, web search) are not built yet either (both wait on M8), and a test asserts nothing has informally added a third. The 2026-08-28 entry below rejected an allowlist entry for a single hardcoded host (the model download) on the grounds that "one allowlist entry" costs the proxy's whole "cannot leak by misconfiguration" property. A live database connection is a harder case than that one: the destination is not a fixed, known host but whatever the user types, so permitting it safely means a genuinely new mechanism — a per-source, user-authorised, revocable exception — not a config line. Building that is a change to C1's sole enforcement point (`askwell.egress`), and belongs in a security-labelled ticket with its own review, not folded into a `frontend`-labelled wizard ticket sized at 4-6 hours. Confirmed live rather than assumed: a smoke-test connection to `sandbox:5432` (reachable because `api` already shares that Compose network for C3's sake) round-tripped end to end — connect, introspect, `schema_notes` written, source `ready` — while the same request against a real external IP address came back `network_blocked` and against a real external host name came back `host_unresolved`, which is the concrete shape of the second finding below.

A second, narrower finding came out of building the classifier: **a host name and a blocked network are indistinguishable today**, though a literal IP address and a blocked network are not. DNS resolution for anything outside the `internal` network fails exactly like a genuine typo (`socket.gaierror`, both cases), so `_classify_network_error`/`_classify_postgresql_message` correctly report `host_unresolved` for a real, correctly-typed external host name right now — which is misleading in exactly the way `docs/data-sources.md` §4's edge case warns against, and is not fixable from this module; it needs the destination to be resolvable at all, which is downstream of #345. A literal IP address skips DNS and fails at the socket instead (`ENETUNREACH`), which *is* classified correctly as `network_blocked` today, and is what the smoke test above exercises. Filed as part of #345 rather than a second issue, since the fix is the same one.

**Consequences:** the wizard's four distinguishable failures are real and tested (`api/tests/test_connections.py`, `api/tests/test_connections_db.py`, `api/tests/test_sources_api.py`) against every driver-level exception each library actually raises, and the connect→introspect→ready path is proven correct against a reachable Postgres (the sandbox instance). What does not yet work on a real install is connecting to a database outside the Compose network at all — which is every live database this ticket is nominally for, since "one machine" almost always means the host machine or the LAN, not a container peer. Until #345 lands, a cold-start walkthrough with a real external database will end at `network_blocked` or `host_unresolved`, not at a working connection. This is stated on the route itself (`ConnectionRoute`'s "not wired up yet" copy covers the write probe only, not this) and in the ticket's own closing comment, rather than left for someone running the walkthrough to discover unexplained.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-CONN-FE-096`; `docs/architecture.md` §5.1; issue #345; the 2026-08-26 and 2026-08-28 entries below; `api/src/askwell/connections.py`, `api/src/askwell/egress.py`.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-DUMP-FE-090`: a dump-extension file that does not read as a dump is refused as unsupported, not silently reclassified as plain text

**Decision:** `filetypes.detect`/`add-source.ts`'s `detect` still enters the dump-detection branch when the extension is `.sql`/`.dump`/`.backup`, even when the content check (`_looks_like_a_dump`) says no — but the outcome of that combination changed. Previously the extension alone routed the file to the dump route regardless of content (issue #341: a prose letter renamed `letter.sql` came back `format: "a SQL dump"`, refused as an unidentified *dump*, and suggested resaving it with `pg_dump`). Now, when the extension claims a dump and the content does not back it up, the file is refused outright — `format: "plain text"`, `route: "files"`, `refusal: REFUSED_DUMP_UNSUPPORTED` — rather than either being silently indexed as plain text (dropping the extension check entirely, the fix issue #341 itself proposed as its recommended option) or told it is a dump Askwell could not identify (the previous, wrong behaviour).

**Why:** issue #341's own recommended fix — drop the extension half of the `or` so only content decides — was checked against this ticket's own testing note before being taken as-is: "Select a text file renamed to a dump extension and confirm it is refused as *unsupported*." Dropping the extension check entirely does not produce that outcome; it produces the file quietly landing on the files route as plain text, which is neither what the user named it (a database export) nor an honest refusal — it is a third, unrequested behaviour that happens to not be the old bug. Keeping the extension as a *trigger* for the dump-detection branch, but changing what a content mismatch produces inside that branch, is what actually satisfies the acceptance criterion: the user who names something `.sql` and drops it here is told plainly that it does not read as a database dump, with the same route-out framing (`docs/ux/add-source.md` §5's "Unsupported format" state) every other route-mismatch already uses, rather than being handed a `pg_dump`-specific hint that has nothing to do with their letter.

**Consequences:** `REFUSED_DUMP_UNSUPPORTED` is a new, distinct refusal from `REFUSED_DUMP_ENGINE_UNKNOWN` — the two must never be merged, because they answer different questions ("this is not a dump at all" vs "this is a dump but Askwell cannot tell whose"). A `.sql` file that is genuinely CSV-shaped (rare, but possible — e.g. a delimiter-heavy comment block) is still refused as a non-dump rather than rerouted to the table route, on the same reasoning `M1-ADD-VAL-024` already established for `.md`/`.txt`/`.html`: an extension that claims one route is never silently handed to a different one by a heuristic.

**Refs:** `AGENTS.md` §3 C3, `docs/ux/add-source.md` §3 and §5, `api/src/askwell/filetypes.py` (`detect`, `REFUSED_DUMP_UNSUPPORTED`), `web/lib/add-source.ts`, issue #341.

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-DUMP-FE-090`: dump engine detection is best-effort from the header, and an unrecognised engine refuses rather than defaults to PostgreSQL

**Decision:** `askwell.filetypes._dump_engine` (mirrored as `dumpEngine` in `add-source.ts`) classifies a textual dump's likely origin — `"postgresql"`, `"mysql"`, `"sqlserver"`, or `"unknown"` — from a small set of header markers unique to each tool's own export format (`pg_dump`'s own preamble comment; `mysqldump`'s header line and its `/*!NNNNN` version-gated comment syntax; SQL Server's `SET ANSI_NULLS ON`/`SET QUOTED_IDENTIFIER ON`/`GO` pragmas, which are not valid syntax in either of the other two engines). `"unknown"` is refused with `REFUSED_DUMP_ENGINE_UNKNOWN`, which asks rather than asserts — never silently treated as PostgreSQL and sent to `import_dump`.

**Why:** the alternative — defaulting anything unrecognised to PostgreSQL and letting `psql --set ON_ERROR_STOP=1` be the real check — was rejected because it trades an honest, immediate refusal for a confusing one two steps later: a sandbox database created, a worker job dispatched, and a failure reported as a `psql` syntax error the user has no reason to connect to "wrong database engine." `docs/ux/add-source.md` §3's own edge case — "a dump whose engine cannot be determined — the user is asked rather than guessed at" — is explicit that guessing is the wrong failure mode here, not merely a style preference.

**Consequences:** a real PostgreSQL dump with an unusual header (hand-edited, or produced by a tool other than `pg_dump` that does not emit the preamble comment) will be refused as unrecognised rather than imported. Accepted for v1: the assumption named in the ticket itself ("Engine detection from the dump's header is reliable enough to route the message") is a judgement call, not a guarantee, and the failure mode of a false "unknown" is a refusal with a route out (resave with `pg_dump`, or connect live, or export as CSV) rather than a corrupted import.

**Refs:** `AGENTS.md` §3 C3, `docs/data-sources.md` §7, `docs/ux/add-source.md` §3, `api/src/askwell/filetypes.py` (`_dump_engine`), `web/lib/add-source.ts` (`dumpEngine`).

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-CSV-ING-094`: a table reload rebuilds the whole table, and the owner role is unsealed for the window rather than left sealed

**Decision:** answering a `date_format` clarification does not `ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN ... TYPE date USING ...` the one column in place. `askwell.table_load.reload_source` re-parses the source CSV/spreadsheet from `sources.root_path`, re-derives every `date_format` answer given so far for that source from `clarifications` itself, and re-runs the exact same create-and-insert path `load_source` uses for a fresh load — `DROP TABLE IF EXISTS` then `CREATE TABLE`, one row at a time, catching a cast failure per row. To do this, `askwell.sandbox` gained `unseal_owner`, symmetric to the existing `seal_owner`: `reload_source` re-grants the owner role's `CONNECT` on the source's sandbox database before rebuilding and reseals it once the rebuild finishes, rather than leaving a table source's owner grant permanently open the way a dump's is permanently closed.

**Why:** `ALTER COLUMN ... TYPE ... USING` is one statement — Postgres aborts the whole thing on the first row it cannot cast, which is exactly the all-or-nothing behaviour `docs/backlog`'s own validation rule for this ticket exists to rule out ("Load failures are always reported per row, never dropped silently"). Making a column-level `ALTER` report failures per row instead would need a stable row-to-value join key the table has no other reason to carry — an internal ordinal column added at creation purely so a later `UPDATE ... WHERE ordinal = ...` could target one row, which is schema surface every future consumer (the SQL-generation epic, `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100` onward) would then have to know to ignore. A full rebuild needs no such column: it reuses the one row-insertion path this ticket already built and already tests, so "insert a row, catch what fails" has one implementation, not two. The cost is re-parsing the file and re-inserting every row on every reload rather than touching one column — accepted because a reload is a rare, user-initiated event (one clarification answered), not a hot path.

Sealing the owner role after every load, the way `dump_import.import_dump` does unconditionally, was rejected for a table source specifically because a CSV's lifecycle is not "loaded once, read forever" the way a dump's is (`docs/backlog`: "an import is a single attempt" — true for a dump, false for a table waiting on a clarification answer). Leaving the owner permanently unsealed for every table source instead — the other alternative — was also rejected: it would mean the shared owner role stays connectable to every table source's database indefinitely, which is the same shape of cross-source reach issue #330 closed for dumps, just left open here instead of closed. Unsealing only for the duration of an actual rebuild keeps the invariant issue #330 established — the owner is connectable to a database only while genuinely doing owner-privileged work on it — while still allowing the one operation a table source legitimately needs after `ready` that a dump never does.

**Consequences:** a reload is `O(rows)` work every time, not `O(changed rows)` — acceptable at CSV/spreadsheet scale, and already named as a known limitation ("very wide files may load slowly") in the ticket itself. Cumulative overrides are re-derived from `clarifications` on every reload rather than cached, so a source can be reloaded any number of times, in any order, and always reflects every answer given so far — but it also means `reload_source` does one query per reload to reconstruct that map rather than an O(1) lookup, an acceptable trade for not needing a second, harder-to-keep-consistent store of the same fact. Reversing the "no internal ordinal column" choice would require a schema migration and would obligate `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`'s own introspection to filter it out of what a user's SQL ever sees.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` `M4-CSV-ING-094`; `api/src/askwell/table_load.py`; `api/src/askwell/sandbox.py` (`unseal_owner`); `api/src/askwell/reapply.py`; issue #330 (the owner-seal precedent this reasons from).

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-DUMP-VAL-089`: caps enforced by a watchdog thread, not a check between chunks

**Decision:** `dump_import._load_blocking` enforces both the size and time caps from a background `threading.Thread` that runs for the whole life of the `psql` subprocess and calls `proc.kill()` itself the moment either is crossed, plus one final authoritative check right after `psql` exits (success or failure) before the outcome is decided. Not a check placed between reads of `dump_path`, which was the first approach tried.

**Why:** most of a real dump's wall-clock time is not spent being fed to `psql` — it is spent blocked *inside* a single `proc.stdin.write` once the OS pipe buffer fills and `psql` is busy running a statement, or inside the final `proc.wait()` once every byte has already been handed over and `psql` is still executing something (a big `CREATE INDEX`, or the edge case's own `pg_sleep`). A cap check that only runs between chunk reads never runs during either of those, which means it would never fire for exactly the case the ticket's own edge case names: "an abort during a long-running statement — the statement is terminated rather than waited on." An independent watchdog thread has no such blind spot; it polls on its own schedule regardless of what the main thread is doing, so `proc.kill()` reaches a stuck `write()`/`wait()` immediately. The final backstop check exists because the watchdog polls on `CAP_POLL_SECONDS`, and a load that finishes faster than one poll interval (an empty sandbox database's own catalog overhead can already be over a small cap before a single byte of the dump is fed in — a real scenario, not a contrived one, since it is exactly how the size-cap test proves measurement is against loaded size rather than file size) would otherwise report success with a cap already blown, purely because the watchdog never got a turn.

**Consequences:** `_load_blocking` now starts a daemon thread per import; killed via `proc.kill()` (SIGKILL) rather than a graceful stop, because the goal is ending a stuck client immediately, not giving `psql` a chance to clean up (there is nothing for it to clean up — the sandbox database it was loading gets dropped `WITH (FORCE)` by the caller regardless). If a future change needs multiple concurrent imports to share one process-wide monitor instead of one thread per import, that is a new decision; nothing here assumes only one import runs at a time, but nothing shares state across imports either.

**Refs:** `AGENTS.md` §3 C3, `docs/data-sources.md` §3, `api/src/askwell/dump_import.py` (`_load_blocking`, `check_once`, `watchdog`), `api/tests/test_dump_import.py`.

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-DUMP-VAL-089`: dump caps live in the `settings` table, not on `Settings`

**Decision:** the size and time caps are read and written through `askwell.dump_import.get_dump_size_cap_bytes`/`set_dump_size_cap_bytes` and their time equivalents, backed by the `settings` key/value table (`askwell.settings_store`), the same mechanism `askwell.clarify`'s clarification cap already uses. Not a field on `Settings` (`askwell.config`).

**Why:** `docs/data-sources.md` §3 states both caps are "user-adjustable," and `Settings` is environment configuration read once at process start (`AGENTS.md` §3's own description) — changing a `Settings` field means restarting the process, which is not what "adjustable" means for a single-user desktop product with a settings screen. `askwell.clarify.set_clarification_cap` already established this exact pattern (a validated setter, an unconditional decision record on every change, a typed getter with a stated default) for the same shape of problem, so reusing it rather than inventing a second one was the only real choice.

**Consequences:** a cap change takes effect on the *next* import, never the one in flight — `import_dump` reads both caps once, at the start of a given import, in the same session that creates the sandbox database, and threads them through to `_load_blocking` as plain values rather than re-reading the setting mid-load. `set_dump_time_cap_seconds`'s audit payload stores `previous`/`new` as strings rather than the `float` its own signature carries, because `audit.compute_hash` rejects a bare `float` outright (a float does not round-trip identically through jsonb, so a later chain verification would report tampering that never happened) — discovered by `scripts/dev.sh test-db` failing on this exact ticket, not found in review beforehand.

**Refs:** `AGENTS.md` §3 C3, `docs/data-sources.md` §3, `api/src/askwell/dump_import.py`, `api/src/askwell/clarify.py` (`set_clarification_cap`, the precedent), `api/src/askwell/audit.py` (`_reject_floats`).

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-DUMP-ING-088`: the owner role is sealed after a successful load, not replaced with per-database credentials — issue #330 closed by option 1

**Decision:** `askwell.sandbox.seal_owner` revokes `askwell_sandbox_owner`'s own `CONNECT` on a sandbox database the moment `askwell.dump_import.import_dump` finishes loading it successfully, leaving only `askwell_sandbox_readonly` attached for the query path afterwards. This is option 1 from issue #330 — the fixed-role design `M4-DUMP-DEPLOY-087` shipped, kept, closed by revoking rather than by generating a credential pair per database (option 2).

**Why:** the owner role is what runs a dump's own untrusted content, and it is the only role issue #330 named as a real exposure — the readonly role, which stays attached to every database it has ever loaded, is not the one anything untrusted ever authenticates as. Sealing the owner the instant a load succeeds means the role that will run the *next* dump's content can never simultaneously hold `CONNECT` on a database from a previous, unrelated import — the two are never live at once. This closes the gap for the case that matters (a hostile dump reaching an earlier one through the shared credential) without the cost option 2 would add: a generated pair per database means persisting and later decrypting sandbox-specific credentials, machinery `docs/data-sources.md` §4 already commits to building for live connections' credentials (`sources.config_encrypted`) but that does not exist yet, and pulling it forward into this ticket just to close a gap the simpler fix closes just as completely was rejected as the wrong ticket to carry that cost. A failed load is not sealed at all — its database is dropped outright, so there is nothing left to protect.

**Consequences:** re-introspecting or otherwise touching a loaded dump's database as the owner is no longer possible after import — anything that needs write access to a sandbox database again (there is no such need identified; `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`'s re-introspection is read-only, matching `askwell_sandbox_readonly`) would have to re-derive a reason to unseal it, which nothing here provides. If a future ticket needs the owner to write to an already-loaded database again, that is a new decision, not an oversight to route around silently.

**Refs:** `AGENTS.md` §3 C3, `api/src/askwell/sandbox.py` (`seal_owner`), `api/src/askwell/dump_import.py`, issue #330 (closed), `docs/decisions.md`'s `M4-DUMP-DEPLOY-087` entry above.

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-DUMP-ING-088`: structural table-name introspection only, full schema indexing stays `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`

**Decision:** a successful dump import returns the loaded database's table names (`information_schema.tables`, `table_schema = 'public'`) and nothing more — no column types, keys, relationships, or anything written to `schema_notes`. `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`, listed as depending on this ticket, is where types, keys, relationships and retrieval-indexing of names actually happen.

**Why:** the ticket's own scope says "handing off to schema introspection on success," not "introspecting the schema" — and the backlog's own `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100` entry describes exactly the fuller work (types, keys, relationships, indexing for relevance-based retrieval) as a separate, later ticket that depends on this one. Building that here would duplicate work `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100` is explicitly scoped to do, under a different name, with no eval or acceptance criteria of its own to check it against. Writing placeholder `schema_notes` rows (say, one per table with a generated, contentless description) was considered and rejected: `schema_notes.description` and `.origin` carry real meaning elsewhere (`docs/memory-and-clarification.md` — user-supplied notes outrank inferred ones, and `origin='inferred'` is a specific, measured claim about how a description was produced) that a bare table name does not honestly satisfy.

**Consequences:** `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100` has a real database and a real dump-import outcome to build against, but nothing in `schema_notes` yet — it is starting from the same "nothing exists" state a live connection's first introspection would, which is the correct starting point for a ticket that has not landed. The cold-start walkthrough phrase "open the library and confirm the source is listed with its tables" is only fully true once `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100` (or a UI wired to this ticket's own return value) lands; this ticket proves the table names are recoverable, not that they are rendered anywhere yet.

**Refs:** `docs/backlog/M4-it-answers-from-my-data.md` (`M4-DUMP-ING-088`, `M4-SCHEMA-ING-100`), `api/src/askwell/dump_import.py`.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-DUMP-DEPLOY-087`: two fixed sandbox roles, not a generated pair per database — with a known limit accepted and filed rather than silently carried

**Decision:** the sandbox's isolation uses two fixed, shared login roles created once by `deploy/sandbox/10-roles.sh` — `askwell_sandbox_owner` (what dump content runs as) and `askwell_sandbox_readonly` (the query path) — rather than a freshly generated, per-database role pair for every imported source. What actually confines one sandbox database from another is `PUBLIC`'s default `CONNECT` privilege, explicitly revoked on each database the moment `askwell.sandbox.create_database` creates it and granted back only to those two roles, only on that one database.

**Why:** the ticket's own Acceptance Criteria describe exactly two roles — "an owner used for import and a read-only role used for querying" — and building anything else without being asked is scope the ticket did not authorise. It is also the smaller, more auditable surface for what this ticket actually delivers: no importing exists yet (`M4-DUMP-ING-088`), so there is no real caller that needs per-database credentials persisted anywhere, and `sources` has no column to hold them — only `sources.sandbox_db`, a name. A generated-pair design was considered (and appears to have been the direction of an earlier, unmerged attempt at this same ticket, per issues #321/#322) and rejected *for this ticket* on that basis, not because it is wrong: it is more thorough precisely where the fixed-role design is weakest.

That weakness is real and is being named rather than hidden: `askwell_sandbox_owner` accumulates `CONNECT` on every sandbox database it has ever been granted onto, for as long as that database lives — so once two dump-backed sources are simultaneously live and neither has been dropped, the shared owner role can reach both. A structural read of C3 ("wrecks only its own copy") is not fully satisfied across *concurrent* sources; it is fully satisfied for the single-database case this ticket's own acceptance test walks through (creating one sandbox database, refusing everything from `postgres`/`template1`, dropping it), and for the instance-wide boundaries verified against a live instance (superuser creation, `COPY ... TO PROGRAM`, both large-object APIs, connecting to the instance's own maintenance database).

**Consequences:** `M4-DUMP-ING-088` (which decides what "finished importing" means and is the first real caller of `create_database`) has to either accept the residual cross-database reach while a dump is loading, revoke the owner's `CONNECT` on a database once its import completes (leaving only the readonly role attached), or move to generated per-database credentials at that point instead — filed as issue [#330](https://github.com/Rumeasiyan/askwell/issues/330) rather than left to be rediscovered. `M4-DUMP-ING-088` is also where `create_database`/`drop_database`'s two-connection, session-taking shape (`api/src/askwell/sandbox.py`) gets its first real caller — both now require an `AsyncSession` and write their own `audit_decisions` record (`"sandbox_database_created"` / `"sandbox_database_dropped"`, the latter carrying a `reason`), a structural fix applied after review found the initial version only recorded a `structlog` line (issue #322).

Separately, and worth recording because it cost real time to find: `CREATE DATABASE` does **not** copy `template1`'s `pg_database.datacl` onto the database it creates — `datacl` is a shared, cluster-wide catalog, not part of what a template's file copy carries over, and a freshly created database's null `datacl` means "the built-in default applies," which is `PUBLIC` has `CONNECT`. A first draft of `deploy/sandbox/10-roles.sh` revoked `PUBLIC`'s `CONNECT` on `template1` believing it would propagate to every sandbox database created afterwards; it does not, verified by creating a database from a locked-down `template1` and finding its `datacl` empty. `pg_proc` (function privileges — what makes the large-object revokes work) **is** part of a template's physical copy and does propagate; the two catalogs behave oppositely and both mistakes are the kind that look correct until inspected. `askwell.sandbox.create_database` therefore revokes `CONNECT` on its own database explicitly, every time, and does not rely on anything instance-wide for it.

**Refs:** `docs/data-sources.md` §3, `AGENTS.md` §3 C3, `api/src/askwell/sandbox.py`, `deploy/sandbox/10-roles.sh`, `compose.yaml`, issues #321, #322, #330.

## 2026-09-18 — `M4-CSV-ING-093`: a column that disambiguates in both directions is reported through the existing generic ambiguous-column path, not a new "malformed" candidate shape

**Decision:** a date column whose values disagree with each other about format (one row only valid day-first, another only valid month-first — `detect_date_format`'s `MIXED` verdict) is reported the same way `infer_column_types` already reports a column mixing a thousands separator with a plain decimal: `inferred_type="string"`, `ambiguous=True`, a `ambiguity_reason` naming the conflict, surfaced through `build_candidates`' existing generic `table_column` branch (a free-text "what is it?" question, no `options`). Only the genuinely-undecidable case — no row rules either format out — gets the new two-option `date_format` trigger with `options=["DD/MM/YYYY (day first)", "MM/DD/YYYY (month first)"]`.

**Why:** the ticket's own edge case says a column mixing formats within itself should be "reported as malformed rather than asked about as if it were consistent." Offering the same two-button choice for both cases would be exactly that mistake — the two-option question presumes a single answer covers every row, which is true for the ambiguous-but-consistent case and false for the mixed case. A third candidate shape (a dedicated "malformed date column" trigger, or reusing `MalformedTable`, which today is reserved for ragged row widths at the file level, not a single column's internal inconsistency) was considered and rejected: `table_infer.py` already has a working precedent for exactly this shape of problem (mixed thousands/plain decimals) and reusing it keeps one code path for "a type was attempted, the data didn't cooperate, ask a free-text question" rather than growing a second one for dates specifically.

**Consequences:** the mixed-date case never gets ranked at the `date_format` tier (second, ahead of `document_identity`) — it ranks wherever unplaced `table_column` candidates fall today (below `unreadable_scan`, per the gap `M4-CSV-ING-092` already left and this ticket did not close, filed as issue #327). If that ordering turns out to matter for mixed-date columns specifically, revisit once `table_column`'s own priority slot is decided.

**Refs:** `api/src/askwell/table_infer.py` (`detect_date_format`, `build_candidates`), `api/src/askwell/clarify.py` (`_TRIGGER_PRIORITY`), `docs/data-sources.md` §2.

## 2026-09-18 — `M3-EVAL-TEST-086`: fixture memory facts are seeded through a hand-inserted `clarifications` row, not real detection

**Decision:** the memory-application eval subset's five "apply" and three "supersede" tasks seed their fixture facts by inserting a `clarifications` row directly (`status='pending'`, a fixed subject/question) and then calling the real `askwell.review.answer_clarification` on it — not by running `askwell.clarify`'s own detectors against the fixture corpus and waiting for one to raise the exact question needed.

**Why:** the ticket's own Assumption says "fixture facts can be seeded through the normal clarification path so the test exercises the real route" — the *write* path, not the *raising* path. `askwell.clarify`'s three tests (cannot determine, material, user plausibly knows) exist to catch genuine ambiguity in real content; there is no honest way to make them fire on demand for a specific, invented "which of these two years' figures is currently in force" fact without either fabricating a document that happens to trigger a real detector (fragile, and couples this suite's fixtures to detector internals it does not otherwise depend on) or adding a fifth detector whose only purpose is to be triggered by this eval — scope well beyond a fifteen-task subset. Inserting the row directly and answering it through `answer_clarification` exercises the actual write, the actual `memory_superseded`/`clarification_answered` decisions records, and the actual `fact_usage` wiring `M3-APPLY-BE-079` built — everything downstream of "a clarification was raised" is real; only the raising itself is skipped, and the ticket does not claim to test raising (that is `M3-RAISE-BE-068`–`071`'s own eval surface, which does not exist yet). The alternative rejected: writing straight to `memory` with a hand-built `INSERT`, which was rejected specifically because it bypasses `answer_clarification` and would not exercise the real route at all.

**Consequences:** the eval fixture depends on `clarifications.source_id` being a valid FK, so `seed_memory_fixture` reads back whichever `sources` row `eval.grounded.seed_corpus` created rather than inventing one. If `askwell.clarify`'s raising path ever gets its own eval coverage, that suite should seed through real detection on purpose — this one should keep skipping it, since re-adding that dependency here would make this suite's own facts fragile to unrelated detector-tuning changes.

**Refs:** `eval/memory_apply.py`, `eval/suites/memory_apply.v1.json`, `docs/build-plan.md` quality gate ("Memory application", 15, ≥ 0.85), `docs/backlog/M3-it-learns-my-material.md`'s `M3-EVAL-TEST-086` entry.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M3-MEM-FE-083`: "grouped by subject" is one row per subject with nested history, not a visible group header

**Decision:** `askwell.memory.get_memory_screen` returns one flat, already-sorted list of rows — one row per active `memory` subject or `schema_notes` table/column position — with that row's own supersession history nested inside it (`MemoryScreenRow.history`), rather than a two-level structure of subject-group headers each containing the fact(s) under them.

**Why:** `docs/ux/memory.md` §2 says "One list grouped by subject," and §3's own row example (`▪ invoices.st_cd ... [Edit] [Delete]`) shows a single row per fact, not a header plus children. Reconciling the two: `write_memory_fact`/`write_schema_note`/`correct_memory_fact`/`correct_schema_note` (`M3-STORE-BE-076`, `M3-CORRECT-BE-082`) already guarantee at most one *active* row per subject or position — a user-origin write always retires whatever was active before it. So "grouped by subject" cannot mean multiple simultaneous facts collected under one heading, the way `clarifications.ts`'s `ClarificationGroup` collects several pending items under one source; there is structurally nothing to group. What the doc's "grouped" is actually pointing at is that a subject's *history* — what it used to say — travels with its current row rather than living on a separate screen, which is exactly what §4's "History: every prior value with dates" and §5's "Conflicting facts: later wins, earlier shown struck through" describe. Building a literal two-level group structure to satisfy the word "grouped" would have meant inventing a container that is empty of siblings on every real subject, and made the default sort (inferred-first, `docs/ux/memory.md` §2's own explicit requirement) ambiguous — sorted within groups, or across them? A flat list sorted globally has no such question.

**Consequences:** If a future ticket needs actual multi-fact grouping (e.g., several distinct-but-related subjects clustered under one heading — not named anywhere in `docs/ux/memory.md` today), `get_memory_screen`'s flat shape does not provide it and would need a second grouping key added deliberately, not inferred from "subject" the way this entry reads it. `M3-MEM-FE-084` (interactions) can rely on one row id per subject/position without a second lookup to find "the" active fact for a group.

**Refs:** `api/src/askwell/memory.py` (`get_memory_screen`, `MemoryScreenRow`, `MemoryHistoryEntry`), `docs/ux/memory.md` §2–§5, `web/components/memory/memory-screen.tsx`.

---

## 2026-09-18 — `M3-CORRECT-FE-081`: the chip's own HTTP surface goes into `askwell.memory` itself, and "Correct" on an inferred fact writes a new fact rather than raising

**Decision:** `askwell.memory` gained `register_memory` (`GET /memory/facts/{kind}/{id}`, `POST .../correct`, `POST .../delete`) in the same module as the write-side logic it calls, not a new `askwell.memory_api`. And `correct_fact`, the dispatcher the chip's "Correct" button calls, does not simply forward to `correct_memory_fact`/`correct_schema_note`: when the target is currently `origin = 'inferred'`, those raise `CannotCorrectInference` by design, so `correct_fact` catches that and calls `write_memory_fact`/`write_schema_note` with a user origin instead — a fresh fact that retires the inference the same way any user-origin write already does, rather than surfacing the raise to the chip as a failure.

**Why:** `M3-CORRECT-BE-082`'s own `docs/BRAIN.md` entry said plainly that no route called `correct_memory_fact`/`delete_memory_fact`/etc. yet, and named this as the first thing whichever of the chip or the memory screen started next would need — the same shape `M3-REVIEW-BE-072a` filled for `clarifications` after `M3-RAISE-BE-068`–`070` left it with no HTTP surface either. A separate `askwell.memory_api` module was considered and rejected: `askwell.review` already keeps its routes beside its logic in one file, and splitting them here would mean the *next* person touching `correct_memory_fact`'s contract has to remember a second file exists that calls it. On the inferred-fact fallback: the alternative was to have the chip's UI branch on `origin` before ever calling the route (only show "Correct" as enabled for a user-origin fact) and surface a distinct "Confirm" action for an inference, matching `docs/ux/memory.md` §3's own row example (`[Confirm] [Edit] [Delete]` for an inferred fact). That is the *right* long-term shape and is exactly what `M3-MEM-FE-083`'s memory screen is expected to build — but this ticket's own Scope is "one chip, one popover, two actions" (Correct, Delete), and a chip that shows "Correct" for every fact but silently 409s on an inferred one is a broken control, not a smaller version of the right one. Making `correct_fact` do the sensible thing (assert instead of failing) means the two-action chip is honest immediately, without inventing a third action this ticket was not asked to build.

**Consequences:** A correction of an inferred fact from the chip is recorded as a fresh `origin = 'correction'`/`'user'` row, not a `memory_superseded` decision on the inference itself — the inference's own row is superseded as a side effect of the new write (`write_memory_fact`'s existing "a user-origin write retires any active fact for the same subject" rule), which is the same audit shape a real `correct_memory_fact` call produces, just reached by a different path. When `M3-MEM-FE-083` builds the memory screen's own `[Confirm]` action, it should **not** reuse `correct_fact` for that — confirming an inference must not create a new row (`docs/ux/memory.md` §4: "Confirm ... No re-processing; the content did not change"), which `correct_fact` cannot express since it always treats a change in value as the signal to write.

**Refs:** `api/src/askwell/memory.py` (`register_memory`, `correct_fact`, `delete_fact`, `get_fact_detail`), `api/tests/test_memory.py`, `api/tests/test_memory_api.py`, `docs/ux/memory.md` §3–4, `docs/BRAIN.md`'s `M3-CORRECT-BE-082` entry.
